# Wisconsin — Poll Worker Guides & Training Materials

Official poll-worker manuals and training resources for Wisconsin, compiled for the VB Election Desk knowledge base from 'Pollworker Guides.xlsx'.

## Wisconsin Elections Commission — Part 14 Election Day

- **Resource category:** Direct manual, guide, or training-resource page
- **Availability / limitations:** Official statewide clerk and poll-worker training materials. Direct download link.
- **Source:** https://elections.wi.gov/media/39611/download (fetched 2026-09-09)
- **Capture note:** The workbook's /media/ download route is bot-walled; captured the same official file from WEC's static path (ELAW Part 14 'Election Day', First Edition June 2026).

### Full text

Part 14 
Election Day
Election Day Roles; Setting Up and Opening the Polls; Observers; 
Registration; Challenges; Poll Books; Voting Procedure; Voting 
with Assistance; Curbside Voting; Provisional Ballots; Common 
Issues; Processing Absentee Ballots; Counting Votes; Write-Ins; 
Overvotes & Crossvotes; Closing the Polls; End of Night; Local Board of 
Canvassers; Transmitting Results; Municipal Board of Canvassers
Part Fourteen contains the entirety of what used to be the Election Day 
manual. It contains all election administration topics election inspectors 
will need for Election Day, from start to finish. It can be printed or saved as a 
separate resource specific to everything clerks and election inspectors need 
on Election Day.

First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 2
Chapter 14.2: Roles of Election 
Officials
Although most election functions on Election Day will be carried out by 
municipal clerks and their poll workers, county clerks and the Commission also 
have important roles to play.
Sec. 14.2.1: Role of the 
Commission on Election Day
The Commission provides extended hours leading up to and on Election Day. In 
the days before and after, Commission Staff are often available until at least 6 
p.m. On the weekend before Election Day, Commission Staff are also available 
to assist clerks with whatever they need.
Note: On Election Day, Commission Staff are available from 5 a.m. to midnight.
Local election officials should use general channels of communication on 
Election Day, rather than contacting Commission Staff individually, except in 
special cases. On Election Day, staff will be busy answering calls and emails and 
may not have time to get back to individual contacts.
The best way to get in touch is to call the Help Desk at (608) 266-8005 or email 
Elections@wi.gov. By contacting the main channels, questions will be routed to 
the next available specialist.
Commission Staff are available to assist everyone from voters to county 
clerks with scenarios ranging from general election questions to polling place 
locations to questions about a voter’s record and registration. Wis. Stat. § 7.08(11).
 Ɋ The WEC Help Desk assists with transferring calls to specialists, helping with 
WisVote password reset and login issues, and routine questions from voters, 
including where they vote, absentee ballot questions, using MyVote, and more.
 Ɋ The Election Administration team assists with more detailed questions, 
such as counting write-ins, acceptable forms of proof of identification and 
proof of residence, provisional voting processes, and routing materials at 
the end of night.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 3
 Ɋ The Public Information Officer manages communications between the 
agency and the public and media, and can assist with interacting with the 
media, crafting press releases, crisis communication, and social media.
 Ɋ  The Legal team assists with questions related to legal compliance, 
including statutory obligations, legal risks, or more serious election day 
matters without obvious solutions.
 Ɋ Badger Book staff are available to assist with any questions or issues 
regarding the state’s electronic poll book system, including download 
issues before polls open, technical and connectivity issues, and end-of-
night processes.
Note: On Election Day, if there are security or safety issues, the 
Commission will be in contact with information and next steps.
Here are a few examples of topics Commission Staff cannot 
assist local election officials with on Election Day:
 Ɋ Any statutes outside of our jurisdiction (including town, city, and municipal 
statutes and statutes covered by other agencies, such as the Wisconsin 
Ethics Commission or the Department of Motor Vehicles)
 Ɋ Personal legal questions
 Ɋ Status of absentee ballot beyond what shows on the MyVote absentee 
ballot tracker, including location and sufficiency
 Ɋ Questions or concerns about specific polling places — these should be 
directed to the municipal clerks in charge of the polling places
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 4
Sec. 14.2.2: Role of the County 
Clerk on Election Day
Though they don’t have as many specific Election Day statutory responsibilities, 
county clerks are an important resource to support their municipal clerks on 
Election Day.
Extra Ballots
On Election Day, county clerks are on standby with backup ballots for the 
relevant elections in case any municipal clerks are starting to run low. In that 
case, county and municipal clerks work together using previously established 
procedures to get ballots to the affected polling places before they run out. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.10(1)(a).
Help with Questions
County clerks can assist with scenarios ranging from general election questions 
to polling place locations to questions about a voter’s record and registration. 
They can also answer questions from clerks and poll workers regarding election 
administration, including questions regarding proper training and procedure of 
election officials Wis. Stat. § 7.10(9).
Help Solve Poll Worker Shortages
Since poll workers are required to be a qualified voter of the county of the 
polling place in which they serve according to Wis. Stat. § 7.30(2)(a), county 
clerks are a valuable resource in case municipal clerks are having trouble 
staffing polling places on Election Day. County clerks should work with municipal 
clerks ahead of Election Day to build an awareness of potential election official 
shortages and surpluses as a part of the contingency plans.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 5
Sec. 14.2.3: Role of the Municipal 
Clerk on Election Day
On Election Day, each polling place is run by the Chief Inspector, who operates 
under the supervision of the municipal clerk. Wis. Stat. § 7.36.
The municipal clerk should be available to monitor polling places, check 
supply and ballot levels, respond to news media inquiries, and assist with any 
problems that arise. They should be responsive to questions or issues that 
arise about any of the pre-Election Day duties they fulfill. They should also be 
available to poll workers for advice, requests, or assistance. They also have 
obligations to their voters and should assist with scenarios ranging from 
general election questions, to polling place locations, to questions about a 
voter’s absentee ballot status. Wis. Stat. § 7.15(10).
Best Practice: The municipal clerk should avoid stationing themselves 
inside the voting area, and from spending extended periods of time at the 
polling place. This is especially true if the municipal clerk is a candidate 
on the ballot, but it is also to avoid the municipal clerk performing the 
duties of poll workers when they have not been so appointed. Finally, 
municipal clerks may need to be called away during Election Day, and so 
the operation of the polling place cannot depend on them.
Authorizing a Deputy to Assume Responsibilities
If necessary, municipal clerks may deputize someone to take over or assist with 
official duties on Election Day. To deputize someone, a clerk must put in writing 
exactly what the deputy is authorized to do and for what specific time frame. An 
Oath of Office is also required and can be found on elections.wi.gov/forms.
Municipal Clerk as Election Registration Official (ERO)
A municipal clerk may serve as an ERO and register voters at the polling place on 
Election Day, as long as they meet the qualifications to be an poll worker. However, they 
may not do this if they are a candidate on the ballot for that election. Wis. Stat. § 6.55(6).
End of Night Responsibilities & Reporting Results
Municipal clerks are responsible for organizing and routing election materials 
and making the results public. See Chapter 14.24: “Closing the Polls, LBOC, & 
End of Night Process” on page 219.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 6
Chapter 14.3: Setting up the 
Polling Place
This section focuses on setting up the voting area within the polling place 
prior to polls opening on Election Day. For information about other zones of the 
polling place, parking, exterior route, entrance, and interior route, see Part 11: 
“Accessibility.”
Sec. 14.3.1: Introduction
There are six main activities that occur in the 
voting area in every polling place:
 Ɋ Voter registration
 Ɋ Check in at the poll book
 Ɋ Ballot issuance
 Ɋ Marking of Ballots
 Ɋ Tabulation of Ballots
 Ɋ Absentee processing
There is no one-size-fits-all solution to setting up a voting area, but there are 
accessibility standards all polling places must follow, see Chapter 11.7: “Polling 
Place Set-Up.” Remember many tasks require two poll workers to complete. 
Ensure there is enough seating at every station. See Chapter 14.5: “Election 
Observers” on page 13 for information about setting up an observer area.
Note: Municipal clerks may have also provided more detailed 
instructions for how a particular polling place should be set up and 
what materials go at which station. Refer to those instructions in 
conjunction with this chapter of the manual.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 7
Setting up the Polling Place
Sec. 14.3.2: Setting up the 
Registration Area
 Ɋ It is recommended to put the registration table nearest the entrance to 
the voting area since that is a voter’s first step if they are not registered or 
need to update their name or address.
 Ɋ Providing chairs will make voters more comfortable while they complete a 
registration form.
 Ɋ If a municipality uses more than one polling place, there must be ward 
maps or street directories posted prominently. Wis. Stat. § 5.35(6)(c). These 
can be on or near the registration table.
 Ɋ A line may form if there are many voters who need to register. Place the 
registration table in a location where there is room for a line that will not 
disrupt other activities in the voting area.
 Ɋ Posters depicting acceptable forms of proof of residence and photo ID 
can be posted in areas where voters wait in line to prompt them to have 
their documents ready.
Sec. 14.3.3: Setting up the Check-
In Area/Poll Book Table
The check-in table is where voters state their name and address, show photo 
ID, sign the poll book, and are assigned a voter number. This is often the point 
where long lines start to form. It is recommended that seating be provided for 
voters who are not able to stand for long periods. At polling places that serve 
many voters, it may be more efficient to split the poll book alphabetically 
and have multiple check-in tables. Since two copies of the poll book must be 
maintained, this may take up a lot of space.
When deciding where to place these tables, designate room for lines to form that 
will not disrupt other activities in the voting area. It may be helpful to put masking 
tape on the floor to guide voters to form a line in a specific place or direction. It is 
not recommended to use cones or other barriers as they can become tripping 
hazards or narrow paths of travel to less than 36 inches wide which would no 
longer meet accessibility standards, see Sec. 11.3.2: “Stating Name And Address 
at the Poll Book.” 2010 ADA Standards for Accessible Design § 403.5.1.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 8
Sec. 14.3.4: Setting up the 
Ballot Issuance Table
At the ballot table, voters hand their voter number to the poll workers. The poll 
workers select the ballot style that the voter should receive based on their 
address and issue the ballot to the voter.
Best Practice: A stack of voter numbers can pile up quickly, so it may 
be necessary to place them in a basket or bin.
Sec. 14.3.5: Setting up the Voting 
Booths & Accessible Equipment
There must be at least one voting booth for every 200 voters who voted in the 
last general election. Wis. Stat. § 5.35(2). The exterior of voting booths must be 
in view of the poll workers, and booths and voting equipment must be separate 
from other activities in the polling place. Wis. Stat. § 5.35(4). Voting booths can 
be products sold as voting booths, carrels built onto a wall, tables with privacy 
screens, etc.
Every polling place needs at least one accessible voting booth where a paper 
ballot can be marked from a seated position. For dimensions of an accessible 
voting booth, see Sec. 11.7.6: “Voting Area.” Wis. Stat. § 5.25(4)(a). They should 
be arranged in a way that provides privacy to the voter. Wis. Stat. § 5.35(2). 
Keep in mind that if a voter is seated, anyone walking behind them will be able 
to look over their shoulder to see how they’re marking their ballot. Every polling 
place also needs at least one piece of accessible voting equipment arranged 
to provide privacy, see Sec. 11.7.6: “Voting Area.” Wis. Stat. § 5.25(4)(a); HAVA 
§ 301(a)(3)(B).
Accessible Voting Equipment
Every municipality, even those that hand count ballots, is required to have 
accessible voting equipment at every election. The accessible equipment must 
be set up and turned on prior to polls opening at 7 a.m. For more information 
on the set up and operation of accessible voting equipment, see Chapter 11.7: 
“Polling Place Set-Up” or the municipal clerk.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 9
Sec. 14.3.6: Setting up 
the Tabulator Area
Voters cast their ballots either by depositing them into electronic tabulators 
or ballot boxes (for hand count jurisdictions). In some places, voters cast 
their ballots on direct-recording electronic (DRE) equipment. No matter how 
a voter casts their ballot, the tabulator must be set up prior to polls opening. 
Municipal clerks will have provided detailed instructions for setting up electronic 
tabulators, and poll workers should follow those instructions and contact their 
municipal clerk with concerns.
Sec. 14.3.7: Setting up the 
Absentee Processing Area
Absentee ballots must be opened in the same room where votes are 
cast, so the voting area also must have a designated area for processing 
absentee ballots.
For information on setting up an absentee processing area, see Sec. 11.3.5: 
“Returning an Absentee Ballot.”
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 10
Chapter 14.4: Opening the Polls
Each polling place must open no later than 7:00 a.m. Wis. Stat. § 6.78(1m). However, 
poll workers must ensure that observers are able to enter the polling place in time 
to observe the zeroing of any tabulators. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(1)(a).
Best Practice: The zero tape should be generated no later than 6:50 
a.m., and if so, that is the earliest point that observers may observe. 
Before generating the zero tape, poll workers should check to see if any 
observers are trying to access the polling place.
The Chief Inspector oversees opening the polling place and should keep track 
of the exact time. See Wis. Stat. § 7.36. To accomplish this, the chief Inspector 
will need to establish a clear chain of communication to lead other poll 
workers in efficiently carrying out all opening tasks.  Id.  Relying on this chain of 
command, if the chief Inspector believes that any issue may lead to a delay 
in opening the polls, the chief Inspector should contact the municipal clerk to 
explain the circumstances. Id.  Throughout the day, any significant question 
regarding the proper procedure of election officials should be referred to the 
municipal clerk. Id.
Best Practice: The chief Inspector must ensure that:
 Ɋ The doors to all entrances, including any alternate accessible 
entrances are unlocked and usable by 7:00 a.m. This likely requires 
assigning possession of keys or codes to the chief Inspector, or other 
poll workers, ahead of election day.
 Ɋ The poll workers test the tabulator by securing a printed results “zero 
tape” showing a zero count for every candidate and referendum.
 Ɋ The opening of the polls is announced out loud to the room so that 
votes and election officials know that voting may begin.
 Ɋ The exact time that the polls opened is recorded in the Inspectors’ 
Statement (EL-104). If the polls open late, even by a minute, the 
occurrence and the reason must be clearly documented on the EL-104.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 11
Poll workers are responsible for maintaining an environment at the polling place 
that allows voters to cast their ballots without disruption from the opening of 
the polls to the closing of the polls. Poll workers must also ensure that the voting 
process is transparent and open to the public.
Poll workers may allow people into the polling place prior to 7:00 a.m. to observe 
the zeroing of the tabulator, however, voters may not check-in or receive a ballot 
prior to 7:00 a.m. See Wis. Stat. § 6.78(1m).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 12
Chapter 14.5: Election Observers
A member of the public who is not a candidate at the election has the right to 
observe election proceedings. Wis. Stat. § 7.41(1). All clerks will need to establish 
observation areas and train election officials to conduct election observation. 
Wis. Admin. Code Chapter EL 4 addresses observation and all election officials 
who will be involved in election observation should be familiar with this rule. 
This chapter discusses the rule requirements in detail and also provides best 
practices for effectively managing observation.
Sec. 14.5.1: Managing 
Election Observation
Observers may observe at every polling place, in-person absentee voting 
(IPAV) site,1 board of absentee ballot canvassers location, Special Voting 
Deputy location, 2 central count location, and recount location. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.02(11) .
In some municipalities, this could mean multiple observer areas will be needed, 
with several officials trained to manage the sites. At a recount, the board of 
canvassers is in charge of observation. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.02(6). For all 
other locations, the default individual in charge of election observation at a 
site will be either the Chief Inspector of a polling place or the municipal clerk. 
Id. The Chief Inspector or clerk has the option to delegate management of 
election observers at a site to another election official — either the poll workers 
or the clerk’s authorized representatives. Id. Whoever is in charge of election 
observation at a site is called the designated election official Id.
The rules for election observation cannot “be construed to distract, disrupt, 
obstruct, slow, or prevent a qualified voter from casting a lawful ballot or 
registering to vote.” Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.01. This establishes a balance 
between the primary right of voters to register and vote and the right of 
observers to be present, and it shows that election officials must always ensure 
that voting continues unencumbered.
1 The only exception is a clerk’s office that is not located in a public building, i.e. the clerk’s private home.
2 Subject to certain limitations. Observation at Special Voting Deputy facilities is unique and is covered 
in Sec. 7.3.15: “Rules for Observers”.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 13
Best Practice: All Chief Inspectors should be trained to manage 
election observation and should train at least one additional poll 
worker at each polling place (more if different shifts of individuals will 
be present at different times). One authorized representative of the 
clerk working at each in-person absentee voting location should also 
be trained in managing observers.
This gives the clerk and Chief Inspectors the option to delegate 
this responsibility for the efficient management of the site. If a 
municipality has more than one IPAV site in operation at any time, 
the clerk will need to designate at least one official for each site to 
manage election observation.
Sec. 14.5.2: Establishing 
Observer Areas
The observer area will be managed by the designated election official. The clerk 
or their designee should plan out the location of the observer areas in advance 
to ensure that each site is in compliance with Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(1).
Observer areas3 must be established within 3 to 8 feet of every:
 Ɋ Registration Table
 Ɋ Check-in Table where voters announce their names and addresses or 
election officials announce the names of absentee voters
 Ɋ Ballot Remake Table, see Wis. Stat. § 5.85(3)
If there is more than one of any table type, there must be an observation area 
within 3 to 8 feet of it. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(1)(b). Several observation areas 
may be needed to meet this requirement. At smaller locations, it may also be 
possible for one observer area to be within 3 to 8 feet of multiple tables.
Observer areas must be positioned to not disrupt or interfere with the 
proceedings, and to minimize contact between observers, voters, and 
election officials. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(1 )(a). In addition to the 3-to-8-
foot rule, each observer area must enable observers to readily observe all 
3 Wis. Stat. § 7.41(2), Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(1)(b).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 14
public aspects of the voting process. Wis. Stat. § 7.41(2), Wis. Admin. Code 
§ EL 4.03(1)(a). This means, essentially, that the tasks statutorily required to 
be carried out at an observable location by election officials must be within 
sight of the observers (i.e. not conducted in a separate room) even though 
they may be more than 8 feet away.
Best Practice: Brightly colored tape may be used to mark observer 
areas and clarify exactly where an observer may be during the day. 
Larger sites that may have many observers are encouraged to make 
the observer areas as clear as possible so that both observers and 
election officials know where observers can observe.
If any observable location could not establish sufficient observer areas meeting 
the above requirements, the designated election official must record the reason 
that the requirements were not met and must send a copy of that document 
to the Commission within 60 days of the applicable election. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.03(5). This record may be a page of the Inspectors’ Statement, or 
another convenient document. This does not include a reasonable limitation of 
observers representing the same organization.
Sec. 14.5.3: Accessibility
Members of the public have the right to observe elections, which means that all 
observer areas must be accessible to people with disabilities. Wis. Admin. Code 
§ EL 4.03(1)(c). There must be space for mobility equipment, chairs, and other 
disability aids brought to the site by an observer with a disability. Id. Further, 
observers must have access to chairs already available at the site and must be 
able to use a restroom at the site if election officials have access to a restroom. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(3).
Note: The restroom used by election officials and observers does not 
necessarily need to be the same restroom if there are multiple options, 
provided the access is similarly convenient.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 15
Sec. 14.5.4: Supplies
Each observable location should have a sufficient number of copies of the 
Commission’s Elections Observers Log (EL-109) and enough copies of the 
Commission’s Election Observers: Rules-at-a-Glance (EL-109R) brochure so 
every observer can receive one. Additionally, a few copies of the Order to Leave 
Observable Location (EL-110) should be available.
Sec. 14.5.5: Observer Check-in Process
Any member of the public4 has the right to become an observer by signing the 
observer log and agreeing to follow the observer rules. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 
4.02(10), (13), EL 4.04(1), see Wis. Stat. § 7.41(1). Anyone intending to observe must 
notify the designated election official of that intent. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(1). 
A member of the public is not an observer until the check-in process is complete.
Best Practice: Observers should easily find where to check in. The 
designated election official5 should be ready to receive members of 
the public wishing to check in as observers at least 10 minutes before 
any voting activity starts so that observers intending to see the zeroing 
of any tabulators may see this event.
The designated election official is in charge of the observer check-in process. 
For each member of the public, the designated election official must:
 Ɋ Require the observer to enter his or her full name, street address, and 
municipality onto the observer log along with any organization the 
observer represents. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 4.03(2)(a), EL 4.04(1).
An observer represents an organization when they were deployed 
by, assigned to the location by, or who identifies as representing an 
organization, and an organization includes but is not limited to political 
4 This means anyone besides an election official, accessibility reviewer, a candidate on the ballot for 
the site, or a registered write-in candidate for the site.
5 A “designated election official” is the Chief Inspector, if the observable location is a polling place, or 
the clerk, or any other election official designated by a Chief Inspector or clerk to carry out the election 
responsibilities under this chapter. At a facility served by special voting deputies, designated election 
official means the special voting deputies. At a recount, designated election official means the board 
of canvassers.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 16
parties, candidates, or campaigns. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 4.02(14), (17). An 
observer is not required to represent any organization and may leave the 
space blank if that is the case. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(1).
 Ɋ Require the observer to provide photo ID showing their name and ensure 
the ID reasonably resembles the individual. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 
4.03(2)(a), EL 4.04(1). The ID need not meet the requirements of a photo ID 
for voting purposes. It simply must contain an image of the individual that 
the designated election official can use to determine that the individual 
has properly entered their name on the observer log. Id. For example, an 
out of state Driver’s License or a library card with a photo and the person’s 
name would suffice for observation purposes.
 Ɋ Offer each individual a summary of the rules for observers. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.03(2)(b).
Note: The Commission has designed the Election Observers: Rules-at-
a-Glance (EL-109R) brochure as an easy way for election officials to 
meet this requirement. The official should also provide a brief verbal 
summary of the main points.
 Ɋ Explain to each observer how they may ask questions during the day, how they 
can move between different observer areas, and where any ballot would be 
remade during the day. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(2)(b), see Wis. Stat. § 7.41(2).
 Ɋ Provide each observer with a sticker, badge, or other item that identifies the 
individual as an observer and distinguishes them from an election official, 
and direct them to an observer area. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(2)(c).
If there are groups of observers arriving together, the explanations can be given 
to the group as a whole, but any observer arriving alone must be given the 
same information. See Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(2).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 17
Sec. 14.5.6: What Observers May Do
Once an individual completes the check-in process and becomes an observer, 
they have the right to observe all public aspects of the voting process. Wis. Stat. 
§ 7.41(2), Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(1)(a). To observe public aspects of the voting 
process means to see and hear the election activities that take place at an 
observable location, from an established observer area, except for confidential 
information. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 4.02(12), (16), 4.03(10).
Other specific things that observers can do to facilitate their observation:
 Ɋ Observers may communicate with and ask questions of the designated 
election official as explained during the check-in process. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.04(4), (14). Observers may communicate with and ask 
questions of other election officials at the discretion of the designated 
election official as explained during the check in process. Id.
Best Practice: Generally, the designated election official should 
be available for observer questions during the day as time allows. 
Other election officials — unless otherwise required — do not need 
to be available to observers. A reasonable balance may be for the 
designated election official to explain that other officials may answer 
questions when they are not busy. During some elections, other 
officials may be actively working on tasks the entire day.
 Ɋ If an observer asks an election official to repeat a name or address that 
has just been said aloud, the official must repeat the name once and 
may repeat the name or address again at the official’s discretion. Wis. 
Admin. Code § EL 4.03(6).
 Ɋ Observers may view the public portions of the poll book during times 
when voting and voters will not be impeded in the designated election 
official’s discretion. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.45(1m), 7.41(4), Wis. Admin. Code § EL 
4.03(8). This excludes all confidential and protected voters.
Note: This includes Badger Books used as e-pollbooks but does not include 
a Badger Book currently being used to register voters. Election officials 
must disclose the existence of any confidential poll book, how many voters 
appear on the list, and the number of them who have voted. Wis. Stat. § 7.41(4).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 18
 Ɋ Observers may converse with other observers as necessary as long as 
such conversation is quiet and does not distract voters or election officials. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(8).
 Ɋ Observers may move between established observer areas in a manner 
explained by the designated election official during the check-in process. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.41(2), Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(3).
 Ɋ Observers may silently text and send emails. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(12).
 Ɋ Observers may view absentee certificate envelopes that have been set 
aside to be rejected at the discretion of the designated election official. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(7).
Best Practice: Election officials may wish to establish a specific 
location, or specific times, for observers to view rejected envelopes so 
that all observers may see them.
 Ɋ An observer who is an elector, which means an adult U.S. citizen who 
has resided in a Wisconsin election district or ward for 28 consecutive 
days, and who is not disqualified from voting due to any reason such as a 
current felony conviction, may challenge a voter for cause in accordance 
with Wis. Stat. §§ 6.925, 6.93, 6.935, Wis. Admin. Code Ch. EL 9, § EL 4.04(5).
Note: Because only voters, election officials, and observers are allowed 
in the voting area. Wis. Stat. § 5.35(4), a person waiting to challenge a 
voter needs to sign in as an observer. See Wis. Stat. § 5.35(4).
 Ɋ If a voter approaches an observer and asks for assistance, the 
observer may provide that assistance under Wis. Stat. §§ 6.82, 6.87(5), 
or 6.875(6)(c)1. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(15), provided they are not the 
voter’s employer or an agent of that employer or an officer or agent of a 
labor organization which represents the voter.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 19
Sec. 14.5.7: What Observers May Not Do
To ensure the orderly operation of an observable location, and to protect the 
right of voters to vote securely and privately, there are regulations limiting 
observer activities and observers must follow the designated election official’s 
instructions. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(2).
 Ɋ Observers may not physically handle any election related materials or 
materials provided by the voter. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 4.02(12), EL 4.03(9), 
see Wis. Stat. § 5.87(1).
 Ɋ Observers may not view confidential information, including specific 
items but also may include other documents that a voter brings to an 
observable location. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 4.02(5), EL 4.03(10).
Confidential information includes an operator’s license or identification 
card number issued by the Wisconsin department of transportation, 
a birth date, a social security number or any portion thereof, 
accommodation information on a voter registration form, information 
concerning a confidential voter, guardianship information, a voted ballot, 
and a communication by a voter to a person rendering voting assistance 
under Wis. Stat. §§ 6.82, 6.87(5), or 6.875(6)(c)1. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 
4.02(5). The type of document submitted as photo ID is not confidential 
information, even though the information printed on it is. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.02(5). The designated election official, and all election officials, 
must take care to ensure that confidential information is not shown to 
observers. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(10).
 Ɋ Observers may not engage in any loud, boisterous, or otherwise 
disruptive behavior, that, in the discretion of the designated election 
official, threatens the orderly conduct of the election or interferes with 
voting or registration. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(6), see Wis. Stat. § 7.41(3)(a).
 Ɋ Observers may not engage in electioneering or distribute election 
related material. Wis. Stat. § 7.41(3)(b), Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.02(18), (15), 
EL 4.04(9). Observers cannot display the name or likeness of, or text related 
to, a candidate, party, or referendum position appearing on the ballot, 
or display text which describes, states, or implies that the observer is an 
election official. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(10).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 20
 Ɋ An observer also may not engage in any conversation concerning a 
candidate, party, or question appearing on the ballot. Wis. Admin. Code 
§ EL 4.04(11).
 Ɋ Observers may not use a communication device inside an observer area 
to make an audio or video communication, such as making a phone call 
or livestreaming the event. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(12).
 Ɋ Observers may not create or transmit photos, videos, or audio 
recordings at a polling place, in-person absentee voting site, or SVD 
voting site. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(7).
 Ɋ Observers may not initiate a conversation with a voter. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.04(13). If a voter initiates a conversation with an observer, the 
observer may briefly respond in a way that does not interfere with other 
voters, such as by explaining that the observer is observing the election 
and may also refer the voter to an election official if appropriate. Id. A brief 
wave or greeting to a known individual is not prohibited. Id.
Sec. 14.5.8: Limiting 
Number of Observers
The designated election official may limit the number of observers representing 
the same organization due to the physical limitations of the location or the 
orderly administration of the election, or both. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(4), 
see Wis. Stat. § 7.41(1). If any organization is limited for this reason, the same limit 
must apply to all organizations. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(4). The designated 
election official will use the organizations that have been entered on the 
observer log to make this determination. See Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 4.02(14), 
(17), EL 4.03(2)(a), EL 4.04(1).
Note: If the designated election official limits the number of observers, 
they may need to monitor the number of observers leaving the site, as 
this may allow other observers to take their place. Observers can be 
instructed to tell the designated election official when they are leaving.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 21
Sec. 14.5.9: Communications Media
A communications media member is an individual who notifies the designated 
election official that the individual seeks to record or report information 
outside the voting area. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 4.02(4), EL 4.07(1). This is a broad 
definition that includes more than just traditional media members such as 
those working for an established newspaper, TV station, or radio station. See 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.02(4). The voting area is where voters receive, prepare, 
or deposit their ballots, or where voters cast their votes on a voting machine. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.02(18).
If a media member wishes to record information outside of the voting area, they 
must identify themselves and any organization they represent, which may be 
a business or a non-profit, to the designated election official. Wis. Admin. Code 
§ EL 4.07(1).
Note: While this information is not recorded on the observer log unless 
the media member also signs in as an observer, it should be recorded 
on the Inspectors’ Statement.
The designated election official, working with the Chief Inspector or clerk 
as necessary, may in their discretion allow the media member to use 
video and still cameras outside of the voting area. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 
4.07(1), (2). Any camera use may not allow the recording of any confidential 
information — including voted ballots — and may not disrupt or interfere with the 
election. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.07(1). The designated election official may limit 
the amount of time any media member may use cameras at a site, but any 
such limitation must be uniformly applied to all media members. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.07(2).
Any media member that wishes to observe the election from within the voting 
area must sign in as an observer and be bound by all applicable observer rules, 
including applicable limitations on camera use. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.07(3).
Note: While media members may record information at the discretion 
of the designated election official, they must be outside of the voting 
area and therefore have less access than observers, and they have no 
right to see and hear all election activities taking place at a site unless 
they sign in as an observer and agree to be bound by all observer rules.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 22
Best Practice: Municipal clerks should work with Chief Inspectors 
and designated election officials as necessary to establish an area 
where media members may record non-confidential information. 
Appropriate placement will depend on the layout of the site. Further, 
specific times of the day could be established for media purposes. 
Media members wishing to appear at a site are encouraged to notify 
a municipal clerk well ahead of the election to provide time to explain 
any regulations that will be placed on media members at a site. 
Likewise, clerks are encouraged to contact any media members and 
explain any regulations if they happen to be aware that the media 
member wishes to appear at a voting site.
Sec. 14.5.10: Warning & Removing 
Election Observers
Election observers agree to follow the observer rules when they sign the 
observer log. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(1). An observer who does not follow 
the rules or election official instructions may be warned and, if the conduct 
continues, removed from the site. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 4.03(11), EL 4.04(2), see 
Wis. Stat. § 7.41(3).
The following process must be followed if the designated election official 
believes that an observer has violated an applicable rule or statute:
1. The designated election official shall first, verbally or in writing, warn 
an observer to cease conduct that violates an observer rule, statute, or 
direction of the official. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(11).
Best Practice: The warning should very clearly explain what conduct 
caused an issue and how to resolve that issue. For example, an 
observer who continues to start conversations with voters should be 
told observers cannot initiate conversations with voters, warned to 
stop doing so, and told that a failure to stop could result in the observer 
being asked to leave the location.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 23
2. If an observer does not cease the offending conduct following a warning, 
the designated election official may issue a written order to the observer 
to depart the location. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(11)(a), (b), see Wis. Stat. 
§ 7.41(3). If the designated election official is not either the Chief Inspector 
or municipal clerk, the designated election official must notify the Chief 
Inspector or municipal clerk and explain the circumstances. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.03(11)(a).
The written order must be given to the observer and must state the reason 
the order was issued and contain the signature of the designated election 
official. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(11)(b). The Commission has established 
the Order to Leave Observable Location (EL-110) for this purpose. If the 
designated election official issuing the order was appointed from a 
partisan list of nominees, the official must offer to an official appointed 
from another partisan list of nominees, if such a person is available on a 
timely basis, the opportunity to sign the form and to note any agreement 
or disagreement with the order. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(11)(c). The order 
is valid regardless of whether an official from another party list is available 
to review the order or disagrees with the order. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 
4.03(11)(c). A copy of the order must be kept with the Inspectors’ Statement 
and also sent to the Commission within 60 days of the incident. See Wis. 
Admin. Code § EL 4.03(11)(d).
3. After an observer has been given a written order to leave, the observer 
must leave the location. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(11)(b). If the observer 
refuses to leave, the designated election official may summon law 
enforcement to remove the observer from the location. Wis. Stat. § 7.37(2), 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(11)(b).
4. If any observer was ordered to leave the location, a copy of the order and 
of any other documentation associated with the order must be provided 
to the Commission within 60 days of the incident. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 
4.03(11)(d). The copy can be a scan or picture of the order that was kept 
with the Inspectors’ Statement.
Best Practice: Before reaching the warning stage, the designated 
election official may remind an observer about the content of the 
observer rules or of a specific instruction from an election official. 
A conflict between an observer and an election official can itself be 
disruptive, and reasonable steps to avoid a disruptive conflict are 
worthwhile.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 24
Sec. 14.5.11: Location-
Specific Requirements
The rules covered in this chapter apply generally to all observable locations, 
but certain aspects are site specific, such as operating hours. Please review the 
following rules for any sites you will be involved with.
Polling Places
Election observation must begin at 7 a.m. or whenever the tabulators are zeroed 
out on election day, whichever is earlier, and observation ends at 8 p.m. or 
whenever the last voter in line has voted and left the polling place. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.05(1)(a), see Wis. Stat. § 7.41(1). To ensure that observers may begin 
at 7 a.m. or when tabulators are zeroed out, observers should be able to check 
in ten minutes before this time. Observers are allowed to remain after voting 
concludes to attend the canvass meeting under Wisconsin’s open meetings 
law, but no observer may create or transmit photographs, videos, or audio 
recordings of the interior of the observable location until the public canvassing 
meeting has begun. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(1). If any observer is allowed 
access outside of these time frames, all observers must be allowed the same 
access. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(1)(a).
In-Person Absentee Voting (IPAV) in a 
Clerk’s Office or Alternate Site
Election observation is allowed during all hours that IPAV activity may occur at 
any site. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(2)(a), see Wis. Stat. § 7.41(1). However, if voting 
occurs in a clerk’s private home, the clerk is not required to permit observation. 
See Wis. Stat. § 7.41(1), Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(2)(a). Observation of the return 
of voted by-mail absentee ballots to a municipal clerk’s office or alternate site 
by the United States postal service is not covered by the Observer rule unless 
it occurs in the same location and during the same hours as the issuing and 
voting of absentee ballots. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(2)(a). The initial enclosing 
and securing of absentee ballots at an IPAV site is covered by the rule and 
observable. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(2)(b).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 25
Board of Absentee Ballot Canvassers (“Central Count”)
The meeting of the Board of Absentee Ballot Canvassers is most commonly 
but colloquially referred to as “central count.” It is where a municipality has 
elected to count all absentee ballots at a central location. See Wis. Stat. § 7.52.6 
Many municipalities statewide use this process to tally their absentee ballots. 
Election Observation is allowed during all hours when an absentee ballot 
board of canvassers is meeting to canvass absentee ballots beginning no 
later than when the tabulators are zeroed out. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(3)(a), 
see Wis. Stat. § 7.52(1)(a). Observers may create or transmit photographs, 
videos, or audio recordings of the observable location, but may not record any 
confidential information, including any voted ballot, or any individual returning a 
ballot or correcting a certificate envelope. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(3)(b).
Special Voting Deputy (SVD) Voting Sites
Regulations for the observers who may accompany SVDs to a facility are 
discussed in Chapter 7.3: “Types of Absentee Voters: SVDs” concerning SVD 
voting. See Wis. Stat. § 6.875(7), Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(4).
Recount
Election observation is allowed during the hours a recount is occurring. Wis. 
Admin. Code § EL 4.05(5)(a), see Wis. Stat. § 9.01(1)(b)11. The petitioner of the 
recount, all opposing candidates, interested persons, and all associated 
counsel are not election observers and not subject to the observer rule, though 
they must identify themselves as such to the designated election official. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(5)(b), see Wis. Stat. § 9.01(3). Though there will not 
be any check-in table or registration table, the designated election official 
must establish at least one observation area. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(5)(c). 
Observers may create and transmit photos, videos, or audio recordings of the 
location, but may not record any confidential information. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 
4.05(5)(d).
“Central Count”
Election observation is allowed at any central counting location where all 
ballots for a municipality, or municipalities, are counted. Wis. Admin. Code 
§ EL 4.05(6)(a), see Wis. Stat. § 5.87(1). Though there will not be any check-in 
table or registration table, the designated election official must establish at 
least one observation area in which observers may observe the proceedings.  
6 Statutes distinguish between what is commonly called “central count,” which is actually a meeting of 
the Board of Absentee Ballot Canvassers, and an actual central counting location where all the ballots 
of a municipality are counted. See below.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 26
See Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(6)(a). Observers may create and transmit 
photos, videos, or audio recordings of the location, but may not record any 
confidential information. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(6)(b).
Note: Do not confuse this “central count” with the board of absentee 
ballot canvassers above. This “central count” is an option for counting 
all in-person election day ballots at a central location, which may be 
delegated by multiple municipal clerks to their county clerk. See Wis. 
Stat. §§ 5.84, 5.85(1), (5), 5.86, and 5.87(1).
Sec. 14.5.12: After Observation Ends
After all voting activity has concluded within the observable location, 
candidates may be present and the prohibition of creating or transmitting 
photographs, videos, and audio recordings does not apply unless such action is 
disruptive or interferes with the administration of the election. Wis. Admin. Code 
§ EL 4.06. At a polling place, observers may remain as members of the public 
to watch the canvass proceedings, but cannot create photos, videos, or audio 
recordings until the canvass begins. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(1)(b).
The designated election official must return the observer log to the clerk after 
the observable activities have concluded. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(2)(d).
Any documents that must be provided to the Commission can be sent to 
elections@wi.gov.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 27
Chapter 14.6: Election Day 
Registration
Sec. 14.6.1: Introduction
Wisconsin is one of about thirty states that permit same-day voter registration 
at the polling place on Election Day, meaning unregistered voters can register to 
vote prior to casting their ballots. Wis. Stat. § 6.55.1 This is known as Election Day 
Registration (EDR), or colloquially as “same-day registration.”
All voters must be registered before being issued a ballot unless specifically 
exempt from registration. Wis. Stat. § 6.27. New and former Wisconsin residents 
voting Presidential-only ballots are not required to register, and military voters voting 
by absentee ballot are also not required to register. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.15, 6.18, 6.22(3).
Note: Although military voters are not required to register, they may still 
choose to do so. Military voters can utilize special federal forms to accomplish 
registration. See Chapter 7.4: “Types of Absentee Voters: UOCAVA” for  more 
information about how military voters may register to vote.
Sec. 14.6.2: Does the Voter 
Need to Register?
Every polling place must have a designated area set up to register voters.
When a voter enters the polling place, poll workers should ask:
1. “Are you registered to vote?”
a. If no: Voter directed to registration table
b. If yes: Proceed to Question 2
2. “Have you changed your name or moved since the last time you registered?”
a. If no: Voter can proceed to poll book
b. If yes: Voter likely needs to re-register
1 Statute refers to this process as “polling place registration,” but it is more commonly known as 
election day registration.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 28
Voters may not know that they are not registered, or that they need to re-
register, until they attempt to check in to the poll book to request their ballot.
If poll workers cannot find a voter’s name in the poll book, prior to 
directing the voter to re-register, they should take the following steps:
 Ɋ Confirm that the voter is at the correct ward in the correct polling place. If 
the voter is at the wrong polling place or ward, provide instructions for how 
to get to the right location. Wis. Stat. § 6.55(3)(a).
 Ɋ Confirm the spelling of the voter’s last name, particularly if it contains an 
apostrophe, to see if it appears in a different part of the poll book.
 Ɋ Confirm whether the voter is a Protected Voter (like a judicial officer). If 
they are, their name will appear in a separate section of the poll book.
If the poll worker confirms that none of the scenarios above apply, the voter 
should be directed to re-register.
Best Practice: Voters may sometimes get upset when they are told 
they need to re-register, especially if they believe they have always 
voted at the same polling place with no issues. There could be several 
reasons why a voter’s name does not appear in the poll book, even if 
the voter insists that they are registered.
If the voter is very insistent, poll workers can suggest that they try to 
contact the office of the municipal clerk, either before casting their 
ballot or after re-registering, whatever they prefer. The municipal clerk 
will be able to examine their voter record and determine the reason 
they do not appear in the poll book.
Another option is to reassure the voter that they can re-register today 
at the polling place, be permitted to vote, and then work with the 
municipal clerk after voting to ensure that their voter record is correct.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 29
Sec. 14.6.3: Confirming Voter 
Not on DOC Ineligible List
If a voter needs to register, or re-register, prior to having them begin the 
registration process, poll workers must first check and see if the voter’s name 
appears on the Department of Corrections (“DOC”) list of ineligible voters. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.55(2)(cs). If the voter’s name appears on the list, the poll workers 
shall inform the voter that they are not eligible to register to vote. Id. If the 
voter insists that they are eligible, poll workers shall permit them to vote, but 
shall mark “ineligible to vote per Department of Corrections” on the Voter 
Registration Application (EL-131). Wis. Stat. § 6.55(2)(cs). If the voter wishes to 
cast a ballot after registering, poll workers shall start the voting process and 
then must challenge the voter under Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(dm). See Chapter 14.7: 
“Challenging Electors on Election Day” on page 43 for more information on 
challenging voters.
Sec. 14.6.4: Completing the Voter 
Registration Application
Voters completing an EDR fill out a Voter Registration Application (EL-131) in the 
same way a voter would prior to Election Day.
Proper completion of the Voter Registration Application (EL-131) will ease the 
process of entering information into WisVote and ensure the voter will appear 
on the poll list for Election Day. See Wis. Stat. § 6.36(2)(a). It is the responsibility of 
the municipal clerk to notify a voter of any insufficiency in their voter registration 
application within 5 days, if possible. Wis. Stat. § 6.32(2).
Best Practice: If the voter is missing proof of residence, the Missing POR 
Notification Letter (EL-132) is a customizable template that is available 
for this purpose.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 30
Front Side of Voter Registration Application
This is a summary of each entry on the EL-131 voting registration form:
1. Qualifications
Registrants must be able to check each of the four boxes in this section. These 
boxes correlate with the legal qualifications for voting. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.02, 6.03.
The registrant can register at 17 years old if they will be 18 years old by the time 
of the next election. Wis. Stat. § 6.05.
Note: A person less than 18 years of age cannot vote in a primary election 
even if they will be 18 years old on the date of the general election.
2. Your Name
The registrant should provide their full name as it appears both on their 
Wisconsin Driver License or Wisconsin DOT–Issued Identification Card, if 
applicable, and the proof of residence document provided for box 8 on the 
form. Wis. Stat. § 6.33(1).
3. About You
Date of Birth
Month, day, and year of birth is required. Wis. Stat. § 6.33(1). Best Practice: Voters 
should be encouraged to use all four digits of their year of birth (e.g. “1989” 
instead of “89”), but if someone writes the last two digits, the application should 
not be rejected.
Contact Information: Phone Number and Email Address (OPTIONAL)
Registration applications should not be rejected because a voter has failed 
to, or refuses to, provide their phone number or email address. Voters are not 
required to provide their phone number or email address, but doing so is useful 
in case the clerk needs to contact the voter to correct errors in the registration 
form or paperwork. See Wis. Stat. § 6.32. Clerks can still contact voters at the 
addresses they provide, but email or phone may be quicker and more efficient 
to resolve issues in a timely manner.
Be Advised: Any phone or email address provided becomes a public record.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 31
4. The Address Where You Live
This is the voter’s address where they reside, which may be different than their 
mailing address. Wis. Stat. § 6.10.
This section will also be used to indicate military or permanent overseas 
statuses. Military voters are not required to register to vote but some choose 
to do so anyways. Wis. Stat. § 6.22(3). Overseas voters, both permanent and 
temporary, are required to register. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.24(3), 6.27. See Chapter 7.4: 
“Types of Absentee Voters: UOCAVA” for more information on military and 
overseas voter registration.
5. Your Mailing Address (OPTIONAL)
Voters are not required to provide mailing addresses to register, though doing 
so is often a good idea in case their municipal clerk needs to contact them. 
Registration applications should not be rejected because a voter has failed to, 
or refused to, provide a mailing address.
Best Practice: If the registrant does not receive mail at their residential 
address, a mailing address should be provided. Failure to provide 
a valid mailing address may result in the voter registration being 
inactivated since they would not be able to receive the verification 
postcard.
6. Prior Registration Information
Name Change
A voter is required to re-register if they change their legal name. See Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.33(5)(a)1.
Best Practice: As it may take some time for a voter to receive 
proper documentation of their new legal name, a registered voter 
may continue to vote using their former name until the necessary 
documentation to re-register is received.
Address Change
A registered voter is required to re-register if they wish to vote from their new 
residence. See Wis. Stat. §§ 6.10(1), 6.33(5)(a)1.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 32
7. Identification
To complete the Voter Registration Application (EL-131) or online voter 
registration, the registrant must provide either a Wisconsin Driver License 
number or Wisconsin DOT–issued Identification Card number, or the last four 
digits of their Social Security Number. Wis. Stat. § 6.33(1). This does not require 
visual confirmation by the clerk or a poll worker, and the voter may write down 
or recite the number if they choose. A voter cannot be required to show their WI 
Driver License, WI DOT–issued ID card, or Social Security card as a condition of 
registration, unless the voter is offering it as acceptable proof of residence. Wis. 
Stat. §§ 6.33(1), 6.34(2), (2m).
Wisconsin Driver License or Wisconsin DOT–issued ID Card Number
If the registrant has been issued either a WI Driver License or WI DOT–issued 
ID Card that is unexpired or not cancelled, they must provide the number 
and expiration date, even if the registrant’s driving privileges were revoked or 
suspended. Wis. Stat. § 6.33(1) (requiring a “valid” license or ID).
Best Practice: If the registrant has a WI Driver License or WI DOT–Issued 
ID Card that is unexpired but does not have the number or expiration 
date readily available, they may call DMV at (608) 266-1069, option 1, to 
look up the information.
If the registrant has either a WI Driver License or WI DOT–Issued ID Card that is 
currently expired or cancelled, they must provide the last 4-digits of the Social 
Security Number instead. Wis. Stat. § 6.33(1).
Social Security Number: Last Four Digits
If the registrant has not been issued a WI Driver License or WI DOT–Issued ID 
Card or the document is expired or cancelled, the registrant must provide the 
last 4-digits of their Social Security Number. Wis. Stat. § 6.33(1).
If the registrant does not have either a WI Driver License or WI DOT–Issued ID 
Card and has not been issued a Social Security Number, they may indicate 
this by checking the box signifying they have none of these documents. See 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 3.04(3)  implementing 52 U.S.C. § 21083(5)(A)(ii). Voters 
in this circumstance check the appropriate box on the EL-131 but still must 
be permitted to register to vote.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 33
8. Proof of Residence
The registrant will affirm that they are providing a valid form of proof of 
residence with this application. The document must list the voter’s complete 
name and Wisconsin residential address. See Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(b).
9. Signature and Certification
The registrant should read this language before certifying that they are an 
eligible voter and have resided in the ward for at least 28 days. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.33(2)(b). They will provide their signature and the date they are signing. Id.
10. Assistant
If the registrant required another person to complete this form on their 
behalf due to a physical disability, the assistant should sign and provide their 
residential address here. Wis. Stat. §6.33(2)(a).
Official Use Only Section
The election official receiving the application (clerk, poll worker, election 
registration official)2 should review the proof of residence. See Wis. Stat. 
§§ 6.32(2), 6.33(1), 6.34.
Proof of Residence Types
At the top of the section, the official will circle the Proof of Residence type. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.33(2)(a).
The following are the Proof of Residence types listed by their abbreviations in the 
Voter Registration Application (EL-131). See Sec. 14.6.5: “Proof of Residence” on 
page 37 for more specific information on what constitutes sufficient proof of 
residence or is required in each category.
 Ɋ WI DL: Wisconsin DOT–Issued Driver License.
 Ɋ WI ID: Wisconsin DOT–Issued Identification Card.
 Ɋ UTIL: Any form of utility bill.
 Ɋ BANK/CC: A bank or credit card statement.
 Ɋ PYCK: Paycheck or Paystub.
 Ɋ STDNT ID: A student ID that includes a photo (address not required).
 Ɋ GOV DOC: Any government-issued document.
2 Any staff member, or any other individual, appointed as an ERO would need to complete election 
official training before registering any qualified voter to vote.” Wis. Stat. § 7.315(4).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 34
 Ɋ LSE: A residential lease (may not be used to register by mail).
 Ɋ GOV ID: Any other identification card or license issued by a Wisconsin 
governmental body or unit.
 Ɋ EMPL ID: An identification card issued by an employer in the normal course 
of business and bearing a photo of the card holder.
 Ɋ RES CARE: A contract or intake document from an occupant of a 
residential care facilities that specifies the occupant currently resides in 
the facility.
 Ɋ TAX: A real estate tax bill or receipt for the current year or the year 
preceding the date of the election.
 Ɋ HMLSS: A signed letter on public or private social security agency 
letterhead identifying a homeless voter and describing the individual’s 
residence for voting purposes.
Proof of Residence Issuing Entity
The clerk should write the name of the entity that issued the proof of residence 
document. Wis. Stat. § 6.33(1). This is necessary to ensure that the proof of 
residence satisfies one of the legal types. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a).
Examples: WI DMV, MG&E, WE Energies, etc.
Proof of Residence Number, if any
If a unique number associated with the proof of residence is seven or more 
digits in length, the last four digits should be recorded here. See Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.36(1)(a)12. For example, bank accounts are usually ten digits long, but only 
the last four digits should be recorded.
If a unique number associated with the proof of residence is six or fewer digits 
in length, only the last two digits are recorded here. See Id. For example, if an ID 
has five digits, only the last two would be recorded.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 35
Date Complete/POR Received
Best Practice: If the application is complete and the proof of 
residence is acceptable (see below), the election official who received 
the application (election registration official, clerk or poll worker) 
should date it.
Election Day Voter Number
If registering on Election Day, the voter will be issued a voter number before 
they are given their ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(b). This number should also 
be recorded on the supplemental poll list. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(c). For more 
information on the Election Day Registration (EDR) procedures, See Chapter 14.6: 
“Election Day Registration” on page 28.
WisVote ID #
This number is assigned by the clerk only once the registration has been 
entered in WisVote. See Wis. Stat. § 6.36(1)(a)4.
Confidential Voter ID #
If the registrant has registered through the confidential voter process, they will 
be issued a number that should be listed here. See Wis. Stat. §§ 6.33, 6.47(3).
Official’s signature
 Ɋ The election official first receiving the application (election registration 
official, clerk, poll worker) should review the form for completeness and 
legibility before signing. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.32, 6.33(1).
 Ɋ If the application is incomplete or illegible, the election official should not 
sign the form. See Wis. Stat. § 6.32(2).
 Ɋ If the registrant is still available, ask the registrant to complete the missing 
information or print more clearly. See Wis. Stat. § 6.32(2).
Best Practice: If the registrant is no longer available, the election official 
should submit this form to the clerk with a note explaining the deficiency.
 Ɋ The clerk should contact the registrant within 5 days of receipt of any 
incomplete or illegible applications. Wis. Stat. § 6.32(2).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 36
Bottom Row
The clerk should record this information to properly determine the offices 
and referenda for which a voter is eligible to vote. This is entered when the 
registration is processed. In the case of Election Day registrations, it must 
be done right away to make sure the voter gets the correct ballot. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.33(1).
 Ɋ Ward
 Ɋ Sch. District
 Ɋ Alder
 Ɋ City Supr.
 Ɋ Ct. of App
 Ɋ Assembly
 Ɋ St. Senate
 Ɋ Congress
Back Side of Voter Registration Application
If the registrant resides somewhere without a designated address, the registrant 
should use the map in box 4 on the backside to diagram where they reside in 
relation to known streets or landmarks. See Wis. Stat. § 6.10(1).
If the registrant needs accommodations at their polling place (curbside voting, 
braille materials, etc.), they should make those requests on the bottom of the 
application. See Wis. Stat. § 6.36(1)(a)10.
If the registrant is interested in being a poll worker, they can indicate in the 
checkbox on the bottom of the application.
Sec. 14.6.5: Proof of Residence
Everyone (except military and permanent overseas voters and new and former 
Wisconsin residents) who wishes to register to vote or update their voter 
registration must present a proof of residence document to prove that the 
person resides at the address listed on the Voter Registration Application (EL-131). 
Wis. Stat. § 6.34. If the person is registering in-person with an election official, 
they do not need to provide a copy of the document for the election official to 
keep; they may simply show it to the election official so the official can verify the 
registration in real time. See Wis. Stat. §§ 6.32, 6.34.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 37
Best Practice: Electronic versions of proof of residence are acceptable 
and can be displayed on a phone or other electronic device. The 
document must contain the voter’s current and complete name 
and current and complete Wisconsin residential address. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.34(3)(b). If the document has an expiration date, it must not be 
expired on the day registration is made. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(c). Note 
that some documents (like a utility bill) do not have an expiration date 
and are still considered valid.
Note: Types of acceptable proof of residence are specifically listed by 
state law. Wis. Stat. § 6.34 . If a proof of residence document does not 
fall into one of the following categories, it cannot serve as acceptable 
proof of residence.
The following documents constitute acceptable proof of residence:
 Ɋ An unexpired Wisconsin Driver License or receipt for license, even if driving 
privileges were revoked or suspended. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)1.
 Ɋ An unexpired Wisconsin DOT–Issued Identification Card or receipt for 
identification card. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)2.
 Ɋ Any other official identification card or license issued by a Wisconsin 
governmental body or unit, unless otherwise prohibited by state law. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)3. See Wis. Stat. § 66.0438 for special guidelines for locally-
issued identification documents.
 Ɋ Any identification card issued by an employer in the normal course of 
business and bearing a photo of the card holder, but not including a 
business card. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)4.
 Ɋ A real estate tax bill or receipt for the current year or the year preceding 
the date of the registration. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)5.
 Ɋ A utility bill for the period commencing not earlier than 90 days before 
Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)8.
 ɉ Examples include gas, electric, water, sewer, telephone/cell phone, 
cable/satellite, TV, Internet, etc.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 38
 Ɋ A residential lease. A residential lease should contain, at minimum:
 Ɉ Landlord Name
 Ɉ Tenant Name
 Ɉ Address Subject to Lease
 Ɉ Term
 Ɉ Rent
 Ɉ Landlord Signature
 Ɉ Tenant Signature
 ɉ If one or more of these are missing, it is up to the clerk to determine if 
the information contained is sufficient for proof of residence.
Note: A residential lease cannot be used as proof residence if a voter is 
registering by mail or online. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(2).
 Ɋ A university, college or technical institute identification card (must 
include photo, address not required) with one of the following:
 ɉ A fee payment receipt issued to the cardholder by the university, 
college, or technical college dated no earlier than 9 months before the 
date of the election. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)7.a.
 ɉ A certified and current list of students who reside in housing sponsored 
by the university, college, or technical college. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)7.b.
Note: This list should be provided by the university, college, or technical 
college and the election official must verify that the student presenting 
the card is included on the list.
 Ɋ Bank statement. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)9. This can include:
 Ɉ A bank statement issued by a bank or credit union
 Ɉ A mortgage statement issued by a bank or credit union
 Ɉ A home equity credit line statement issued by a bank or credit union
 Ɉ A credit card statement issued by a bank or credit union, but not an 
offer for a credit card
 Ɉ Paycheck or paystub documentation of direct deposit. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.34(3)(a)10.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 39
 Ɋ A check or other document issued by a unit of government. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.34(3)(a)10.
 ɉ Units of government include federal, state, county, municipal, school 
district, tribal, etc., both foreign and domestic.
 ɉ Examples of government documents (not an exhaustive list):
 Ɉ Vehicle registration
 Ɉ BadgerCare/Medicare statements3
 Ɉ Social Security statements3
 Ɉ Public school correspondence
 Ɉ Federal student loan notices3
 Ɉ Change of Address letter from the U.S.P.S.
 Ɉ Government-owned medical facilities, such as UW Hospitals and 
Clinics, county-owned health care clinics and VA Hospitals
 Ɉ A signed letter on public (government) social service agency 
letterhead identifying a homeless voter and describing the 
individual’s residence for voting purposes.
 Ɋ A contract or intake document from an occupant of a residential care 
facility, such as a nursing or group home, or community-based residential 
facility that specifies that the occupant currently resides in the facility. A 
room number is not required. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)12.
 Ɋ An identification card issued by a federally recognized Native American 
Wisconsin tribe. Wis. Stat. § 6.34(3)(a)(13).
Electronic Proof of Residence
Otherwise valid proof of residence documents presented electronically on 
a device such as a laptop, smartphone, or tablet are acceptable for voter 
registration purposes.4 Electronic forms of proof of residence are not prohibited 
by state law. See Wis. Stat. § 6.34, which requires only “an identifying document.”
3 If issued by a government agency
4 On August 28, 2012, the G.A.B. voted unanimously to allow electronic versions of acceptable proof of 
residence during the voter registration process.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 40
The standard of review should be the same whether the document is 
physically presented to an election official or viewed in an electronic format. 
See Id.
Election officials may not reject  an otherwise valid proof of residence 
merely because it is in an electronic format. See Id.  (requiring only “an 
identifying document”).
If the document is not readable in the format presented or the election official is 
otherwise unable to determine that it constitutes a valid proof of residence, the 
election official may reject the document and require the registrant to present 
another form of proof of residence. See Wis. Stat. § 6.34(2).
Election officials are not required to provide computers or Internet access for 
voters to use to obtain electronic copies of proof of residence to facilitate voter 
registration. It is the responsibility of the registrant to show or provide a copy of 
their proof of residence. Id.
Election officials are not required to handle a registrant’s electronic device but 
may do so with the registrant’s permission.
Best Practice: If the proof of residence is presented using the 
registrant’s electronic device, the election official may ask the 
registrant to expand the size of the type or image, or otherwise 
navigate the document or image so that the election official may view 
any required information (i.e., name of the registrant, address, account 
number, date, type of document, and issuing entity or institution).
Sec. 14.6.6: EDR for Confidential Voters
If a voter at the polling place says they are not registered, but also that they 
wish to register as a confidential voter, the registration cannot be completed 
at the polling place. Instead, the voter must be directed to the office of the 
municipal clerk to register, even on Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.55(2)(cm). The 
municipal clerk shall then follow the registration process for confidential voters 
described by Wis. Stat. § 6.47(3). See Chapter 14.10: “Confidential Voters at the 
Polling Place” on page 84 for more information on confidential voters.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 41
Sec. 14.6.7: Designating a Separate 
Location for Registration
If there are space concerns in the voting area, the governing body of the 
municipality may by resolution designate another readily accessible location 
for registration in the same building. Wis. Stat. § 6.55(2)(c)1.
1. If this process is utilized, the municipal clerk is required to prominently post 
a notice of the registration location at the polling place. Id.
2. Upon completing the registration, the clerk serially numbers the 
registration and gives one copy to the voter for presentation at the polling 
place. Id.
3. The clerk shall also issue a certificate addressed to the poll workers at the 
polling place directing that the voter be permitted to cast his or her vote. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.55(2)(c)2.
a. The clerk enters the voter’s name and address on the face of the 
certificate, and it should be numbered serially and prepared in 
duplicate. Id.
b. One copy goes with the voter, the other is retained by the clerk. Id.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 42
Chapter 14.7: Challenging 
Electors on Election Day
A challenge is when an voter or election inspector claims, with cause, that 
a voter is not qualified to vote. Wis. Stat. § 6.925. Challenges can be brought 
before Election Day, as well as whenever a voter is casting a ballot, be it in 
person on Election Day or by absentee ballot. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.92–6.97.
This chapter only applies to challenges made on Election Day.
Sec. 14.7.1: What Types of 
Challenges Exist?
There are four types of voter challenges:
 Ɋ Pre-Election Day Challenges to Voter Registration: These challenges are 
brought by voters of the jurisdiction directly to the municipal clerk or board 
of elections commissioner. Do not refer to this chapter for challenges 
brought under Wis. Stat. § 6.48. These challenges follow a different process 
than Election Day challenges.
 Ɋ Election Day Challenges in Person at the Polls by Voters: These challenges 
can be brought in person by any voter of the state at the polling place on 
Election Day, and can only challenge a voter’s qualifications to vote, not 
the process by which they cast their ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.925.
 Ɋ Election Day Challenges to Absentee Voters: At the Polling Place – Wis. 
Stat. § 6.93; At “Central Count” – Wis. Stat. § 7.52(5). These challenges can 
be brought by any voter of the state in the same manner and using the 
same grounds as challenges in person at the polling place.
 Ɋ Election Day Challenges Made by Election Inspectors: These challenges 
can be brought by election inspectors, either against the voter’s 
qualifications to vote, or because the voter has not followed the correct 
voting procedure. Wis. Stat. § 6.92.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 43
Sec. 14.7.2: What is a 
Challenge “for Cause”?
All challenges must be made for cause, regardless of who is bringing the 
challenge and who the voter is. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.92, 6.925. “For cause” means 
there needs to be a legal reason to bring the challenge, something beyond 
speculation or unsubstantiated claims. “For cause” also means that the 
challenge must be made based on personal knowledge or suspicion that the 
voter is not a qualified voter. Challenges based on race, ethnicity, or the ability 
to speak English, and no other basis, are not permitted.
Sec. 14.7.3: What Can be 
Challenged by Electors?
Any elector of the state can challenge a voter’s qualifications to vote, not the process 
by which they cast their ballot. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.03, 6.925. The challenging elector need 
not be an elector of the municipality in which they are making the challenge, they 
just need to be an elector of the state. Note that if a challenger themselves is not 
qualified to vote (i.e. not an elector), they cannot bring the challenge. Id.
Grounds for Qualification Challenges by Electors
 Ɋ Citizenship:  The voter is not a U.S. Citizen.
 Ɋ Age:  The voter is not at least 18 years old on or before Election Day.
 Ɋ Residency:  The voter has not resided in the municipality for at least 28 
days before the election.
 Ɋ Felony Status:  The voter has not completed the terms of their sentence.
 Ɋ Competency to Vote:  The voter has been adjudicated incompetent by a court.
 Ɋ Bet or Wager:  The voter has placed a bet or wager on the election.
 Ɋ Voted Previously at the Same Election:  The voter has already cast a ballot 
for this election. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.02, Wis. Stat. § 6.94.
Electors cannot challenge another voter based on an argument that proper 
voting procedures were not followed. Compare Wis. Stat. § 6.92(1) (which 
permits this type of challenge when made by election inspectors) with Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.925 (which does not for challenges made by electors).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 44
Sec. 14.7.4: What Can be Challenged 
by Election Inspectors?
Election inspectors, also referred to as poll workers, can challenge any of 
the qualification to vote grounds discussed above in Sec. 14.7.3, and may 
also challenge any voter when any of the following situations occur:
