 Ɋ The voter refuses to sign the poll book where physical disability does not 
prevent signing of poll list. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(am).
 Ɋ The voter has insisted they are eligible to vote even though their name 
appears on the Department of Correction’s ineligible list. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.29(2)(am).
 Ɋ The voter has returned their absentee ballot, but their name appears on 
the Department of Correction’s ineligible list. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(c).
 Ɋ A voter completing an EDR has insisted they are eligible to vote even 
though their name appears on the Department of Correction’s ineligible 
list. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.55(2)(cs) and (3)(b), 6.79(2)(dm).
 Ɋ Any other failure to adhere to voting requirements. Wis. Stat. § 6.92
Note: If poll workers believe an absentee certificate envelope is 
insufficient, they should follow the ballot rejection procedures, not the 
voter challenge procedures. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b).
High school election inspectors may not bring challenges. Wis. Stat. § 6.92(2).
Sec. 14.7.5: Challenge 
Procedure & Form EL-104c
Whenever an Election Day challenge is brought, the procedures outlined 
in Wis. Admin. Code EL Chapter 9  should be followed. The Commission has 
prescribed a specific form, Challenge Documentation (EL-104c) , that walks 
election inspectors through all the proper requirements and procedures. The 
EL-104c, as well as the procedures laid out in EL Chapter 9, are the primary 
resources for challenge procedure.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 45
Inspectors are required to complete an EL-104c for every challenge made at the 
polling place, whether or not a ballot is issued to the challenged voter. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 9.05.
A sample of the EL-104c is located at the end of this chapter. Refer to it for more 
detail on how to complete the specific challenge procedure.
Best Practice: Whenever a challenge is made, election inspectors 
should notify the chief election inspector and the municipal clerk 
so that all election officials are aware of the challenge. While the 
municipal clerk is not needed to resolve the challenge, knowing that 
there is one or more challenges will be helpful to them as they prepare 
Election Day materials for the municipal board of canvassers meeting 
following the election.
Sec. 14.7.6: Overview of the 
Challenge Process
The steps below provide a general, narrative overview of the challenge process. 
When administering a challenge, election inspectors should use and refer to the 
EL-104c as that form contains all required procedures, questions, and steps.
 Ɋ Challenger Oath:  When a challenge is made, the challenging voter is 
placed under oath and asked to make a sworn statement giving the 
reason for the challenge and answering questions posed by the election 
inspectors. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.02(1), (2). If the inspector is making the 
challenge, they do not need to be placed under oath.
 Ɋ Inspector Questions:  The election inspector administering the challenge 
follows the questions and procedures on Form EL-104c to administer and 
document the challenge. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.01(2). This includes 
administering an oath to the challenged voter if the voter is voting in 
person, and if an absentee voter is available, and asking questions 
relevant to the challenge. Wis. Stat §§ 6.92(1),6.925, Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 
9.01(1), (2), 9.02(3).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 46
 Ɋ Withdrawing the Challenge:  Once the challenged voter has responded 
to the challenge, the challenging inspector or voter has an opportunity 
to withdraw his or her challenge. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.01(3), 9.02(4). If 
the challenge is withdrawn, a ballot is issued to the voter with no special 
marks, a notation is made in the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104), and no 
mark is made in the poll book. Id.
 Ɋ Oath of Eligibility:  If the challenge is not withdrawn, the election inspector 
administers the affirmation or oath of eligibility to the challenged voter, if 
present. Wis. Stat. § 6.94, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.01(4), 9.02(5).
Best Practice: Indicate “sworn” on the poll book.
 Ɋ When the Voter is not Issued a Ballot:  If the challenged voter refuses 
to answer fully any relevant questions put to him or her by the election 
inspector administering the challenge, they shall not be issued a ballot. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.94, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.01(6), 9.02(7). If answers the 
challenged voter gives to the inspector’s questions demonstrate their 
ineligibility to vote, they shall not be issued a ballot. Id. If the voter refuses 
to take the oath, they shall not be issued a ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.94, Wis. 
Admin. Code § EL 9.01(5), 9.02(6).
 Ɋ When the Voter is issued a Ballot:  If the voter makes the oath and asserts 
their eligibility, and their answers to the inspector’s questions demonstrate 
that they meet the voting requirements, the challenge process is 
complete, and the voter is issued a ballot and permitted to vote. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.94, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.01(7), 9.02(8).
Important: There is no requirement for the challenged voter to provide 
proof or documentation. If they make the oath of eligibility and answer 
all pertinent questions, they shall be issued a ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.94, 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.01(7), 9.02(8).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 47
 Ɋ Voting Procedure:  Once the challenge is complete, a ballot is issued to 
the voter with “Section 6.95” and the voter number marked on the back 
of the ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.95, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.03. If direct-record 
electronic (DRE) voting equipment is used, the ballot should be remade so 
these notations can be added. Id. This notation will signal to canvassers, 
and potential recounters, that the ballot of this voter had been challenged. 
The challenged voter then votes the ballot like normal.
 Ɋ Documentation:   The entire EL-104c must be completed regardless 
of the outcome of the challenge, including if it was withdrawn. The 
completed EL-104c is attached to the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104). 
Election inspectors should also make note of the challenge in the notes 
on the EL-104. Finally, inspectors shall write “Challenged” beside the 
voter’s name in the poll book, along with the reason for the challenge. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.03 .
Sec. 14.7.7: Challenging 
Absentee Voters
The same challenge procedures used for voters at the polling place also apply 
to absentee voters. Wis. Stat. § 6.93, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.04. Only election 
inspectors can challenge absentee ballots that did not comply with voting 
procedures. Wis. Stat. § 6.92(1).
The absentee voter is unlikely to be present in the polling place when their ballot 
is challenged on Election Day. For municipalities using a board of absentee ballot 
canvassers (“central count”), those inspectors may “call before it any person 
whose absentee ballot is challenged.” Wis. Stat. § 7.52(5)(b). Since statute and 
administrative code are clear that absentee voter challenges should be treated 
the same as in-person voter challenges, the Commission believes that election 
inspectors at polling places may also call before them any person whose 
absentee ballot is challenged. Wis. Stat. § 6.93, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.04.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 48
Best Practice: Contact Challenged Absentee Voters.
State law is clear that election inspectors may attempt to contact 
absentee voters who are being challenged to give them an opportunity 
to participate in the challenge process. However, the law is silent on 
how this should be accomplished.
The Commission has issued the following guidance to assist election inspectors:
1. Contacting the challenged absentee voter is discretionary, and the 
decision of whether to do so is made by the election inspectors at the 
polling place or at “central count.” See Wis. Stat. § 7.52(5)(b):
“For the purpose of deciding upon ballots that are challenged 
for any reason, the board of absentee ballot canvassers may 
call before it any person whose absentee ballot is challenged 
if the person is available to be called.” (Emphasis added).
2. Contact by phone is preferred, and inspectors may leave a voicemail 
if given the option to do so.1 If the voter has an email address on file, 
inspectors may also reach out by email. If the voter has neither phone 
number nor email address on file, the election inspectors may, in their 
discretion, attempt to contact the voter by any other feasible means. If 
communication is not possible, the challenge proceeds. See Step 6 Below.
3. If the absentee voter wishes to respond to the challenge, they must do 
so in person before the election inspectors. See Wis. Stat. § 7.52(5)(b). 
Inspectors should not attempt to conduct a challenge over the phone 
because there is no way to verify that the person on the other line is the 
challenged voter.
Best Practice: If the challenged absentee voter does not answer the 
phone, the election inspector placing the call should leave a voicemail 
if possible and give the voter an hour to respond to the message before 
proceeding with the challenge. If it is not possible to leave a voicemail, 
the challenge proceeds.
1 The municipal clerk or their staff may contact challenged voters in addition to election inspectors.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 49
4. If the voter arrives, the inspectors should ask the voter to present photo ID 
so they can confirm that the person is the challenged voter. See Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.79(2)(a) (requiring photo ID for in person voting, which an in-person 
challenged voter would have already completed) Wis. Stat. § 12.13(1)(d) 
(prohibiting any person from impersonating a registered voter or posing 
as another person for the purpose of voting)
5. If the challenged voter does not respond, does not appear, or cannot 
appear, the challenge proceeds. The challenger is placed under oath, and 
answers the questions put to them by the inspectors. See “Best Practice: 
Voter is not available” below. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.02(2).
6. All challenges can be reviewed and decided by the boards of canvassers.
Best Practice: Voter is not available
The law does not answer what election inspectors should do if the 
challenger asserts, under oath, that an absentee voter is not qualified 
to vote, but the absentee voter is not available to appear to complete 
the challenge process.
Normally, a voter is permitted to cast a challenged ballot if the voter swears 
to the oath of eligibility and their answers to inspectors’ questions demon-
strate that they are eligible to vote. Wis. Admin. Code § EL§ 9.01(7), 9.02(8).
But the law does not answer how a challenge should be resolved if 
the absentee voter is unavailable to swear the oath of eligibility and 
answer questions. In such circumstances, fairness to the voter should 
compel election inspectors to accept the challenged voter’s ballot 
under the Wis. Stat. § 6.95, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.03 process.
A determination of the challenge itself would then be made by the 
board of canvassers under a beyond a reasonable doubt standard. 
Wis. Stat. §§ 6.325, 6.95, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.06.
See Sec. 14.7.8: “Review of Challenges by Boards of Canvassers” on 
page 51 for guidance on the beyond a reasonable doubt standard.
In other words – Election inspectors should always count a challenged 
absentee ballot unless the voter appears and gives answers through 
the challenge process that demonstrate they are not eligible to vote, or 
refuses to take an oath.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 50
Sec. 14.7.8: Review of Challenges 
by Boards of Canvassers
The municipal board of canvassers, and, if applicable, the county board of 
canvassers, and the Commission Chairperson can review and decide any 
challenges during their canvasses. Wis. Stat. § 6.95, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.06. 
The “beyond a reasonable doubt” standard should be used to determine the 
validity of challenged ballots. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.325, 6.95, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.06.
Note: Election law does not define “beyond a reasonable doubt” for the 
purpose of voter eligibility. However, in criminal law the term “reasonable 
doubt” means doubt based upon reason and common sense.
It is a doubt for which a reason can be given, arising from a fair and 
rational consideration of the evidence or lack of evidence. It means 
such a doubt as would cause a person of ordinary prudence to pause 
or hesitate when called upon to act in the most important affairs of life.
A reasonable doubt is not a doubt which is based on mere guesswork 
or speculation.
The decisions of the boards of canvassers, and the Commission, can be 
appealed under the recount statute, Wis. Stat. § 9.01, Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.06.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 51
Sec. 14.7.9: Frequently Asked Questions
Q What happens to the EL-104c when it is complete?
A It should be attached to the Inspector’s Statement and retained with all 
other Election Day materials for the boards of canvassers. See Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 9.06.
Q The challenged voter answered all the questions, but the election 
inspector doesn’t believe they’re being truthful, or still isn’t sure if the voter 
is qualified to vote. Can they ask for documentation or proof?
A No. Election inspectors are only permitted “to ask questions which 
are appropriate as determined by the commission, by rule, to test the 
qualifications of the challenged voter.” Wis. Stat. §§ 6.92, 6.925. The 
Commission’s administrative rules specify what those questions are Wis. 
Admin. Code § EL 9.01(2). Neither statute nor administrative rules permit 
election inspectors to ask or require the challenged voter to produce 
documents or proof to resolve the challenge. If the challenged voter 
“answers fully all relevant questions,” takes the required oath of eligibility, 
and their answers indicate that they meet the voting qualification 
requirements, they must be issued a ballot. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 9.01(7), 
9.02(8).
Best Practice: The voter may choose to produce documentation 
voluntarily, and the election inspectors can consider documents if 
offered, but they cannot prompt the challenged voter to do so.
Q Can election inspectors consult with other resources to try and determine 
the outcome of a challenge?
A No. The law provides for a careful process where both the challenger and 
the challenged voter must answer questions and make certain assertions 
under oath. Just like election inspectors cannot ask or compel the voter to 
produce documentation to prove their eligibility, they also cannot consult 
with other resources to try and determine if the voter is eligible. If the voter 
makes the oath of eligibility, and their answers to the questions listed in 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 9.01(2) establish that they are eligible, they must be 
permitted to vote. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 9.01(7), 9.02(8).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 52
Q What if the challenge process becomes disruptive to the polling place?
A Any voter who is abusing the challenge process can be warned and/or 
removed using the same procedures for disruptive election observers. Wis. 
Admin. Code § EL 9.02, Wis. Stat. §§ 7.37(2) and 7.41(3). See Chapter 14.5: 
“Election Observers” on page 13 for more information on removing 
disruptive observers.
Q Do challenges apply to absentee voters?
A Yes. If someone is voting by absentee ballot, their qualifications can still be 
challenged by another voter, using the same process.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 53

ELECTOR 
CHALLENGES
What a challenge 
looks like
When a voter is challenged, the 
election inspector will:
 ●Ask both the person 
challenging and the 
person being challenged, if 
present, to take an oath.
 ●The person being challenged 
will be asked questions 
to determine if they are 
allowed to vote.
 ●The inspectors will use the 
procedures spelled out in 
statute and administrative 
code to determine if the 
challenged elector will 
be issued a ballot.
WIS. STAT. § 6.92; WIS. ADMIN. CODE EL §§ 9.02 – 9.06.
What are Elector 
Challenges?
Any elector may challenge for 
cause any person offering to 
vote whom the elector knows 
or suspects is not a qualified 
elector. WIS. STAT . § 6.925.
A voting challenge is when 
someone claims a person is not 
qualified to vote, whether they are 
voting in person or absentee.
Who Can Make a Challenge?
Only eligible voters (called electors) 
can challenge someone for cause.
Challengers must be:
 ●Over the age of 18
 ●U.S. citizens
 ●Residents of Wisconsin
 ●Eligible to vote in Wisconsin 
(they do not need to be 
registered to vote)
WIS. STAT. § 6.925; WIS. ADMIN. CODE EL §§ 9.02 – 9.06.
Wisconsin Elections 
Commission
WEC Helpdesk: 
608-266-8005
WEC Helpdesk e-mail: 
elections@wi.gov
Cover photo by Michael Newman via Creative 
Commons.
Scan the code to view this 
document online: 
elections.wi.gov/elector-
challenges
What is a proper 
challenge?
All challenges must be “for cause.” 
The term “for cause” has a specific 
legal meaning, and it relates to the 
qualifications of the challenged voter.
Specifically, a voter may only be 
challenged for the following reasons:
 ●The elector is not a U.S. citizen.
 ●They are not 18 or older.
 ●They do not reside in 
the district where they 
are trying to vote.
 ●They have a felony 
conviction and are still 
serving their sentence.
 ●They have been adjudicated 
incompetent by a judge 
with a loss of voting rights.
 ●They already voted in 
the same election.
WIS. STAT. § 6.925; WIS. ADMIN. CODE EL §§ 9.02, 9.04.
What cannot be challenged?
Challenges based on race, ethnicity, 
not speaking English, or other 
unsubstantiated claims are not 
allowed.
An elector cannot challenge:
 ●absentee certificate 
and application
 ●adherence to other 
voting requirements
Absentee Challenges
If someone is voting by absentee 
ballot, their qualifications can still be 
challenged by another elector, using 
the same process as if they were 
voting in person.
WIS. STAT. § 6.93.
Administering Challenges
If a person is challenged as unqualified by an elector, election inspectors 
must follow the procedures in Wis. Admin. Code EL §§ 9.02 – 9.06.
Inspectors should use form EL-104c to administer the challenge.
One of the inspectors may administer the oath or affirmation to the 
challenged and challenging electors and ask the electors appropriate 
questions to test the elector’s qualifications.
WIS. STAT. § 6.92.
Penalties for Misuse
Any elector who abuses the right 
to challenge under Wis. Stat. § 
6.925 may be subject to sanctions 
available to inspectors under Wis. 
Stat. § 7.41(3). The chief inspector 
of a polling place or the municipal 
clerk may order the removal of any 
such person if the person disrupts 
operations or violates Wis. Stats. §§ 
12.03(2) or 12.035. Wis. Stat. § 7.41(3).
WIS. STAT. §§ 7.41(3), WIS. ADMIN. CODE EL § 9.02.
Chapter 14.8: Getting to Know 
the Poll Book
Sec. 14.8.1: Introduction
Poll books contain the names and addresses of registered voters in a reporting 
unit. Wis. Stat. § 6.36(2)(a). The municipal clerk will provide poll books to each 
polling place ahead of Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a). Poll books are 
generated from WisVote, which is Wisconsin’s statewide voter database. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.36(2)(a).
If a municipality uses paper poll books, each ward or combination of wards 
will have two poll books, which poll workers must mark identically on Election 
Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a). Poll workers verify existing voters and record newly-
registered voters in the poll book to ensure only registered voters cast a ballot 
and to maintain accurate information about each registered voter who receives 
a ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2).
See Part 10: “Introduction to Badger Books” for information on electronic 
pollbooks, referred to as e-pollbooks or Badger Books.
Sec. 14.8.2: Anatomy of a Voter Entry
The voter entry in the poll book contains all of the pertinent information 
necessary to check the voter in and permit them to vote. Figure 1 explains each 
part of the voter entry and what it means.
Figure 1: Anatomy of a Voter Entry
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 61
1. Ballot Style Number:  This indicates which ballot style the voter should 
receive based on where they live. This is used to ensure the voter gets the 
correct ballot.
2. Ward Number:  This indicates which ward the voter lives in based on where 
they live. This is used to ensure the voter gets the correct ballot.
3. District Combo Code:  This code is assigned to the voter’s address and 
indicates their municipality, ward, school district, and whether they live in a 
ward split.
4. District Boundary Code:  This code indicates certain voting districts, such 
as their congressional district (CG), school district, technical college (TC), 
assembly district (AD), and aldermanic district (AL).
5. Notes:  If a poll worker needs to add any notes to the poll book for the voter, 
they go here.
6. Voter Registration Number:  This is a unique number assigned to the voter 
by WisVote when their registration is entered into the system.
7. Barcode:  The barcode is associated with the voter’s registration number. 
After the election, it can be scanned by the municipality to record voting 
participation into WisVote.
8. Voter Name:  The name the voter used to register.
9. Voter Address:  The address the voter used to register.
10. Voter Number Line:  Record the voter number in this space once it is 
assigned.
11.  Voter Name:  The name the voter used to register, orientated to face the 
voter so they sign the right line.
12.  Voter Signature Box:  Where the voter signs and where any watermarks 
appear.
13.  Numbered Order in Poll Book:  These numbers refer to how the voter is 
ordered alphabetically in the poll book. They are not the voter number that 
is assigned when issuing a ballot.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 62
Sec. 14.8.3: Watermarks
The poll book also contains different watermarks that appear in or beneath the 
voter signature box. See Chapter 14.9: “Voting Procedure” on page 65 for a 
description of each watermark and what they mean.
Sec. 14.8.4: Parts of the Poll Book
The poll book may consist of up to five different parts, all of which contain 
subsets of voters from the reporting unit.
Regular Poll List
The “regular” poll list is the main part of the poll book and is likely the largest 
part. It contains the names of all the voters who registered to vote by 5:00 p.m. 
on the third Wednesday before Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.28(1)(a). The list is 
alphabetized by last name.
Pre-Printed Supplemental Poll List
The supplemental poll list will appear in a section after the regular poll list. It 
contains the names and addresses of all voters who registered to vote during 
late registration. Wis. Stat. § 6.29. In other words, it will contain the names of 
the voters who registered between the regular registration deadline (third 
Wednesday before Election Day) and 5:00 p.m. the Friday before Election 
Day. For more information about the late registration process, see “Late-
Registration Voters on Supplemental Poll List” on page 65.
Handwritten Supplemental Poll List
This is the part of the poll book poll workers will use after voters have completed 
Election Day Registrations (“EDR”) at the polling place on Election Day. This 
section prints from WisVote with the rest of the poll book as a blank sheet 
where the poll workers fill in new registrants’ names, addresses, and district 
information. The poll workers may also use the Supplemental Poll List form (EL-
107), which contains marked spots to enter this information.
After completing an EDR, poll workers must enter the name, address, and 
Election Day voter number of EDR registrants, as well as the type of proof of 
residence and the name of the issuing identity or organization. See Chapter 
14.6: “Election Day Registration” on page 28 for more information about the 
EDR process.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 63
Confidential Voter List
The confidential voter portion of the poll book is not open to public inspection, 
and no observers should ever be permitted to view it. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.46(2), 6.47.
If there are any confidential voters in the reporting unit, their names and 
confidential voter identification numbers will appear at the back of the poll book 
in a separate section. These voters will present a confidential voter identification 
number and card instead of stating their name and address.
See Chapter 14.10: “Confidential Voters at the Polling Place” on page 84 for 
more information on confidential voter check in.
Protected Voters List
The protected voter portion of the poll book is not open to public 
inspection, and no observers should ever be permitted to view it. Wis. Stat. 
§§ 757.07(2)(a), 19.36(15).
If there are any protected voters in the reporting unit, their names and 
addresses will appear at the back of the poll book in a separate section. 
Protected voters are judicial officers and their qualifying family members who 
have applied to have their personally identifiable information protected. See 
generally, Wis. Stat. § 757.07.
See Chapter 14.10: “Confidential Voters at the Polling Place” on page 84 for 
more information on confidential voter check in.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 64
Chapter 14.9: Voting Procedure
The most important task for poll workers on Election Day is to ensure that 
voters correctly complete all necessary voting procedures so their ballots can 
be counted.
Overview of Voting Process on using a paper poll book:
1. Voter States their Name and Address at the Poll Book
2. Voter Presents Acceptable Photo Identification
3. Poll workers Review Poll Book for Watermarks
4. Voter Signs the Poll Book
5. Poll workers Issue Voter Number and Record in Poll Book
6. Poll workers Confirm and Issue Ballot
7. The Voter Casts their Ballot
Sec. 14.9.1: Step 1: Voter States Their 
Name & Address at the Poll Book
The voter will state their name and address to the poll workers who are 
maintaining the poll books, and the poll workers will confirm that the voter is 
properly registered. The voter, or their assistant, needs to state their “full name 
and address,” which the Commission interprets to mean their first and last 
name, and their street address (including unit number). Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a). 
If a voter refuses to state their name or address (unless they are a confidential 
voter), they cannot be permitted to vote. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(3)(a).
Late-Registration Voters on Supplemental Poll List
Voters who register between the day after the third Wednesday before Election 
Day and by 5:00 p.m. the Friday before Election Day will appear on the pre-
printed supplemental poll list. In the Badger Books, these voters appear in the 
voter search results list with any other voters. Voters who register by this method 
will receive a certificate (EL-133) containing the name and address of the voter. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.29(2)(b).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 65
For more information on the late registration process at the clerk’s office, see 
Sec. 6.2.2: “Methods of Registration.” The voter must present the certificate 
(EL-133) either at the polls for in person voting, or together with their absentee 
ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.29(2)(c).
Best Practice: If the voter’s name appears on the pre-printed 
supplemental poll list, but they do not have their certificate (EL-133), 
the voter should still be permitted to receive and cast their ballot. Since 
the pre-printed supplemental poll list was prepared by the municipal 
clerk, poll workers can safely assume that voters who appear on it 
have been properly registered, even if they forget to bring or include 
their EL-133.
If a voter does not have an EL-133, and their name does not appear on any 
part of the poll book, the poll worker should attempt to contact the clerk and 
determine if they have a record of the voter’s registration. If there is no record 
of the voter’s registration in the clerk’s office, then the voter must complete an 
Election Day Registration (EDR).
Voters Unable to State Their Name or Address
A voter who is unable to state their name and address may have the poll 
worker or an assistant of their choosing state their name and address on 
their behalf. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(8). The assistant should state the voter’s name 
after the poll worker verifies the voter’s proof of identification (see Step 3). Id.
Best Practices: The assistant may read the name and address on 
behalf of the voter directly from the poll book or the voter’s photo ID. 
Voters can also provide their information in writing to their assistant or 
directly to poll workers.
Confidential Voters
Confidential voters do not state their name or address out loud and should not 
be asked to do so. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(6). Instead, confidential voters present their 
identification card or serial number issued under Wis. Stat. § 6.47(3). For more 
information on confidential voters, see Chapter 6.3: “Confidential Voters.”.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 66
Confidential voters appear in their own, dedicated section of the poll book. 
For more information about this section of the poll book, see Chapter 14.10: 
“Confidential Voters at the Polling Place” on page 84.
Protected Voters
Protected voters, such as judicial officers and their qualifying family members, 
are not exempt from needing to state their name and address and must do 
so out loud. Their special legal protections only extend to their information 
being made publicly available by a governmental agency or data broker. See 
generally Wis. Stat. § 757.07.
Protected voters appear in their own, dedicated section of the poll book which 
is not available for public inspection. For more information about this section 
of the poll book, see Chapter 14.10: “Confidential Voters at the Polling Place” 
on page 84.
Sec. 14.9.2: Step 2: Voter Presents 
Acceptable Photo Identification
After the voter, or their assistant, has stated their name and address, poll 
workers can find them in the poll book. If the voter’s name does not appear 
in the poll book, the voter may not be registered, or the voter may need to 
re-register. If the voter’s name does not appear in the poll book, refer to the 
troubleshooting in see Chapter 14.6: “Election Day Registration” on page 28, 
and, if necessary, direct the voter to register or re-register.
Poll workers must then ask the voter to show an acceptable form of proof of 
identification. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a). Proof of photo ID is mandatory for every 
voter, except for confidential voters and certain voters with suspended licenses. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.79(6), (7).
What is an Acceptable Photo ID?
The only photo IDs that can be accepted are the ones specifically listed by 
state law. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(16c), 5.02(6m), Wis. Stat. § 343.165(8). All forms of 
photo ID must contain the name of the individual and their photograph.1 Wis. 
Stat. § 5.02(16c). Common examples of each type of acceptable photo ID are 
included “Appendix 14.9A Acceptable Photo IDs for Voting in Wisconsin”
1 The only exceptions to the photograph requirement are individuals who received special DOT-
licenses without photographs for religious reasons. See Wis. Stat. §§ 343.14(3m), 343.50(4g).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 67
The voter must present an original, hard copy photo ID. See Wis. Stat. 
§ 5.02(6m) (requiring the document that was “issued” to the voter). A scan, 
photocopy, or photograph pulled up on a phone is not acceptable.
If a voter presents a photo ID that is not acceptable, or if a voter does not have 
an acceptable photo ID, they must vote using the Provisional Ballot process. 
See Chapter 14.10: “Confidential Voters at the Polling Place” on page 84 to 
complete that process.
Acceptable Forms of Photo ID Include:
 Ɋ A Wisconsin DOT-issued driver’s license, even if driving privileges are 
revoked or suspended. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(a)1. The driver’s license can be 
expired, but only since the last General Election. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(a).
 ɉ Note: It does not need to contain the voter’s current address.
 ɉ Note: It does not need to be a REAL ID.
 Ɋ A Wisconsin DOT-issued identification card. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(a)2. The 
ID card can be expired, but only since the last General Election. Id.
 ɉ Note: It does not need to contain the voter’s current address.
 ɉ Note: It does not need to be a REAL ID.
 Ɋ A Military ID card issued by a U.S. uniformed service. Wis. Stat. 
§ 5.02(6m)(a)3. The ID card can be expired, but only since the last General 
Election. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(a)3.
 Ɋ A U.S. Passport. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(a)4. The passport can be expired, but 
only since the last General Election. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(a).
 ɉ Note: Some valid U.S. passports look like cards instead of books.
 Ɋ A certificate of naturalization that was issued not earlier than two years 
before Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(b).
 ɉ Example: For the 2024 General Election, the only naturalization 
certificates that are acceptable were those issued since the 2022 
General Election.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 68
 Ɋ An unexpired driving receipt issued by Wisconsin DOT. Wis. Stat. 
§ 5.02(6m)(c). These are issued under Wis. Stat. § 343.11. This must be the 
original receipt issued by the Wisconsin DMV (not any other state). It is 
a government document that can be used to vote for 45 days while the 
voter is waiting on their physical card to be mailed to them.
 Ɋ An unexpired identification card receipt issued by Wisconsin DOT. Wis. 
Stat. § 5.02(6m)(d). These are issued under Wis. Stat. § 343.50. This must be 
the original receipt issued by the Wisconsin DMV (not any other state). It 
is a government document that can be used to vote for 45 days while the 
voter is waiting on their physical card to be mailed to them.
 Ɋ An identification card issued by a federally recognized Native American 
tribe in Wisconsin. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(e). These are also known as Tribal 
ID cards.2
 Ɋ An unexpired identification card issued by an accredited Wisconsin 
university, college, or technical college. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(f). Students 
presenting unexpired student ID cards do not need to show proof of 
enrollment.3 The student ID must contain the date of issuance and 
signature of the holder, and must contain an expiration date indicating 
that the card expires no later than 2 years after the date of issuance. Wis. 
Stat. § 5.02(6m)(f).
2 Tribal ID cards are issued by Native American nations, and any questions regarding eligibility should 
be directed to the nations. The Wisconsin nations that currently issue Tribal ID cards include: the Bad 
River Band of the Lake Superior Tribe of Chippewa Indians, the Forest County Potawatomi Community, 
the Ho-Chunk Nation of Wisconsin, the Lac Courte Oreille Band of Ojibwe, the Lac du Flambeau Band 
of Lake Superior Chippewa, the Menominee Indian Tribe, the Oneida Tribe of Indians, the Red Cliff Band 
of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians, the Sokaogon Chippewa Community, the Stockbridge Munsee 
Community, and the St. Croix Chippewa Indians.
3 On July 29, 2020, the 7th Circuit Court of Appeals issued its decision in the One Wisconsin decision 
and determined that students who present a qualifying unexpired student ID as proof of identification 
are not required to also provide a proof of enrollment document to meet the requirement. However, if 
a student presents a qualifying expired student ID as proof of identification, they must then provide a 
proof of enrollment document to meet the requirement.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 69
 Ɋ An expired identification card issued by an accredited Wisconsin 
university, college, or technical college. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(f). Students 
presenting expired student ID cards must provide proof of enrollment.4 
Proof of enrollment includes, but is not limited to, a fee receipt, class 
schedule, or an enrollment verification form. The student ID must contain 
the date of issuance and signature of the holder, and must contain an 
expiration date indicating that the card expires no later than 2 years after 
the date of issuance. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(f).
 Ɋ An unexpired Veterans Affairs ID card. Wis. Stat. § 5.02(6m)(g).
 Ɋ A temporary identification card receipt issued by Wisconsin DOT through 
the Identification Petition Process (IDPP). Wis. Stat. § 343.165(4). These are 
valid for 60 days.
On Badger Books, the IDs listed above are the only options available to select. 
Each ID still needs to be reviewed according to the criteria for that form of 
identification.
Common Reasons that a Photo ID Cannot be Accepted
Most voters will present a Wisconsin driver’s license or ID card as photo ID, but 
there are some common reasons that photo IDs cannot be accepted.
Out of State License
A valid, unexpired driver’s license or photo ID from another state may be a 
valid photo ID for other purposes, but it is not valid as a photo ID for voting in 
Wisconsin.
Expired Outside of Time Frame
Each type of photo ID has its own restrictions for when it can or cannot 
be expired, so poll workers should refer to this list often, especially when 
encountering unusual forms of photo ID.
Global Entry or NEXUS IDs
Voters may present special types of identification from certain federal travel 
programs, such as a Global Entry ID or NEXUS ID. These may be valid IDs for 
other purposes, but they are not valid forms of Wisconsin ID for voting purposes.
4 See Footnote 3.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 70
Reviewing the Photo ID
Once the voter has presented an acceptable form of photo 
ID, the poll workers review it for three required components, 
each of which is discussed further below:
 Ɋ The voter’s name.
 Ɋ The voter’s photo.
 Ɋ An expiration date.
Poll workers do not need to use the photo ID to verify the voter’s address. A 
photo ID containing an address that does not match the voter’s address in the 
poll book is still an acceptable photo ID.
Note: Poll workers can ask a voter to remove their ID from their wallet if 
they cannot see the relevant information.
The Voter’s Name
The poll workers verify that the name on the photo ID conforms to the voter’s 
name in the poll book. The name on the ID may be a variation of the name 
on the poll list, and they are acceptable as long as they are reasonable (i.e. 
“Robert” vs. “Bob” or “Margaret” vs. “Peggy”). Variations of initials or upper- 
or lower-case letters are also permitted, as are the presence or absence of 
hyphens or apostrophes in last names.
If poll workers do not believe that the name on the photo ID conforms to the poll 
book, the voter may not be permitted to vote but may vote a Provisional Ballot. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.79(3)(b). See Chapter 14.13: “Provisional Voting” on page 95.
The Voter’s Photo
All acceptable forms of photo ID must contain the voter’s photo, except for 
specific Wisconsin driver’s licenses or ID cards where the voter has been 
granted a religious exception. The poll workers verify that any photo reasonably 
resembles the voter. Poll workers should take into account that changes in a 
voter’s physical appearance do not invalidate the photo ID.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 71
Best Practice: Whether a photo ID reasonably resembles the voter 
is a subjective, fact-specific decision made by poll workers, but it 
is something that should follow common sense. The photo ID does 
not need to exactly resemble the voter, but variations in hair color or 
length, facial hair, age, weight or other surface-level changes are all 
reasonable.
Note Regarding Face Coverings: Most voters will not have to remove 
their face covering to verify they reasonably resemble the photo on 
their ID. The requirement is that the photo on the ID must “reasonably 
resemble” the voter, and Commission staff believe this verification can 
be done in most instances without face covering removal. There may 
be rare instances where a poll worker may request the brief removal 
or rearrangement of the face covering during this process, but these 
instances would be the exception.
If poll workers do not believe that the photo on the ID reasonably resembles the 
voter, the voter may not be permitted to vote but may vote a Provisional Ballot. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.79(3)(b). See Chapter 14.13: “Provisional Voting” on page 95.
Best Practice: Before issuing a provisional ballot, poll workers should 
confer with the Chief Inspector to ensure there is consensus that the 
photo ID does not reasonably resemble the voter.
The Photo ID’s Expiration Date
The poll workers will verify that the photo ID is unexpired or, if expired, it meets 
one of the expiration date specifications listed in the list of acceptable photo 
IDs above. If the photo ID does not meet the expiration date requirements, it is 
not a valid photo ID. If the voter cannot produce another acceptable photo ID, 
they can vote by Provisional Ballot. See Chapter 14.13: “Provisional Voting” on 
page 95.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 72
Sec. 14.9.3: Step 3: Poll Workers 
Review Poll Book for Watermarks
Before the voter signs the poll book, the poll workers should review the voter’s 
information line for any watermarks or notations by the municipal clerk. 
Watermarks are added to the voter signature box on the paper poll book and 
in their own column to the left of the voter’s name in Badger Books. Watermarks 
convey important information regarding participation and voter eligibility. Some 
clerks utilize markings, highlighter, or other symbols in lieu of watermarks. If a 
poll worker sees something notated or indicated in the poll book and isn’t sure 
what it means, they must stop and confer with the Chief Inspector.
Absentee Issued & Absentee Received
These watermarks appear in the poll book to indicate that the voter has either 
been issued or returned their absentee ballot. Both require action by the poll 
worker prior to the voter signing the poll book and being issued a ballot. Any 
voter who mails or personally delivers their absentee ballot to the municipal 
clerk is not permitted to vote in person on Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.86(6). 
These watermarks ensure that this statute is followed.
Absentee Issued
Figure 2: This watermark indicates that an absentee 
ballot was issued to the voter.
If this watermark appears, poll workers should ask the following question of the 
voter:
Q Have you mailed or personally delivered your absentee ballot to the clerk’s 
office?
A If YES – Voter cannot vote in person. They have already voted. This is true 
even if the voter says they don’t think their ballot is going to make it to their 
clerk on time.
A If NO – Voter can still vote in person. Any voter can choose not to return 
their absentee ballot and still vote in person on Election Day.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 73
Note: Some municipalities maintain an Absentee Ballot Log (EL-124) that 
poll workers can use to determine if an absentee ballot has been returned. 
Poll workers can refer to this resource, but should still ask the voter the 
question above even if their name does not appear on the EL-124.
Absentee Returned
If this watermark appears in the poll book, it means the municipal clerk has 
recorded that the voter has already returned their absentee ballot. The voter 
cannot vote in person, should not sign the poll book, and cannot be issued a 
ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.86(6).
Figure 3: Badger Books will not allow a voter with this notation 
to be checked in through the in-person process.
 
Figure 4: Absentee Returned watermark as it appears in the poll book
Note: A voter still may not vote at the polling place even if the absentee 
ballot they returned has issues or is rejected. Wis. Stat. § 6.86(6). Instead, 
the municipal clerk has the option to return the absentee ballot to the 
voter for a correction, if time permits, or contact the voter and allow 
them to cure any defect in person. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9). See Chapter 14.18: 
“Correcting Defective Certificate Envelopes” on page 161.
Have You Moved?
This one does not appear as a watermark in the signature box, but it does 
appear in words printed beneath the signature box, so it’s important to keep a 
careful eye out for it before the voter signs the poll book.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 74
Figure 5: Have You Moved watermark as it appears in the poll book
This watermark appears whenever there is information in a voter’s record 
that indicates that they may have moved since the last time they voted.5 The 
information about a possible move comes from secure reports received by the 
Commission from the Electronic Registration Information Center (“ERIC”).6 For 
more information about ERIC and voter list maintenance, see Chapter 3.4: “ERIC 
Movers” on page 6.
If a poll worker sees these words beneath the voter’s signature box, they should 
ask the voter if the voter has moved to a new address.
 Ɋ If the voter has NOT moved, they can sign the poll book and continue 
with the voting process. This will later indicate to the clerk that the voter’s 
record should be updated from Active-Movers to Active-Registered once 
participation from the election has been entered.
 Ɋ If the voter says they HAVE moved, they should be given the option to re-
register at their new address.
Confidential
This watermark indicates that the voter is a confidential voter under Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.47. Confidential voters should appear in their own designated section 
of the poll book. The purpose of this watermark is to remind poll workers 
that confidential voters do not need to state their name or address or show 
proof of ID. See Step 1 and Step 3 of this Chapter. For more information on 
confidential voters, see Chapter 14.10: “Confidential Voters at the Polling 
Place” on page 84.
5 The Commission discussed and approved the procedures for the use of this watermark, among 
other things related to the ERIC movers data, at public meetings on September 24, 2019, December 2, 
2019, January 11, 2021, March 2, 2021, and June 10, 2021.
6 For a detailed history of the ERIC movers data and how the Commission has handled it over 
the years, see: Wisconsin Mails Postcards to Voters Who May Have Moved | Wisconsin Elections 
Commission .
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 75
Figure 6: Confidential watermark as it appears in the poll book
Military Absentee/Perm Overseas Absentee
These watermarks indicate that the voter is a military or overseas voter. They are 
functionally the same as the Absentee Issued / Absentee Returned watermarks 
but give poll workers extra information about the voter in case it is useful. These 
watermarks don’t require any specific, additional action other than what 
should normally be done for Absentee Issued / Absentee Returned.
Figure 7: Overseas Absentee Returned Watermark
Could also say “Absentee Issued”
Figure 8:  Military Absentee Returned Watermark
Could also say “Absentee Issued”
Figure 9:  Permanently Overseas Watermark
This one by itself means the voter is registered as permanent overseas but does not 
have an absentee request on file. These voters cannot vote at the polling place.
Military Must Rgstr
If the “MILITARY MUST RGSTR” watermark appears by the voter’s name, the 
voter needs to be directed to register to vote just like a regular voter, including 
providing proof of residence. This watermark appears when a voter was 
previously exempt from registration as a military voter so they could vote 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 76
absentee. Military voters do not need to register to vote, or provide proof of 
residence, when voting by absentee ballot. When military voters vote at their 
polling places, they are no longer exempt from voter registration, and they 
must register to vote if they want to be issued a ballot at the polling place. 
This watermark indicates that the voter should be directed to register to vote 
before casting their ballot in person at the polls. On Badger Books, the only 
option will be to complete an Election Day Registration (EDR).
Figure 10:  Poll Book Military Watermark
Figure 11:  Badger Book Military Watermark
See Chapter 7.4: “Types of Absentee Voters: UOCAVA” for more information 
about military voter exemptions and voting.
Exempt
There is a checkbox in the voter record to indicate that the voter is unable 
to sign the poll book. When that box is checked, it generates the “Exempt” 
watermark. If this watermark is present, poll workers should still confirm with 
the voter that they are unable to sign the poll book. The watermark would not 
stop poll workers from challenging the voter if they don’t agree that the voter is 
unable to sign.
See Sec. 14.9.4: “Step 4: Voter Signs the Poll Book” on page 79 for more 
details on voter ability to sign the poll book.
Figure 12: Watermark for voter exempt from signing the poll book
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 77
Multiple Watermarks
Sometimes, poll workers may see more than one watermark appear in the 
signature box depending on the voter’s circumstances. A few examples and 
notations appear below.
Figure 13:  Watermark for unregistered Military Voter 
who has returned an absentee ballot
Effect: Voter cannot vote at the polls because they 
already returned their absentee ballot
Figure 14: Watermark for permanently overseas voter 
who has returned an absentee ballot
Effect: Voter cannot vote at the polls because they already 
returned their absentee ballot and because permanent overseas 
voters cannot vote at the polling place on Election Day
Figure 15:  Watermark for voter exempt from signing the 
poll book who has returned an absentee ballot
Effect: Voter was issued an absentee ballot and may 
also be exempt from signing poll book
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 78
Sec. 14.9.4: Step 4: Voter 
Signs the Poll Book
After the voter has stated their name, presented their photo ID, and the poll 
workers have confirmed there are no actionable watermarks in the poll book, 
the next step is for the voter to sign the poll book. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a). This is 
mandatory — if the voter refuses to sign or writes something other than their 
name (like profanity or someone else’s name), they cannot be issued a ballot. Id.
The voter only needs to sign one copy of the poll book. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(6). Poll 
workers should ensure that all voters are signing the same copy of the poll book, 
which the Commission recommends be the copy that is later provided to the 
county clerk.
If the municipality uses Badger Books, the e-poll book is the copy the voters will 
sign. The paper copy is a backup to be used only in case of power failure.
Make sure the voter signs the correct line in the poll book. If the voter signs on 
the wrong line, document the error on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104). Any 
voter whose line was incorrectly signed by another voter will instead sign the 
supplemental poll list.
Best Practice: Signature guides are helpful tools that are available 
through the Commission. Use the Accessibility Supply Order Form 
(EL-502) to order 8 in. x 5 in. or credit card-sized signature guides.
Voter Assistance
If a voter is unable to sign their name due to a disability, and someone else 
signed their voter registration form to assist them, poll workers should write the 
word “Exempt” in the voter’s signature line. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(6), 6.36(2)(a). If the 
voter was already registered, but claims to be unable to sign on Election Day 
due to a physical disability, and both poll workers working the poll book concur, 
the poll workers shall enter the words “Exempt by order of inspectors” on the 
signature line or select the “Physically Unable to Sign” button to add the note in 
the signature field on Badger Books. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(am).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 79
If both poll workers do not agree to provide the exemption, the voter should be 
provided a ballot, and then that ballot should be challenged by the objecting 
poll workers under Wis. Stat. § 6.92, 6.79(2)(am). The challenged voter may 
then provide evidence of their disability to the local board of canvassers, who 
convene immediately after polls close. Wis. Stat. 6.79(2)(am). For more on 
challenging voters, see Chapter 14.7: “Challenging Electors on Election Day” on 
page 43.
Legibility of the Signature
The voter’s signature does not need to be legible. See Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a) 
(requiring only a signature, not a legible one). The voter signs the poll book 
by making their “mark,” which may or may not be legible. Poll workers are not 
required to compare the voter’s signature to the signature on the voter’s proof 
of ID, and denying the voter a ballot on that ground is improper.
Sec. 14.9.5: Step 5: Poll Workers Issue 
Voter Number & Record in Poll Book
After the voter has signed the poll book, poll workers issue them the next 
available voter number. A voter number is assigned to every voter beginning 
with the number “1.” Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(b). The voter number is recorded 
simultaneously by poll workers on both copies of the poll book. This happens 
automatically in Badger Books. The voter is then given their voter number on a 
small slip of paper. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(e).
Sec. 14.9.6: Step 6: Poll Workers 
Confirm & Issue Ballot
Once the voter’s voter number has been recorded in both poll books, the voter 
exchanges the slip with their voter number for their ballot.
Best Practice: Poll workers should first confirm that the voter is 
receiving the correct ballot style, particularly in polling places where 
voters from multiple wards or different school districts are voting.
Before the ballot is issued to the voter, it should be initialed by two different poll 
workers. Wis. Stat. § 6.80(2)(d). If poll workers forget, and the voter brings the 
ballot back, the poll workers must initial it. Id.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 80
Best Practice: Two initials in the endorsement section of a ballot makes 
the ballot a “live” ballot. No matter how busy the polling place is, or 
how prepared or efficient poll workers want to be, they should never 
pre-initial ballots in advance before issuing them to individual voters. 
Unvoted ballots should always be stored where voters cannot access 
them.
Poll workers should be sure to ask the voter if they have any questions about 
how to correctly cast their ballot. Poll workers should be prepared to answer 
voter questions on how to use the voting equipment, how to mark a ballot, or 
how to place it in the ballot box. If the voter is casting their ballot using direct 
recording electronic (DRE) equipment or if they are using a ballot marking 
device, poll workers should remind them to check the voter-verified paper trail 
before the voter casts their ballot. Finally, poll workers should direct the voter 
to the voting area, and they should make sure they go straight there since the 
voter now possesses a live ballot.
Sec. 14.9.7: Step 7: The Voter 
Casts Their Ballot
The voter must proceed directly to the voting booth to cast their ballot. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.80(2)(a). Ballots cannot leave the poll site. Only one person at a 
time is permitted in each voting both, except children accompanying their 
parents or guardians, or a voter is receiving assistance due to a disability. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.80(1) .
If a voter, by accident or mistake, spoils or erroneously prepares their ballot, they 
may receive another by returning the defective ballot to the poll workers. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.80(2)(c). A voter may be issued up to three ballots total if they make 
mistakes. Wis. Stat. § 6.80(2)(c). A voter spoiling a ballot at the polling place 
before voting it is not affected by the ballot spoliation litigation and guidance 
discussed in Ballot Spoiling Guidance.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 81
Best Practice: Election officials spoil the discarded ballot by tearing it 
to make it unusable, but the pieces should be retained in an envelope 
to be returned to the municipal clerk with other election materials, 
and the request documented on the EL-104 Inspector’s Statement. A 
notation of 2nd or 3rd ballot should be made on the EL-104 (NOT on the 
ballot), as well as in the poll book by the voter’s name.
Once the voter has made all of their selections, the voter places their voted 
ballot into the tabulator or ballot box, thus completing the voting process. 
The voter should then leave the polling place unless they intend to stay as 
an observer.
For more information on observers, see Chapter 14.5: “Election Observers.”
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 82

Chapter 14.10: Confidential 
Voters at the Polling Place
Confidential voters are the victims of domestic abuse, sexual assault, stalking, 
or other qualifying individuals. Wis. Stat. § 6.47(2). Confidential voters apply for 
this status directly with their municipal clerk. It entitles them to special voting 
procedures to ensure that their information remains confidential. For more 
information about how confidential voters request this status with the municipal 
clerk, see Chapter 6.3: “Confidential Voters.”
Sec. 14.10.1:  Election Day Registration 
(EDR) for Confidential Voters
Confidential voters cannot register to vote in person on election day at 
their polling places. State law requires them to “register at the office of the 
municipal clerk of the municipality” where they reside. Wis. Stat. § 6.28(1)(b). If 
a confidential voter appears at the polling place and needs to register to vote, 
they should be directed to the clerk’s office to complete their registration before 
returning to the polling place to vote.
If a voter wants to obtain a confidential voter listing on Election Day, they should 
be immediately directed to the municipal clerk’s office. If the voter has all 
required documents and information to do so, they can obtain a confidential 
listing and then register to vote.
Best Practice: If a voter applies for and receives confidential voter 
status on Election Day, the municipal clerk should coordinate with 
the Chief Inspector of the voter’s polling place to ensure that the 
confidential voter gets properly checked into the poll book so their 
participation can be recorded.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 84
Sec. 14.10.2:  Voting Procedure 
for Confidential Voters
If the municipality has confidential voters, they will be indicated in the separate 
“Confidential” section of the pre-printed poll book. This section appears at the 
back of the poll book. See Chapter 14.8: “Getting to Know the Poll Book” on 
page 61.
Note: This section of the poll book is NOT open to public inspection or 
open records laws.
When checking in to vote, a confidential voter may either:
 Ɋ Present his or her Identification Card for Protected Individual (EL-148),1 or
 Ɋ Announce his or her name and confidential voter identification number
A confidential voter will do either of these options but never needs to 
state their address out loud or provide proof of identification. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.79(6). The Identification Card for Protected Individual (EL-148) contains a 
unique identification serial number assigned to him or her by the municipal 
clerk. Id.. Confidential voters must still sign the poll list if they vote at the polls on 
Election Day. See Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a).
Procedure for Voting at the Polling Place
1. The voter presents his or her Identification Card for Protected Individual 
(EL-148) or announces his or her name and confidential voter identification 
number INSTEAD of announcing his or her name and address at the 
polling place and providing proof of identification. If the confidential voter 
does not have their EL-148 and does not know their unique identification 
serial number, they will need to find and provide that information before 
they can be permitted to vote. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(6). This may be best 
achieved by sending the voter to the municipal clerk, who may be able to 
issue them a duplicate card under Wis. Stat. § 6.47(3).
2. The voter signs the poll book.
1 Although the title of this card is Identification Card for Protected Individual, it can only be used for 
confidential voters.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 85
3. The voter is assigned a regular voter number.
4. The voter is then issued a ballot and allowed to vote like any other voter.
5. The address of the protected individual is not disclosed on the confidential 
portion of the poll book.
Note: The confidential portion of the poll book is NOT open to public 
inspection. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.87(6m), 6.47(2).
Sec. 14.10.3: Protected Voters
Effective April 1, 2025, judicial officers or members of their immediate family 
may request that certain information be protected from public disclosure and 
removed from public display. Wis. Stat. § 757.07. The Commission refers to this 
protection as “data privacy protection.” For more information about this process, 
see Chapter 6.3: “Confidential Voters.”
Protected voters are not confidential voters. They will appear in their own, 
separate section of the poll book, which cannot be made public. Wis. Stat. 
§ 19.36(15). See Chapter 14.8: “Getting to Know the Poll Book” on page 61. The 
protected voter portion of the poll book should be as equally protected from 
observation as the confidential voter portion.
Procedure for Voting at Polling Place
Although the judicial privacy law broadly prevents government agencies 
from publicly sharing the protected information of judicial officers and their 
qualifying family members who seek that protection, the law does not address 
other areas of law where providing an address is required. Unlike confidential 
voters, judicial officers do not receive the same polling place protections.
Voting in Person at the Polls
Protected voters, meaning judicial officers and their qualifying family members, 
are not exempt from the requirement to state their name and address at the 
poll book when voting in person. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a). If a protected voter 
refuses to state their name at the poll book (assuming they are not also a 
confidential voter), they cannot be issued a ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(3)(a).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 86
Protected voters are also not exempt from Wisconsin’s photo identification 
requirement. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a). If a protected voter refuses to show their 
photo ID, they cannot be issued a ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(3)(b). Instead, they 
may cast a provisional ballot, if they are qualified to do so, and if showing their 
ID to the municipal clerk as part of that process would make them feel more 
comfortable than showing it at the polling place. Id.
Voting by Absentee Ballot
Protected voters are also not exempt from providing their residential address on 
their absentee certificate envelope, either as the voter or as a witness. If a voter 
has attempted to write something like “Exempt – Judicial Privacy” or “Exempt – 
757.07,” the certificate envelope is likely insufficient. See Sec. 14.15.12: “Absentee 
Certificate Envelopes”
If the municipal clerk has an approved data privacy request on file for the 
protected voter, no part of the voter’s address on the absentee certificate 
envelope may be made public, nor should observers be permitted to view 
the voter’s address. Wis. Stat. § 19.36(15). Just having protected voter status 
in WisVote is not enough — the municipal clerk of the jurisdiction must have 
separately received a data privacy request for this to apply.
Poll workers are only required to read the names of absentee voters, not 
addresses. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a). Accordingly, it is possible to avoid the 
difficulties raised by reading protected addresses by announcing the names of 
absentee voters.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 87
Chapter 14.11: Voter 
Assistance on Election Day
Voters with disabilities have the right to receive assistance in completing the 
following tasks:
Sec. 14.11.1: Signing a Voter 
Registration Application
Q Who has the right to this assistance?
A Voters who are unable to sign a form due to a disability. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.33(2)(a).
Q Who can provide assistance?
A Any other voter authorized by the voter. Id.
Q How is assistance provided by another voter?
1. The assistant fills in the fields on the form at the direction of the voter.
2. The assistant signs the voter’s name in Section 9.
3. The assistant signs their own name in Section 10 and provides their address.
4. When an assistant signs the voter’s Registration Application by reason 
of the voter’s disability, the voter is exempt from signing the poll book 
when voting in person, and the poll workers should write “exempt” on the 
signature line. Id.
Sec. 14.11.2:  Stating Name & 
Address at the Poll Book
Q Who has the right to this assistance?
A Voters who are unable to state their name and address. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(8).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 88
Q Who can provide assistance?
A The poll worker at the poll book or another person the voter chooses. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.79(8).
Q How is assistance provided?
1. The voter communicates to the poll worker that they are unable to state 
their name and address.
2. The poll worker verifies the voter’s photo ID. Id.
3. The poll worker or an assistant states the voter’s name and address. Id.
4. The voter signs the poll book if able. Wis. Stat. § 6.79(2)(a).
Best Practice: The Commission has the optional form State Your Name 
and Address Card (EL-503) that a voter can use to facilitate receiving 
assistance. This card is not required to access this type of assistance. It 
is a “best practice” to have copies of this card available to voters at the 
poll book table.
Sec. 14.11.3:  Signing the Poll Book
Voters who completed an Election Day Registration (EDR), or who previously 
completed a registration, and had an assistant sign their name on the 
registration form are exempt from signing the poll book, and the poll workers 
must write “exempt” on the voter’s signature line. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.36(2)(a), 
6.79(2)(a).
If the voter previously signed their registration form, or was exempt from 
registration, and is unable, due to physical disability, to sign the poll book, the 
poll workers must waive the signature requirement and enter “exempt by order 
of inspectors” on the voter’s signature line if the poll workers determine that 
the voter is unable to enter their signature due to physical disability. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.79(2)(am). If both officials do not waive the signature requirement, the official 
or officials must challenge the voter’s ballot for cause under Wis. Stat. §§ 6.92(1), 
6.79(2)(am). In the challenge, the voter may provide evidence of their physical 
disability to the board of canvassers. Id.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 89
Note: A voter does not need to provide evidence in order to have the 
signature requirement waived. Poll workers should only proceed with 
challenging a voter’s ballot if the poll worker has cause to believe that 
a voter is fraudulently attempting to attain a waiver of the signature 
requirement. Unless a poll worker would swear under oath that they 
know a voter is able to sign the poll book and can articulate the 
specific reasons why the poll worker knows that to be the case, a voter 
should not be challenged.
Best Practice: If a voter explains that they cannot sign the poll book 
due to a disability the poll workers can ask if the voter had an assistant 
sign their voter registration form. If the voter says yes, write “exempt” 
on the voter’s signature line in the poll book, and if the voter says “no” 
or “I don’t know” write “exempt by order of inspectors” on the voter’s 
signature line. No additional information of any kind needs to be 
provided by the voter.
Sec. 14.11.4:  Marking a Ballot
Q Who has the right to this assistance?
A Voters who are unable to or have difficulty reading, writing, or understanding 
English and voters who are unable to mark a ballot or depress a button or 
lever on a voting machine due to disability. Wis. Stat. § 6.82(2)(a).
Q Who can provide assistance?
A Anyone of the voter’s choice who is not the voter’s employer or an agent 
of that employer, or an officer or agent of a labor organization which 
represents the voter. Id.
Q How is assistance provided?
1. The voter explains to the poll workers that they need assistance to mark 
their ballot. Id.
2. The poll workers, if necessary, explain who can provide assistance and 
how it may be given. Id.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 90
3. The poll workers enter the word “assisted” in the poll book after the name 
of the voter. Wis. Stat. § 6.82(2)(d).
4. Poll workers record the full name and address of the assistant in the poll 
book. Id.
Best practice: Enter the assistant’s name and address in the back of 
the poll book so that a person requesting ballot images and poll book 
images cannot use this information to identify a voter’s ballot. Please 
be aware that assistant information in the poll book may need to be 
redacted in the case of a public records request to prevent a ballot 
from being traced back to a voter. This may happen when there is only 
one voter who receives assistance.
5. The assistant goes into the voting booth with the voter. Wis. Stat. § 6.82(2)(b).
6. The assistant reads the names of all candidates on the ballot for each 
office and asks, “for which one do you vote?” Id.
7. The assistant marks the ballot or depresses the lever or button according 
to the voter’s expressed preference. Id.
8. The assistant cannot request, suggest, or seek to persuade a voter to vote 
for or against any candidate, party, or question. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.82(4).
9. The assistant cannot intentionally misinform the voter about what is 
printed on the ballot, intentionally fail to cast the ballot in accordance with 
the voter’s instructions, or disclose to any 3rd person how the voter voted. 
Wis. Stat. § 12.13(3)(j). To do any of these things may be punishable as a 
Class I felony. Wis. Stat. § 12.60(1)(a).
10. The assistant signs the Certification of Voter Assistance on the ballot. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.82(2)(a).
11. If voting equipment is used, the assistant certifies on the registration list. Id.
Best Practice: At least one poll worker should be trained and ready 
to provide assistance marking a ballot to a voter who requests an 
assistant. Poll workers should have signature guides and magnifiers 
available for voters who have difficulty seeing or writing in a small field.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 91
Chapter 14.12: Curbside Voting
A voter who, as a result of disability, is unable to enter the polling place 
may elect to receive a ballot at the entrance of the polling place. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.82(1)(a). The voter may receive assistance in marking the ballot, if required, 
from a poll worker, or from any other person of the voter’s choice except the 
voter’s employer, an agent of the employer, or an officer or agent of the voter’s 
labor organization representing the voter. Id. An unregistered voter may also 
register to vote curbside. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.55, 6.82(1)(a), 7.15(14).
Best Practice: Keep a waterproof curbside voting go-bag, so poll 
workers have everything they need when a curbside voter arrives.
 Ɋ Security sleeve
 Ɋ Clipboard
 Ɋ Pens
 Ɋ Lighted magnifying glass/flashlight
The poll workers may learn that a voter is at the curbside voting area in a variety 
of ways. An assistant may approach the poll workers in the polling place, a 
greeter stationed within sight of the curbside area may see that a voter wishes 
to vote curbside, or the voter or assistant may call the number or ring the 
doorbell as explained by the sign placed in the curbside area.
Once the poll workers become aware that a voter wishes to 
vote curbside, they should use the following procedure:
1. The poll workers announce in the polling place that a voter has requested 
a curbside ballot, and two poll workers are going to the polling place 
entrance or the vehicle to view the voter’s proof of identification.
a. If having two poll workers leave the polling area would result in fewer 
than three poll workers in the polling area, voting must stop until the 
poll workers return.
2. Two poll workers go to the vehicle to ask the voter for acceptable proof of 
identification. Wis. Stat. § 6.82(1)(a). The assistant, if necessary, may also 
present the voter’s proof of identification to the poll workers. Id.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 92
3. If the voter does not have an acceptable photo ID, the voter may still 
vote a provisional ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.97. If the proof of residence (POR) 
document is missing, the voter must obtain a POR document and may not 
receive a provisional ballot.
4. The poll workers verify that the photograph appearing on the ID 
reasonably resembles the voter. Wis. Stat. § 6.82(1)(a).
5. The poll workers return to the polling area with the voter’s ID and verify 
that the name on the ID conforms to the voter’s name in the poll book. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.82(1)(a).
Note: If the voter is not in the poll book, they may not be registered to 
vote or may be at the wrong polling place, and the poll workers should 
advise the voter on how to proceed. Wis. Stat. § 6.55(3)(a). For more 
information on election day registrations, see Chapter 14.6: “Election 
Day Registration” on page 28.
6. The poll workers then announce that they are issuing a ballot to the voter, 
fulfilling the requirement to state the voter’s name and address. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.79(2)(a), 6.79(8).
7. Two poll workers initial the ballot. Wis. Stat. § 7.37(4). A voter number is 
issued to the voter and recorded in the poll book. Wis. Stat. § 7.37(4).
8. Two poll workers then deliver the ballot to the curbside voter. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.82(1)(a). The Commission recommends that the ballots be delivered in 
a security sleeve.
9. The curbside voter marks the ballot or has the assistant mark the ballot 
and then returns the ballot to the poll workers. (If an assistant marks the 
ballot. See Chapter 14.11: “Voter Assistance on Election Day” on page 
88. Wis. Stat. § 6.82(1)(a). The Commission recommends that the voted 
ballot be placed in the security sleeve by the voter or the voter’s assistant. 
If there is no security sleeve, the ballot must be folded so that the voter’s 
choices are not disclosed. Id.
10. The poll workers return to the voting area and announce: “I have a ballot 
offered by (voter’s name), an elector who, as a result of disability, is unable 
to enter the polling place without assistance. Does anyone object to the 
reception of this ballot?”. Wis. Stat. § 6.82(1)(b).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 93
a. If an objection is made by a qualified voter for cause, follow the 
challenge procedures detailed in Chapter 14.7 on page 43. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.82(1)(a).
b. If no objection is made or after any challenge is resolved, the poll workers 
write the voter’s name in the signature box in the poll book and deposit 
the ballot in the appropriate ballot box or tabulating equipment. Id. The 
poll workers must write “ballot received at poll entrance” after the voter’s 
name in the poll book. Id.
c. This incident should be recorded on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 94
Chapter 14.13: Provisional 
Voting
Sec. 14.13.1: Introduction
In Wisconsin, provisional voting is ONLY used in two situations. If either of the two 
situations listed below occur, the poll workers must inform the voter of their ability 
to cast a provisional ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.97(1) and Wis. Admin. Code § EL 3.04(2).
No Wisconsin Driver License or State ID Number
Individuals who register to vote at the polling place on Election Day who have 
been issued a current and valid Wisconsin Driver License or Wisconsin DOT-
issued Identification Card must provide that number on the registration form. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 3.04(1) . If the individual is unwilling or unable to provide 
the license or state identification card number, and the lack of that number 
is the only missing item of information, the individual may vote provisionally. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 3.04(2) .
Note: If a license has expired, or has been suspended or revoked, it is 
not a current and valid license and does not need to be provided. Wis. 
Admin. Code § EL 3.04(1). In this circumstance, the voter must provide 
the last four digits of the applicant’s social security number if they have 
been issued one, and, if not, may acknowledge this fact and register 
without providing such a number. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 3.04(3). A voter 
may not vote provisionally if the voter does not provide the last four 
digits of their social security number. See Wis. Admin. Code § EL 3.04.
No Proof of Identification
If an individual is unable or unwilling to provide an acceptable form of proof 
of identification, he or she may vote provisionally. Wis. Stat. § 6.97(1). See 
“Appendix 14.9A Acceptable Photo IDs for Voting in Wisconsin” on page 83 
or a list of acceptable forms of proof of identification.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 95
Note: If the poll workers do not believe that the name of the voter 
conforms to the name shown on the proof of identification, or if the 
voter does not reasonably resemble the photograph on the proof of 
identification, the poll workers should not offer a provisional ballot if 
the voter still intends to vote using that ID. Instead, the poll workers 
should challenge the voter’s ballot see Chapter 14.7: “Challenging 
Electors on Election Day” on page 43.
Exceptions
There is NO other situation in which provisional voting should be used.
Provisional ballots are NOT given when:
 Ɋ A voter is at the wrong polling place. See Wis. Stat. § 6.55(3)(a). If a voter 
appears at the wrong polling place, he or she will be directed to the 
proper location. Id.
 Ɋ A person is attempting to register in-person at the polling place and 
cannot provide the required proof of residence.1
 Ɋ A voter is challenged.
Sec. 14.13.2: Procedure
Once it is determined a voter will vote provisionally, the following procedures 
are required:
1. Every provisional voter must complete the affirmation provided on a 
Provisional Ballot Certificate Envelope (EL-123).
1 Though Wis. Stat. §§ 6.79(2)(d) and 6.97 refer to offering a provisional ballot to a voter regarding 
proof of residency, that process was specific to voters who were already registered to vote but who 
had not yet provided a proof of residency document. These voters were required to show POR before 
voting, and if they did not, were allowed to vote a provisional ballot. That process ended in 2011 with 
the passage of Act 23, and because any registered voter who had not already provided POR was 
now required to provide it before voting, all such voters either provided POR to vote or were eventually 
changed to inactive on the registration list due to failing to vote for four years. As of today, there are 
no voters who are required to provide POR to vote, and there is no statute or rule allowing a person 
a provisional ballot if they do not provide a POR document when registering to vote. See Wis. Stat. § 
6.55(2)(b) and Wis. Admin. Code § EL 3.04.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 96
2. The provisional voter completes the certificate envelope in the presence of 
at least one poll worker by providing:
 ɉ Full name
 ɉ Complete address, including municipality and county
 ɉ Indication that they are 18 or older, are a U.S. citizen, have resided at the 
address provided for at least 28 consecutive days prior to the election 
and do not intend to move, and are not currently serving a sentence for
