a felony conviction
 ɉ Signature and date
3. The poll worker completes the certificate envelope by:
 ɉ Signing and dating the certificate envelope.
 ɉ Indicating the type of required information, either “Driver License 
or State Identification Card Number” or “Proof of Identification,” by 
checking the appropriate box or boxes on the certificate envelope.
4. The poll worker issues a provisional voter number (PV#). A voter number is 
NOT issued to the provisional voter at this time, and the provisional voter 
does NOT sign the poll list.
 ɉ The PV# is issued sequentially, starting with “1.”
 ɉ The PV# is recorded in six places:
i. On the back of the ballot
ii. On the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104)
iii. On the Provisional Ballot Certificate Envelope (EL-123)
iv. On the Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123r)
v. On the poll list or supplemental poll list
vi. On the Provisional Voting Information Sheet for the provisional 
voter (EL-123is)
5. The provisional voter votes the provisional ballot, seals the voted ballot in 
the Provisional Ballot Certificate Envelope (EL-123), and returns the sealed 
envelope to the poll worker
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 97
6. The sealed certificate envelope (EL-123) is placed inside the Inspectors’ 
Certificate for Provisional Ballots Envelope (EL-108).
7. The poll worker record the name of the provisional voter, the PV#, and the 
reason for the provisional ballot on the Provisional Ballot Reporting Form 
(EL-123r) and on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104).
 ɉ The Inspectors’ Certificate of Provisional Ballots Envelope (EL-108) must 
be kept secure throughout Election Day.
8. When the polling place closes, the Inspectors’ Certificate of Provisional 
Ballots Envelope (EL-108) must be secured in a separate ballot bag with 
a tamper-evident serialized numbered seal. The serial number shall 
be recorded on the signed ballot container certification attached to 
the bag and on the Inspectors’ Statement. The bag should be marked 
“Provisional Ballots.”
9. Poll workers must provide the provisional voter with the Provisional Voting 
Information Sheet (EL-123is) and should check the applicable reason that 
the provisional ballot was issued.
10. The municipal clerk must be notified of the number of provisional 
ballots issued on Election Day, and the name, address, and PV# of each 
provisional voter who received a provisional ballot. This is accomplished 
by providing the Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123r) to the 
municipal clerk. Wis. Stat. § 6.97(1).
Sec. 14.13.3: Curing a Provisional 
Ballot on Election Day
A voter who was issued a provisional ballot may return to the polling place 
before 8:00 p.m. to provide the missing documentation to the poll workers. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.97(3)(a). The poll workers must review the documentation to 
determine if it is valid. Id.
Best Practice: If the provided documentation is not valid, the poll 
workers should inform the voter and record the incident on the 
Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104) including the type of document 
presented and why it was not acceptable.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 98
If the Provided Documentation is Valid
The provisional ballot must be tabulated if the proper and valid information is 
provided, and the ballot cannot be spoiled and revoted. Wis. Stat. § 6.97(3)(a).
1. Note on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104) and initial and date the 
Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123r) that the voter provided the 
required documentation. Id.
2. Remove the voter’s Provisional Ballot Certificate Envelope (EL-123) from the 
Inspectors’ Certificate of Provisional Ballots (EL-108) envelope. Id.
Best Practice: Verify that the Provisional Ballot Certificate Envelope (EL-
123) is still sealed. If the Provisional Ballot Certificate Envelope (EL-123) 
seal has been broken, the poll workers shall spoil the provisional ballot 
and instruct the voter to cast a new ballot.
3. The poll workers note in the poll book that the provisional ballot is withdrawn 
by writing “withdrawn” next to the PV#. See Id.
4. The voter is issued a sequential voter number, which is recorded on the 
poll list and noted on the Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123r) by 
marking “on poll list” in the column labeled “Voter Number Issued.” See 
Wis. Stat. §§ 6.79(2)(a) and 6.97(3)(a).
5. The poll workers remove the ballot from the Provisional Ballot Certificate 
Envelope (EL-123) and place it in the ballot box or voting tabulator without 
looking at the ballot or allowing anyone else to see what was written on it. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.97(3)(a).
6. Place the EL-123 back in the Inspectors’ Certification of Provisional Ballots 
(EL-108) envelope. Id.
If a provisional ballot is withdrawn before close of polls, and the Chief Inspector 
has already notified the municipal clerk that a provisional ballot was cast, they 
must notify the clerk that it has been withdrawn. Id. In most cases, it should be 
clear on the Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123r) which voters provided the 
missing information and voted, and which did not and may still provide it to the 
municipal clerk.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 99
Best Practice: At the end of voting, highlight the names of voters 
on the EL-123R who may still present the required information to the 
municipal clerk.
Sec. 14.13.4: Processing
A Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123r) must be completed by the poll 
workers at the polling place listing all of the voters who cast a provisional ballot. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.97(1) and (3)(b). The clerk must review the form and send a copy 
to each municipal, county, school or other special purpose district board of 
canvassers that will potentially need to canvass the outstanding provisional 
ballots. Wis. Stat. § 6.97(3)(b). The municipal clerk must keep a copy for his or her 
office and determine that each individual on the list is qualified to vote in the 
election. Id.
All voters who have been issued a provisional ballot must be tracked in WisVote 
on election night. WisVote must also be updated if/when a voter provides the 
missing information. Clerks with a WisVote provider will also need to notify the 
provider after the close of the polls on Election Day. Self-Providers must enter 
the information into WisVote. Please see the WisVote Application Training 
Manual for details.
 Ɋ Reliers must provide the Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123r) to 
their Provider.
 Ɋ Reliers must inform their Provider with updates if/when voters provide the 
missing information.
 Ɋ Providers must enter provisional ballot information into WisVote 
on election night on behalf of their reliers. Please see the WisVote 
Application Training Manual for details.
Provisional ballots are maintained by the municipal clerk (NOT delivered to the 
county clerk with all other election materials). See Wis. Stat. § 6.97(3) and (4).
Provisional ballots are not counted until the required information (either WI Driver 
License Number, State Identification Card Number, or proof of identification) is 
provided to either the poll workers by the close of the polls, or to the municipal 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 100
clerk or deputy by 4:00 p.m. on the Friday after the election regarding ID and 4:00 
p.m. the day after the election regarding a license or identification number. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.97(3)(b) and Wis. Admin. Code § EL 3.04(2).
Provisional voters who need to provide their WI Driver License Number or State 
Identification Card Number can do so in person, over the phone, or electronically 
by email or fax. Provisional voters who need to provide proof of identification must 
provide their ID in person. Wis. Stat. § 6.97(3)(b) and Wis. Admin. Code § EL 3.04(2).
Note: Voters who provide their information after Election Day do not 
sign the poll list. See Wis. Stat. §§ 6.97(3), (4).
If the person voting provisionally does not present the information to the 
municipal clerk by 4:00 p.m. on the Friday after the election, the Provisional 
Ballot Certificate Envelope (EL-123) is not opened. Neither the voter nor the ballot 
is counted as part of the Election Day results if the missing required information 
is not returned by the deadline. Wis. Stat. § 6.97(3)(c).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 101
Chapter 14.14:  Common 
Election Day Issues
Though it is impossible to prepare for every scenario, election officials may 
encounter unexpected events or behavior during Election Day. This chapter 
addresses some common issues that may arise at polling places.
Many of these scenarios don’t have obvious answers in the law. Election officials 
should always use their best judgment, and bear in mind what will be the least 
disruptive to the voting process.
Voters have an absolute right to cast or return their ballot and to be free from 
any disruptive conduct that may hinder that right. The right of a voter to cast or 
return their ballot always outweighs the right of any other individual to engage 
in non-election-related conduct at or near the voting activity areas, even if 
there is not a specific law prohibiting that conduct.
Sec. 14.14.1: Responding to 
Disruptive or Prohibited Conduct
Poll workers have the “full authority to maintain order and to enforce obedience 
to their lawful commands” during the election. Wis. Stat. § 7.37(2). If anyone 
refuses to obey the lawful orders of a poll worker, or is disorderly, or interrupts 
or disturbs the proceedings of the election, poll workers may order law 
enforcement to remove the person from the voting area or take them into 
custody. Id.
Additionally, election observers are prohibited from engaging in “loud, 
boisterous, or otherwise disruptive behavior, that, in the discretion of the 
designated election official, threatens the orderly conduct of the election or 
interferes with voting or registration. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.04(6). See Chapter 
14.5: “Election Observers” on page 13 for additional details on Election 
Observers.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 102
Best Practice: Election officials should use their best judgement when 
responding to disruptive or prohibited conduct. In some instances, 
warnings may be appropriate before escalation to removal, but in 
others, the conduct may be so prohibited or disruptive that immediate 
removal is warranted.
Sec. 14.14.2: Voter Cell Phones and 
Photography in Polling Area
Poll workers may direct anyone to cease their behavior if they are being 
disruptive, including when using cell phones.
Voter Phone Calls
Voters may use cell phones in the polling area if it is not disruptive.
Voters Taking Pictures or Videos
Voters should not be allowed to take photos or videos of other voters or other 
voters’ ballots without their consent. Although voters will of course be seen by 
in-person election observers as they move through the polling place to cast 
their ballots, being recorded or photographed may rise to a different level of 
making the voter feel uncomfortable or even intimidated.
Some voters may wish to take a picture of themselves or to live stream 
themselves in the polling place. Voters can record themselves or livestream 
themselves provided it is not disruptive and does not record other voters.
State law prohibits anyone from “[soliciting] a person to show how his or her 
vote is cast.” Wis. Stat. § 12.13(3)(q). State law also prohibits Wisconsin voters from 
intentionally “[showing] his or her marked ballot to any person or [placing] a mark 
upon the ballot so it is identifiable as his or her ballot.” Wis. Stat. § 12.13(1)(f). Both 
statutes can be implicated when voters take photographs of marked ballots, 
even their own ballot. Taking a photo of a market ballot for personal use is not 
prohibited. Posting that photo on social media, sharing it with someone else, or 
livestreaming voting choices is prohibited by state law.1
1 Under certain circumstances, this prohibition may not be enforceable.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 103
Poll workers should use their best judgment, but if a voter is taking a photo of 
themselves or their ballot quietly and without disrupting voting, it may be best 
to just allow that voter to finish up and leave the polling place. Confronting the 
voter over it may result in a larger disruption to voting than permitting the voter 
to take their photo.
Sec. 14.14.3: Election Observers
An election observer is a member of the public who is present at an 
observable location to observe an election or the absentee voting process. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.02(13), Wis. Stat. § 7.41.
See Chapter 14.5: “Election Observers” on page 13 for detailed information 
on the rights, responsibilities, and conduct of election observers.
Sec. 14.14.4: Federal Observers & 
the COCOA Act
It is becoming more common that poll workers may encounter other types of 
observers, who are entitled to observe under different laws than Wis. Stat. § 7.41.
Congressional Observers & the COCOA Act
Congressional observers are specifically authorized by the Committee on House 
Administration of the U.S. House of Representatives (“the Committee”). They are 
not candidates or current House representatives. They are official congressional 
staff who are being sent by the Committee to observe voting and gather 
information about the conduct of the election, specifically as it relates to House 
races. Congressional observers are a different type of lawful election observer, 
and their presence is authorized by the Congressional Observer Access Act of 
2024 (“COCOA Act”).
Congressional observers should be able to present an observer credential letter, 
which explains their presence and this process.
The COCOA Act grants congressional observers “full access to clearly observe 
all elements of election administration procedures, including, but not limited 
to, access to any area in which a ballot is cast, processed, scanned, tabulated, 
canvassed, recounted, audited, or certified, including during pre- and post-
election procedures.”
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 104
The COCOA Act also says that congressional observers can ask questions 
of election officials, but the law does not seem to require election officials to 
answer them immediately or on the spot.
The COCOA Act mirrors Wisconsin’s observer statute in several important ways. 
First, congressional observers are prohibited from handling ballots or election 
equipment, advocating for a position or candidate, taking any action to reduce 
ballot secrecy or voter privacy, or interfering with a voter or election official to 
vote or carry out their election administration duties. Likewise, congressional 
observers are subject to removal by election officials if the official has a 
reasonable basis to believe they are engaged in, or about to be engaged, in 
intimidation, deception, or disruption. If a congressional observer is removed, 
the COCOA Act provides that notice must be given to the Committee, and the 
Committee can replace them.
However, there are two apparent differences in how the 
COCOA Act treats congressional observers versus how state 
law would treat them as regular election observers:
 Ɋ More Observable Activity – As noted above, the COCOA Act appears 
to permit a broader spectrum of observable activity to congressional 
observers, including “pre- and post-election procedures” including “any 
area where a ballot is … processed”. This may mean that congressional 
observers are entitled to view the types of clerk administrative work that 
regular observers are not permitted to see.
 Ɋ More Detailed Removal Provision – The COCOA Act requires an election 
official to have a “reasonable basis” for removing a congressional 
observer. Our state law grants more leeway and focuses instead on the 
“overt acts” of observers. If election officials believe that removing a 
congressional observer is necessary, election officials should confer with 
their local counsel to ensure that the removal is properly articulated and 
the procedures of the COCOA Act are followed.
The COCOA Act also does not answer whether it intends to preempt existing 
state observer laws in their entirety or in part. The important parts of Wisconsin’s 
observer law already apply to congressional observers because the COCOA Act 
contains similar provisions. It’s less clear whether congressional observers must 
follow other aspects of Wis. Stat. § 7.41, such as whether they must follow the 
three- to eight-foot observable distance requirement. The Commission believes 
that the COCOA Act intends to grant congressional observers greater access 
than Wisconsin’s observer law permits (including the distance requirement), 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 105
however municipal clerks may wish to consult with their local counsel ahead of 
any congressional visit to fully determine the applicability of Wis. Stat. § 7.41 to 
congressional observers.
Congressional observers are still restricted from viewing information that is 
protected by other Wisconsin law — such as information related to confidential 
voters, and Personally Identifiable Information on certain forms of ID.
Other Types of Federal Observers
The Civil Rights Division of the U.S. Department of Justice may also conduct 
monitoring of polling places on Election Day using federal observers, who are 
authorized to observe under the Voting Rights Act. These types of observers 
may also be known as “election monitors.” They are not law enforcement 
officers, and are not there to enforce the law, just to observe and document. 
Their purpose to monitor the election to assess for compliance with the Voting 
Rights Act, and other federal statutes that offer voter protection, such as 
the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA). Both Democratic and Republican 
administrations have sent these federal observers to polling places in previous 
elections, and their use is not new.
Prior to 2013, federal observers were only sent to states and counties that were 
certified for observation by the Attorney General. However, the U.S. DOJ has 
since adopted the position that it will not rely on AG certifications as a basis 
for sending federal observers to monitor elections.2 This means that federal 
observers could be sent to any polling place in any state if the U.S. DOJ believes 
their presence is needed to assess compliance with federal law.
The U.S. DOJ should communicate with municipalities ahead of time to notify 
them of the observation. Municipal clerks should notify their governing bodies, 
their local counsel, and the Commission if they receive notice that US DOJ 
observers intend to appear, or if they do appear at any voting location.
Sec. 14.14.5: Media
Members of the media may be present at the polling place, central count 
location, or at a location where in-person absentee voting is occurring. They 
oftentimes want to shoot “B roll” footage of voting to use as part of Election Day 
stories, or even interview voters or election officials about their experiences.
2 For more information, see: justice.gov/crt/about-federal-observers-and-election-monitoring .
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 106
Members of the media are required to identify themselves, as well as their 
organization, upon arrival to the observable area. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.07(1). If 
they wish to enter the voting area (without cameras), they must check in as election 
observers and agree to be bound by those rules. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.07(3).
Members of the media are permitted to place and use video and still 
cameras outside of the voting area only, and only at the election officials’ 
discretion. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.07(1) . No matter where they are located, 
they are never permitted to record any confidential information, including 
voted ballots, and cannot interfere with voting or the orderly conduct of the 
election. Id.
Election officials may limit the amount of time members of the media can use 
video and still cameras, but if they do, the same limitations must be applied 
uniformly to all communications media members. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.07(2).
Interviews should be held outside of the voting area to ensure doors and 
entrances are not blocked and the voting process is not disrupted.
The municipal clerk should make sure that media is briefed about acceptable 
conduct, including by referring them to Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.07 and the 
Observer Chapter of this manual.
See Sec. 14.5.9: “Communications Media” on page 22 for more information 
about media in the polling place.
Sec. 14.14.6: Polling Place 
Accessibility Reviews
Polling Place Accessibility Reviewers are individuals who may appear at polling 
places on Election Day to conduct accessibility reviews.
The Commission conducts reviews of polling places to check for compliance 
with the Americans with Disabilities Act, Help America Vote Act, and relevant 
state statute. Wis. Stat. § 5.25(4)(a). Reviewers are temporary staff that have been 
trained by the Commission. No notice is given in advance of a review. The goal of 
these reviews is to document the polling place exactly how a voter experiences 
it. These individuals are not considered observers and may complete their 
work outside of any designated observer area. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.02(13). 
Identifying name tags and/or shirts are permitted. A reviewer will provide a 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 107
letter of introduction to the Chief Inspector upon arrival at the polling place. The 
letter includes contact information for the Commission’s staff in case the Chief 
Inspector has any questions or concerns. These individuals may use video and/or 
still cameras to document their findings but will not photograph voters’ faces or 
marked ballots.
See Chapter 14.23: “Polling Place Accessibility Reviews” on page 218 for 
more information on Polling Place Accessibility Reviewers.
Sec. 14.14.7: Electioneering
Electioneering is any activity intended to influence voting at an election. 
Wis. Stat. § 12.03(4) . Note that the statute says “an election,” not “the day’s 
election,” meaning any type of election or campaigning is prohibited in the 
polling place, not just things that are directly related to that day’s ballot. 
For example, partisan campaign attire would be equally prohibited at the 
nonpartisan election, as would attire for former candidates who are not 
currently on the ballot.
Best Practice: State law does not further define what it means to 
influence voting at an election, but such conduct will be fact-specific, 
as determined by the election officials present who observe the 
conduct.
Electioneering is prohibited not only at polling places, but anywhere where 
voting activity occurs, including the clerk’s office, alternate absentee ballot 
sites, and locations served by Special Voting Deputies. Wis. Stat. § 12.03(2). 
Electioneering is prohibited on public property within 100 feet of any building 
entrance a voter may use to access the polling place (or other voting activity 
location). Wis. Stat. § 12.03(2)(b)1. This does not apply to private property within 
the 100 feet rule.
Poll workers are required to enforce the prohibitions against electioneering and 
the distribution of unauthorized election-related material. Wis. Stat. § 7.37(2).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 108
Best Practice: To avoid messy disputes about where the 100-foot 
boundary begins or ends, municipal clerks can work with their local 
law enforcement to have them pace out the appropriate distance from 
the entrance to the polling place and put down cones or other markers 
so there can be no confusion.
The remainder of this section addresses a few instances in which the prohibition 
against electioneering may be relevant. It is by no means an exclusive list, it just 
tries to address common questions the Commission has received.
Mixed-Use Buildings Containing Polling Places
For many polling places, the room containing the voting area is not immediately 
accessible from the exterior door where voters enter. Sometimes voters must 
walk down a series of hallways, or even up or down to different floors, to reach 
the voting area. The electioneering statute does not clearly contemplate these 
types of arrangements, and the path between the exterior door and the voting 
area can sometimes become a mixed-use grey zone where enforcing the 
prohibition against electioneering can become difficult for poll workers.
The law is clear that electioneering is prohibited “within 100 feet of an entrance 
to a building containing a polling place.” Wis. Stat. § 12.03(2)(b)1. The law is 
further clear that electioneering is prohibited “during polling hours on election 
day at a polling place.” Wis. Stat. § 12.03(2)(a)1. A “polling place” means the 
“actual location wherein the voters vote is cast.” Wis. Stat. § 5.02(15).
The Commission believes that if the law protects the area outside the building 
entrance, and the voting area itself, then the law should also protect the 
reasonable internal pathway through a mixed-use building to the voting area. 
This includes the direct walking path and also any accessible routes. This does 
not mean the entire building containing the polling place must be screened 
for potential electioneering. Only the reasonable route from the exterior door 
to the voting area that voters must unavoidably walk through. Accordingly, 
poll workers may enforce the prohibition against electioneering along this 
reasonable, predetermined transit route.
Vehicles
It is not electioneering if a vehicle within 100 feet of the entrance to a polling 
place contains a bumper sticker. Wis. Stat. § 12.03(2)(d). Despite this exception, 
conduct could still cross the line and become electioneering if an individual 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 109
attempts to draw attention to the bumper sticker, or refer to it to influence 
voting. In this example, it is the conduct that could constitute electioneering, not 
the bumper sticker.
There is also an exception for bumper stickers on cars from the prohibition on 
election-related material, as discussed in the next section. Wis. Stat. § 12.035(4)(b).
Poll workers can ask (but not require) voters to move vehicles containing bumper 
stickers if complaints are received or the presence of the vehicle is disruptive.
Note that a bumper sticker means exactly what it says — a decal or other 
affixture applied to the bumper of a car. If a car contains a large sign on the top, 
on the hood, on the side, or anywhere other than the bumper, it is not a bumper 
sticker and does not qualify for the exception.
Employees who work in the building containing the polling place may have 
stickers on their cars but if complaints are made, the employee can be asked 
(but not required) to move his or her vehicle.
Election Materials
There are two types of posted materials that are prohibited. The first are 
campaign-like materials that may meet the definition of “electioneering,” as 
described in the previous section. The second are “election-related material,” 
which means written material which describes, or tries to describe, information 
relating to the rights or responsibilities of voters.
Campaign-Like Materials
Campaign signs or flyers may not be posted or distributed within the polling 
place or within 100 feet of any entrance to a polling place. Wis. Stat. § 12.03(2)
(a)1, 12.03(2)(b)1. If campaign materials are found at a polling place, poll workers 
must promptly remove the material from the polling place. Wis. Stat. § 12.03(3).
Election-Related Materials
“Election-related materials” are those that describe, or try to describe, 
information relating to the rights or responsibilities of voters. Wis. Stat. § 12.035(1). 
Examples may include unauthorized information about polling place hours, 
voter registration requirements, voting procedure, or any other information 
related to the orderly operation of the election. The Legislature has found 
that such materials may “mislead and confuse voters about their rights and 
responsibilities regarding the exercise of the franchise and [that they tend] to 
disrupt the flow of voting activities at such locations.” Wis. Stat. § 12.035(2).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 110
Only the municipal clerk or authorized election officials may post election-
related materials. Wis. Stat. § 12.035(4)(a).
The prohibitions against posting or distributing election-related material are the 
same as the prohibitions against electioneering. No one may do so at a polling 
place, within 100 feet of the entrance, or at other locations where voting activity 
is occurring. Wis. Stat. § 12.035(3).
Election officials or law enforcement may remove or confiscate unauthorized 
election-related material. Wis. Stat. § 12.035(5).
Campaign Attire
Just like any other conduct, campaign attire can cross the line and become 
electioneering if it is intended to influence voting at the election. Wis. Stat. § 12.03(4). 
If a voter is wearing a campaign item that expressly advocates voting for a specific 
candidate or referendum question, poll workers may ask that voter to remove or 
cover the campaign attire or return to the polling place without it. However, poll 
workers should also consider whether asking the voter to remove the campaign 
attire will draw more attention to the campaign attire from other voters. In some 
circumstances, it may be less disruptive to voting (with less of a risk of influence) to 
just allow the voter wearing the campaign attire to vote and leave the polling place.
Sec. 14.14.8: Solicitations
“Solicitations” refer to attempts to sell goods or services, often by a person 
stationed in one location to passersby. Solicitations in the voting area are 
almost certain to interrupt or disrupt the voting proceedings, and are not 
permitted. See Wis. Stat. § 12.13(3)(x). Solicitations within the building along the 
walking path to the polling place are likewise disruptive.
Solicitors can be present outside of the building housing the polling place, as long 
as they do not interfere with the access of voters entering or leaving the polling 
place. Solicitors do not have to be outside the 100-foot electioneering zone, unless 
their conduct crosses the line and intends to influence voting at the election.
Sec. 14.14.9: Petitions on Election Day
It is the formal opinion of the Wisconsin Elections Commission that soliciting 
signatures for a petition within 100 feet of a polling place on Election Day is 
NOT considered “electioneering,” provided that the petition is not related to 
the election at hand and does not attempt to influence the voter regarding 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 111
that day’s vote. For example, petitions for the recall of an elected official (who 
is not on the ballot that day), the institution of referenda, or other petitions to 
a governing body are not considered “election-related material” and do not 
violate the relevant statutes on electioneering.
However, petitioning inside the building containing the polling place on 
Election Day, along the route to the voting area, is considered disruptive, and is 
prohibited. See Id.
Sec. 14.14.10: Exit Polls
Exit polls are permitted by Wisconsin law but must not interfere with the 
orderly conduct of the election. Exit polls in the voting area are almost 
certain to interrupt or disrupt the voting proceedings and are not permitted. 
See Id. Exit polls within the building along the walking path to the polling 
place can be likewise disruptive.
Exit polls should be conducted outside of the polling place. Persons conducting 
exit polls must not interfere with the access of voters entering and leaving the 
polling place. See Id.
Voters should not be given the impression that their participation is required, 
and the exit poll should not resemble a sample ballot or otherwise imply that it 
is part of the election process. See Wis. Stat. § 12.035.
Best Practice: Persons conducting exit polls are encouraged to contact 
the municipal clerk to discuss the conduct of exit polls, in order to take 
into consideration any unique circumstances created by the layout or 
configuration of a particular polling place.
Sec. 14.14.11: Bake Sales & Fundraising 
Events at or near Polling Places
Nonprofit groups, parent-teacher associations, or other interest groups often 
wish to take advantage of voter turnout to hold bake sales, fundraisers, or other 
engagement events at or near polling places. Such activities are not explicitly 
prohibited by law — assuming they do not cross into electioneering — but they still 
must not interfere with the orderly conduct of the election or intimidate voters.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 112
Such events should never be permitted within the voting area, as their presence 
is almost certain to disrupt voting and cause a distraction. See Wis. Stat. § 7.37(2). 
These events may be present within the polling place or along the voter path 
from the entrance at the election officials’ discretion.
Groups wishing to conduct this type of activity at or near a polling place 
should ensure they understand the prohibitions against electioneering and 
unauthorized election-related material, as well as the implications of the 
prohibition against bribery. Wis. Stat. § 12.03, 12.035, 12.11.
Best Practices:
 Ɋ If events like bake sales or fundraisers take place within the polling 
place, they should be set up in a separate room, or in a remote 
location.
 Ɋ Any member of the public that may be at the polling place should be 
allowed the same access to the bake sale or fundraising activity as 
voters.
 Ɋ Election officials should not advertise the presence of the event or 
sale to voters while they are registering, waiting to vote, or casting 
their ballot.
Q A group is passing out water or other freebies to voters who are waiting in 
line either inside or outside the polling place. Is that allowed?
A If the activity is intended to influence voting and is occurring within 100 
feet of the polling place entrance, it is likely electioneering and would 
not be permitted. Wis. Stat. § 12.03. It may still be considered bribery 
depending on circumstances, and concerns about bribery should be 
referred to local law enforcement. Wis. Stat. § 12.11.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 113
Q There is an event set up across the street from the polling place offering 
freebies to the public, but it appears geared towards voters, or is Election 
Day-specific. Is that allowed?
A If it is more than 100 feet from the entrance to the polling place, the event 
is not electioneering, even if it is sponsored by a candidate, political 
party, or other group. It may still be considered bribery depending on 
circumstances, and concerns about bribery should be referred to local 
law enforcement. Wis. Stat. § 12.11.
Sec. 14.14.12: Weapons & 
Concealed Carry
Note: Wisconsin law does not expressly prohibit the carry of weaponry 
in polling places unless certain factors are met, and any prohibition on 
the possession of weapons (including legal possession) is a decision 
that needs to be made at the municipal level.
Best Practice: This decision should be made in advance of the election, 
adequately posted, and passed through an approved resolution or 
ordinance by the governing body.
Prior to Election Day, municipal clerks and their governing bodies should review 
the Commission’s entire guidance on Wisconsin Weapons/Physical Security 
Laws and Applicability to Polling Places.3
Exception: K-12 Schools – Weapons Always Prohibited: Concealed carry and 
open carry are always prohibited in K-12 schools, including when school 
buildings are used as polling places. Wis. Stat. § 948.605(2).
The law generally allows a property’s owner to set restrictions on that property, 
including prohibitions on openly carried or concealed weaponry. Wis. Stat. § 
943.13, 943.14. The prohibition of weaponry in nonresidential buildings (where 
3 Issued Oct. 20, 2020, available at: elections.wi.gov/sites/default/files/legacy/2020-10/Weapons_
Polling%2520Place%2520Memo%252010-23-2020.pdf .
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 114
polling places are most likely to be located) requires the posting of a sign in 
prominent places near all entrances where an entrant can be reasonably 
expected to see the posting. The restriction cannot be applied to a designated 
parking area if the weapon remains in the vehicle. Government facilities are 
also required to post signage near all entrances where an entrant can be 
reasonably expected to see the posting. Wis. Stat. § 943.13.
Local government units may also restrict the right to carry concealed weapons 
in polling places by relying on the special event provisions of the Trespass to 
Land statutes. Wis. Stat. § 943.13(1m)(c)3.
If a voter, observer, or other individual attempts to bring a weapon into the polling 
place, election officials should immediately contact their municipal clerk and local 
counsel to understand the polling place’s rules and restrictions on such weapons. 
If the individual is using or threatening to use the weapon in a dangerous manner, 
election officials should immediately contact law enforcement.Z
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 115
Chapter 14.15: Processing 
Absentee Ballots: Polling Place
Sec. 14.15.1: Introduction
Any registered voter in the state of Wisconsin may exercise the option to vote 
by absentee if, for any reason, they are unable or unwilling to appear at their 
polling place on Election Day. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.20, 6.85(1). For this reason, special 
procedures are in place to protect the voter and the integrity of the process.
Absentee ballots cannot be opened or counted until the polls open on Election 
Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a). The municipal clerk may take steps to organize the 
returned ballots by ward and by last name and may flag issues with eligibility or 
certificate envelopes. No envelopes can be opened before the polls open.
Absentee ballots are counted at the polling place for the ward in which the 
voter resides, or at the designated meeting location of the board of absentee 
ballot canvassers (commonly referred to as “central count”). Chapter 14.16: 
“Processing Absentee Ballots: “Central Count”” on page 152 covers 
absentee ballot processing and handling in “central count” jurisdictions.
Absentee ballots must be returned so they may be delivered to the polling place 
or “central count” location no later than 8pm on Election Day, Wis. Stat. §6.87(6).
All absentee ballots must be processed in the same room votes are cast or at 
a “central count” location so that any interested observer is able to hear the 
public announcement of the names of the absentee voters. Wis. Admin. Code § 
EL 4.03(1)(b)1. and Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a).
Sec. 14.15.2: Polling Place Set-Up & 
Absentee Processing Area
Each polling place should be arranged to support the efficient processing of 
absentee ballots throughout the day. Poll workers should designate a specific 
absentee ballot processing table or zone within the polling place. This area must 
be located in the same room where in-person voting occurs, but positioned to 
minimize disruption to voter check-in, voting booths, and ballot casting.
A35
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 116
The absentee ballot processing table or zone should be equipped with the 
materials necessary to review certificate envelopes, record voter participation in 
the poll list, and securely deposit processed ballots into the ballot box or optical 
scan equipment.
Important materials include:
 Ɋ Ineligible Voter List
 Ɋ EL-102 – Certificate of Rejected Absentee Ballots
 Ɋ EL-103 – Used Certificate Envelopes of Absentee Voters
 Ɋ EL-104 – Inspector’s Statement
 Ɋ EL-124 - Absentee Ballot Log
 Ɋ EL-125 – Absentee ballot carrier envelope/containers
Observation of Absentee Ballot Processing
When setting up an absentee ballot processing area within the polling place, it’s 
important to understand where observer areas are required.
Any table within the polling place where absentee ballots are being 
processed is generally observable (meaning it cannot be concealed from 
view), but not all aspects of absentee ballot processing must be within the 
three- to eight-foot distance of the observable area. See Wis. Stat. § 7.41(2) 
and Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(1)(b).
Activity that must be within three- to eight-feet includes:
 Ɋ The place where absentee voter names are announced. Wis. Admin. 
Code § EL 4.03(1)(b)1.
 Ɋ The place where voters register (if it’s the same as where absentee ballot 
processing occurs) Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(1)(b)2.
 Ɋ The place where absentee ballots are remade. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(1)(b)3.
Additionally, poll workers must allow observers to observe absentee ballot certificate 
envelopes that have been set aside to be rejected. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.03(7).
Any other type of absentee processing activity can be observed from the 
observable area, even if it is more than three- to eight-feet away.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 117
See Chapter 14.5: “Election Observers” on page 13 for more detailed 
information on the rights and responsibilities of election observers.
Sec. 14.15.3: How Absentee 
Ballots Arrive at Polling Places 
or “Central Count”
On or prior to Election Day, the municipal clerk delivers the absentee ballots that 
were received at their office to the polling place or “central count” site. The ballots 
are delivered in a sealed envelope, container, or courier bag that bears the name 
and official title of the clerk and the language found in Wis. Stat. § 6.88(1). (EL-125 
Absentee Ballot Carrier). If a container such as a box is used to transport absentee 
ballots to the polling place, then that container must have the EL-125 attached 
and be properly sealed. Once the container has been sealed, it cannot be opened 
again until the container or envelope is in the same room where votes are being 
cast at the polls during polling hours on Election Day or at “central count.”
Sec. 14.15.4: Processing 
Absentee Ballots at the Polls
This section applies to absentee ballot processing at polling places. See 
Chapter 14.16: “Processing Absentee Ballots: “Central Count”” for modified 
procedures for jurisdictions that utilize boards of absentee ballot canvassers 
(“central count”).
Poll workers can begin processing absentee ballots no earlier than when polls 
open at 7 a.m. on Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a). Absentee ballots must be 
processed in the same room where in-person voters are casting their ballots on 
Election Day. Id.
Poll workers must continue counting absentee ballots at polling places if there are 
still lawfully-returned ballots to be counted after 8 p.m. See Wis. Stat. § 7.52(1)(a) 
(permitting counting of absentees past 8 p.m. at central count), Wis. Stat. § 6.78(4) 
(permitting in person voters to cast ballots past 8 p.m. if they are in line by 8 p.m.), 
Wis. Stat. § 6.87(6) (permitting municipal clerks to transport absentee ballots to 
polling places until 7:59 p.m.).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 118
Poll workers must process absentee ballots continuously from the moment 
polls open at 7 a.m. until all ballots have been processed, even if they are very 
busy at the polling place. Moderation is key, poll workers must make multiple 
assessments throughout Election Day to balance processing absentee ballots 
and voters at the polls.
Sec. 14.15.5: Step 1: Retrieve & 
Document Absentee Ballot Batches
Returned absentee ballots arrive at the polling place either with the rest of the 
election supplies or through couriers throughout the day. Returned absentee 
ballots are stored in carrier envelopes, courier bags, or other containers. See 
Wis. Stat. § 6.88(1), (2). Municipal clerks provide detailed training and instruction 
to their poll workers on where to find the batches of returned absentee ballots 
that are to be processed.
The first step is locating the carrier envelopes or containers (all of which 
will have Form EL-125 attached) and bringing them to the absentee ballot 
processing area. See Chapter 14.3: “Setting up the Polling Place” on page 
7.
In general, poll workers should:
 Ɋ Open the large carrier envelopes or containers (EL-125) containing the 
absentee ballots. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a)
 Ɋ Remove each Absentee Certificate Envelope (EL-122, EL-122M, EL-122 SVD, 
and EL-122 Special) organize or bundle them according to the municipal 
clerk’s training and instructions.
It is likely that there will be a subset of absentee certificate envelopes that the 
municipal clerk has bundled or otherwise flagged for poll workers to set aside 
as “To be Rejected.” Keep these sealed certificate envelopes in a secure location 
until 8 p.m. See Sec. 14.15.14: “Rejecting Absentee Ballots” on page 146.
Similarly, there may also be “Presidential-only” ballots transported alongside 
absentee ballots. These ballots will be in envelopes containing the language: 
“This envelope contains the vote for president and vice president of a new 
resident and shall be opened only at the polls during polling hours on Election 
Day.” See Sec. 5.5.5: “Processing Presidential-Only Ballots on Election Day” for 
information on how to process Presidential-only ballots at the polling place.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 119
Sec. 14.15.6: Step 2: Review Certificate 
Envelopes for Sufficiency
Open or Damaged Envelopes
Before looking at the details of the certificate, poll workers should first 
examine each certificate envelope to see if any are opened or damaged. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b). If a ballot is open, or has been opened and resealed, 
the poll workers shall not count the ballot. Id.
Best Practice: Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b) is intended to prohibit absentee 
ballots from being counted that have been obviously re-opened and 
resealed (torn flap, slit seams, clumsy repair of paper). Sometimes, 
voters tape their absentee ballots closed. Tape on a certificate 
envelope flap alone is not necessarily an indication that the ballot 
has been opened and resealed. Ideally, the municipal clerk will have 
noticed taped or open absentee ballots as they are returned. Poll 
workers should contact the municipal clerk if they encounter any 
certificate envelopes that are either opened or resealed. Ultimately, it 
is the decision of the municipal clerk and the boards of canvassers to 
decide whether these ballots should be accepted or rejected.
Certificate Envelope Sufficiency
Extensive detail on how to review absentee certificate envelopes for sufficiency 
is covered by Sec. 14.15.12: “Absentee Certificate Envelopes” on page 127. 
Poll workers should review that section in its entirety for step-by-step guidance 
on how to review certificate envelopes, and to understand the Commission’s 
guidance for resolving common omissions or errors. It is always up to the 
municipal clerk to decide whether a certificate envelope is insufficient, so it’s 
also important to understand any jurisdiction-specific guidance or training 
from the clerk in addition to the Commission’s guidance.
See Chapter 14.18: “Correcting Defective Certificate Envelopes” on page 161 
for information on correcting incomplete certificate envelopes.
See Sec. 14.15.14: “Rejecting Absentee Ballots” on page 146 for information on 
setting aside incomplete certificate envelopes for rejection.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 120
Sec. 14.15.7: Step 3: Check 
Ineligible Voter List(s)
Ineligible Voter List (Felon List).
Each polling place will be equipped with the Ineligible Voter List, which 
poll workers use to determine if voters are ineligible to vote due to a felony 
conviction. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(c).
If the voter’s name matches a name on the Ineligible Voter List, poll workers 
continue processing the absentee ballot as described below but then issue a 
challenge prior to placing the ballot in the ballot box or optical scanner. Id. See 
Chapter 14.7: “Challenging Electors on Election Day” on page 43 for more 
information on how to challenge absentee voters at the polling place.
Q What if the voter’s envelope, or entry on the poll book, is marked “ineligible 
to vote per Department of Corrections,” but the voter’s name is not on the 
Ineligible Voter List?
A Contact the municipal clerk to resolve the discrepancy. It’s possible that 
the voter has since completed their sentence, though if they were ineligible 
at the time they returned their absentee ballot, that might pose another 
eligibility issue. See Wis. Stat. § 6.87(2) (requiring voters to certify they are 
qualified to vote when completing the absentee certificate envelope).
Best Practice: The poll workers should make a reasonable effort 
to contact the Commission to confirm a voter’s felon status before 
processing the absentee ballot.
See Chapter 3.9: “Voters Convicted of a Felony/Treason/Bribery” for more 
information on voter ineligibility due to a felony conviction.
Adjudicated Incompetent “List”
In addition to the Ineligible Voter List, some municipal clerks may provide 
another list of the voters in their jurisdictions who have been adjudicated 
incompetent, in which a court has determined they are incapable of 
understanding the objective of the elective process. Wis. Stat. § 6.03. Providing 
this “list” is not required by law, but some clerks choose to provide it to aid 
poll workers in confirming voter eligibility. If the clerk has provided this list, it is 
a best practice to check absentee voters against that list before continuing 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 121
with the processing of the ballot. If a voter’s name appears on the adjudicated 
incompetent “list,” poll workers should confer with the municipal clerk and then 
challenge the voter’s ballot. See Chapter 14.7: “Challenging Electors on Election 
Day” on page 43 for more information on how to challenge absentee voters 
at the polling place.
Note: Public Access Caution – Adjudications of incompetency 
(including the identities of individuals who have been so adjudicated) 
are strictly confidential. Wis. Stat. §§ 19.35(1)(a), 54.75. If the municipal 
clerk does provide an adjudicated incompetent “list,” observers should 
not be permitted to view it under Wis. Stat. § 54.75. See Wis. Stat. § 7.41(2) 
(permitting observation of all public aspects of the voting process). 
Likewise, if a challenge needs to be made against a voter due to an 
adjudication of incompetency, the voter’s details shall be shielded and 
protected from public observation. Wis. Stat. §§ 19.35(1)(a), 54.75, 7.41(2).
See Chapter 3.8: “Voters Adjudicated Incompetent by a Court” for more 
information on voter ineligibility due to adjudications of incompetency.
Sec. 14.15.8: Step 4: Check 
in Voters at Poll Book
Before opening the certificate envelopes to retrieve the absentee ballots, poll 
workers must announce the names of the absentee voters (or their confidential 
voter number) and check them into the poll book. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a). 
Note: Poll workers are not required to also read the addresses of 
absentee voters, though there is no harm in doing so. Wis. Stat. § 
6.88(3)(a) (requiring poll workers to “…announce the name of the 
absent elector…” The poll book process should follow the regular voter 
check in process, which is described in further detail in Chapter 14.9: 
“Voting Procedure” on page 65.
Under no circumstances should there be a separate poll book to use 
for processing absentee voters, even if the clerk or poll workers think 
it will be more efficient. Using a separate poll book means that poll 
workers will be unable to check for instances of double voting.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 122
Absentee Voter Number. Statute requires poll workers to enter an indication in 
the poll book next to the voter’s name indicating that they cast an absentee 
ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a). The easiest way to do this is by entering the letter 
“A” to proceed the next available voter number.
Figure 16: Example of how an absentee voter number 
should be recorded in the poll book
Best Practice: The Commission recommends that poll workers should 
check in small batches of absentee ballots. Poll workers can wear signs 
or name tags on the back of their shirts that say “Processing Absentee 
Ballots,” which aim to explain possible voter concerns about why 
someone is handling more than one ballot at a time.
There is no requirement that poll workers must wait in line to check in 
absentee ballots at the pollbook. Waiting in line with in-person voters is a good 
idea to ensure that voters don’t have to wait, but this is a balance that will have 
to be managed by Chief Inspectors. Sometimes, to avoid falling behind on 
processing absentee ballots, poll workers will have to step in front of voters, even 
if the polling place is very busy.
Sec. 14.15.9: Step 5: Open Envelopes 
& Remove & Examine Ballots
The same batch of absentee ballots that were checked into the poll book 
should then be opened together.
Poll workers should:
1. Open each certificate envelope in a manner that does not rip or damage 
the certificate on the face. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a). There should be letter 
openers in the polling place supplies.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 123
2. Carefully remove each ballot from its certificate envelope without 
unfolding or looking at the ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a).
Note: Some municipalities utilize certified ballot marking devices, 
like ExpressVote or ImageCast X, for in-person absentee voting. 
Accordingly, poll workers may encounter these types of ballots when 
opening and processing absentees, in addition to the traditional 
optical scan ballots. These ballots are “skinnier” than optical scan 
ballots but can still be processed by the tabulator without issues.
Figure 17: The endorsement typically appears at the bottom can look like this:
3. Examine the contents of the envelope to ensure that only a single ballot of 
any one kind is enclosed. Id.
Note: It should be uncommon, but occasionally, a certificate envelope 
will contain more than one ballot, inserted by the voter. This is usually 
caused by a voter returning both a primary and general election 
ballot or if a married couple attempts to return both of their ballots 
in the same certificate envelope. If this occurs, poll workers should 
immediately notify the municipal clerk. These incidents should always 
be documented on the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104), including details 
about how the situation was resolved.
4. Unfold and flatten each ballot once they are separated from their 
certificate envelopes. This ensures that poll workers won’t know whose 
ballot is whose.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 124
Best Practice: Check the ballots for any heavy creases or unusual 
marks such as cross-outs, incomplete, or erasures. This is especially 
important for creases that bisect any of the ovals on the ballot, as the 
creases may interfere with the optical scan voting equipment. If such 
creases are present, the ballot should be remade. See Chapter 14.22: 
“Overvoted & Crossover Voted Ballots” on page 211 for procedures for 
remaking ballots.
5. Check for Clerk Initials. Poll workers must examine each ballot to ensure it 
has been endorsed by the issuing clerk. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.88(3)(a), 5.54. The 
endorsement section usually appears on the back of ballots. Absentee 
ballots only need the initials of the municipal clerk or his or her deputies. 
Wis. Stat. § 5.54. It is not necessary for two poll workers to also input their 
initials the way they would do for in-person voting on Election Day.
Figure 18: Endorsement section of an optical scan ballot with 
highlights indicating where to find the municipal clerk’s initials
f
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 125
Q What if the clerk’s initials are missing in the endorsement section of the 
absentee ballot?
A The ballot must still be counted but do not add the initials.1 The incident 
should be thoroughly documented and the ballot(s) counted once poll 
workers can confirm with the municipal clerk that the ballot was properly 
issued. The first step is to contact the municipal clerk to determine and 
confirm that the absentee ballot was properly issued, and the omission of 
the initials was due to clerk oversight. Once that is confirmed, document 
that confirmation (as well as other details of the incident) on the 
Inspector’s Statement (EL-104) and count the ballot.
6. Review Ballot for Overvotes and Crossvotes. An “overvote” is when a 
voter has made more than one candidate selection in a race where 
they are only permitted to vote for one candidate. A “crossvote” is when 
a voter has voted in more than one party’s primary in the partisan 
primary election (either by marking selections in more than one primary 
section on optical scan ballots, or marking selections on more than one 
primary ballot for hand-count paper ballots). If overvotes or crossvotes 
are present, poll workers may have to remake the ballot if voter intent is 
clear. See “Overvoted & Crossover Voted Ballots” on page 211 for more 
information and the procedures for remaking ballots.
Sec. 14.15.10: Step 6: Tabulate the Ballots
Once all ballots in the batch have been reviewed, poll workers should tabulate 
them. For electronic voting equipment, insert each ballot into the tabulator one 
at a time. For hand-count jurisdictions, deposit the ballots into the appropriate 
ballot box.
1 Wis. Stat. §§ 7.51, 6.80, 6.88, 7.37, and 9.01, when read together, unambiguously establish that the lack of 
poll worker or municipal clerk initials on a ballot is not by itself a reason for rejecting the ballot, whether 
in an initial canvass or during a recount. Unendorsed ballots must be counted in the election unless 
there is a reason beyond the lack of an endorsement to reject them, such as when the total number of 
ballots exceeds the total number of voters recorded in the poll books. This section makes evident that 
the only reason that a missing endorsement would result in a ballot not being counted in the Election-
Day canvass is if it is necessary to put the unendorsed ballot aside to reconcile the number of ballots 
with the number of voters who are recorded as having cast ballots in the election. Gonfiantini v. Rock 
County Board of Canvassers, 2025 WI App 26, 416 Wis. 2d 118, 20 N.W.3d 773, 24-1233.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 126
Best Practice: Poll workers should consider wearing a sign or sticker on 
the backs of their shirts saying something like “Processing Absentees.” 
This can reduce voter and observer confusion in case someone is 
wondering why an individual is inserting multiple ballots into the 
tabulator.
Sec. 14.15.11: Step 7: Record Details & 
Preserve Used Certificate Envelopes
Record Details
If anything unusual or noteworthy occurred while processing each batch of 
absentee ballots, ensure that the details of what occurred are recorded on the 
Inspector’s Statement (EL-104). No detail is too small or insignificant — it is much 
better to take detailed notes that are not needed than to omit information that 
could later aid clerks, canvassers, or recounters.
Retain the Used Certificate Envelopes
After the ballots have been tabulated, retain the used certificate envelopes in 
the manner directed by the municipal clerk. See Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(a).
Sec. 14.15.12: Absentee 
Certificate Envelopes
An absentee certificate envelope is the specialized, designated envelope 
containing the voter’s voted absentee ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(2). The certificate 
envelopes serve as a combined absentee ballot application and an absentee 
ballot certificate.
The certificates (containing the voter and witness information) appear on the 
face of the envelopes (the side that does not contain the sealed flap) and are 
designed and prescribed by the Commission for use by all municipalities.2
2 The most recent redesign of the absentee certificate envelopes was completed in August 2023. The 
certificate envelopes underwent extensive usability testing with clerks, voters, witnesses, assistants, 
poll workers, and the United States Postal Service (USPS). See the meeting materials from the August 4, 
2023 meeting for a summary of the project, and request for approval.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 127
There are four variations of the Absentee Ballot Certificate Envelope:
 Ɋ EL-122 Standard Absentee Certificate Envelope (Purple), used in most 
situations. The Commission has also prescribed a Spanish version of this 
certificate envelope (EL-122S).
 Ɋ EL-122M Absentee Certificate Envelope (Brown), used for military and 
permanent overseas voters.
 Ɋ EL-122SVD (Teal), used for absentee voting conducted by Special Voting 
Deputies in residential care facilities and retirement homes.
 Ɋ EL-122SP, used for voters in SVD-eligible facilities that are not served by 
SVDs.
Note about Color: The different colors of certificate envelopes are to 
help poll workers quickly distinguish between certificate types. Text 
on the certificate itself also clearly indicates which type of certificate 
envelope it is. An absentee ballot should not be rejected just because 
the certificate envelope is not printed in color.
Certificate Envelope Sufficiency
Deciding whether a certificate envelope is sufficient is the decision of the 
municipal clerk. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.87(9), 6.88(3)(b). However, this section contains 
the Commission’s guidance for how to interpret the law and procedures for 
returned absentee ballots. Poll workers should also be sure they understand any 
additional instructions or guidance they received from their municipal clerk as 
part of their poll worker training.
Rejectable vs. Correctable by Clerk or Voter
This section offers the Commission’s guidance on each section of each 
certificate envelope, and what poll workers should do if required fields are 
incomplete or missing.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 128
As this section explains in further detail, poll workers should 
keep the following general principles in mind:
 Ɋ Correctable by Voter/Witness OR Rejectable – If information provided 
by the voter or the witness is missing (voter address, voter printed name, 
voter signature, voter DOB, witness signature, witness printed name), the 
certificate is likely insufficient and either needs to be corrected by the 
voter/witness, or the ballot needs to be rejected. The exception is the 
witness address field, which has its own dedicated section See Chapter 
14.17: “Witness Address Requirements” on page 157.
 Ɋ Correctable by Clerk AND Count Ballot3 – If information is missing that 
was supposed to have been provided by the clerk (ward or aldermanic 
district, election date, initials at top for photo ID requirement), the ballot 
should not be rejected. Instead, the municipal clerk should correct the 
certificate envelope by providing the missing information, and document 
the correction on the Inspector’s Statement.
If an absentee certificate envelope is insufficient, the municipal clerk has two 
options for how to proceed. First, they may attempt to return the ballot to the 
voter, together with a new envelope, if necessary, whenever time permits the 
voter to correct the defect and return the ballot in time. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9). See 
Chapter 14.18: “Correcting Defective Certificate Envelopes” on page 161. 
Second, they may decide that the ballot should be set aside to be rejected. Wis. 
Stat. § 6.88(3)(b). See Sec. 14.15.14: “Rejecting Absentee Ballots” on page 146.
How to Review the Absentee Certificate Envelope
This section is designed to be a Step-by-Step resource to aid election officials in 
reviewing absentee certificate envelopes for sufficiency.
Step 1: Know Which Envelope Type is Used
Poll workers should first understand what type of absentee certificate envelope 
they have before them. Most certificate envelopes will be the standard EL-122 
(Purple), which are used by most regular absentee voters. If the certificate 
envelope is for military and overseas voters, voters who used Special Voting 
Deputies (SVDs), or voters who reside in care facilities not served by SVDs, 
consult Steps Five, Six, or Seven for additional components to review.
3 The Commission is prohibited from issuing guidance that municipal clerks can correct, or “cure,” 
missing aspects of the witness address, but that prohibition does not extend to other missing 
information on the certificate information, particularly for information provided by the clerk and not the 
voter or witness. White et al. v. Wisconsin Elections Commission, (2022-CV-001008).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 129
Step 2: Check Initials at Top
The municipal clerk will have entered his or her initials in either of the spaces 
at the top indicating that the voter either provided proof of identification or is 
exempt from doing so. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(2).
Figure 19: Examples of the Initial Lines on each certificate envelope type.
Q If the initials are missing, should the ballot be set aside to be rejected?
A No. Missing poll worker initials generally do not mean that the certificate 
envelope is insufficient. Instead, the first step is to contact the municipal 
clerk to confirm that the absentee voter either properly satisfied the 
photo ID requirement or is properly exempt. If they did provide the photo 
ID, or are exempt from that requirement, the municipal clerk, deputy 
clerk, or delegated poll worker should place their initials on the certificate 
envelope. The only exception is for the EL-122SP (Special) envelope 
described in Step Seven of this chapter. Clerk initials are not required for 
the EL-122SP.
Fixing this issue should only occur on Election Day and the process should 
be documented thoroughly on how the missing information was handled. 
This will resolve the issue permanently, which will also be helpful in case of 
a recount. Though it should be uncommon, if the clerk confirms that the 
voter did not meet the photo ID requirement, or is not properly exempt, 
the ballot should be set aside to be rejected unless the municipal clerk 
attempts to return it to the voter to be corrected. These steps should be 
documented on the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 130
Step 3: Review Voter Information
The clerk or voter must complete the voter information section. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.87(4)(b)1. The information can be provided using a label generated by the 
municipal clerk through WisVote or handwritten by the voter or clerk.
The Election Date must also be completed. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(2). If the clerk uses 
printed labels from WisVote with the voter information, the Election Date will be 
printed on the label.
Best Practice: The Commission strongly recommends that municipal 
clerks generate the voter information section by using pre-printed 
labels generated from WisVote. For instructions on how to do this, see 
Sec. 3.7 of the WisVote Manual.
Q If information from the voter section is missing, should the ballot be rejected?
A Yes, but only if the information is required to be entered by the voter. 
Most clerks use pre-printed voter information labels that already contain 
all the required information for this step. However, if the voter was required 
to input their name and address, and that information is missing or 
incomplete, the ballot should be rejected unless the municipal clerk 
attempts to return it to the voter to be corrected. These steps should be 
documented on the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 131
Q If the voter’s ward or aldermanic district or Election Date are missing, 
should the ballot be rejected?
A No. Although this information is required to appear on the certificate 
envelope, voters cannot reasonably be expected to know their ward 
and aldermanic districts, and it would be unfair to penalize them for it 
by rejecting their ballot. If the ward is present, but not the aldermanic 
district, that is sufficient.4 Likewise, the ballot should not be rejected 
if the municipal clerk failed to input the date of the election. If this 
information is missing, and the clerk has not utilized pre-printed labels 
for the voter information, the municipal clerk or deputy clerk should 
enter the information onto the certificate envelope, and the ballot 
should be counted. Fixing this issue on Election Day will resolve the issue 
permanently, which will also be helpful in case of a recount. These steps 
should be documented on the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104).
Figure 20: The voter information section from the regular 
absentee certificate envelope (EL-122). This section is largely 
the same for all absentee certificate envelope designs
4 Wis. Stat. § 6.87(2) (I, ...., certify subject to the penalties of s. 12.60 (1) (b), Wis. Stats., for false 
statements, that I am a resident of the [.... ward of the] (town) (village) of ...., or of the .... aldermanic 
district in the city of ....,”) (Emphasis added).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 132
Figure 21: Example of a pre-printed voter information label generated by the 
clerk from WisVote. In most cases, this will be affixed over the “Step One” box 
on the envelope, and contains all of the necessary information. The election 
date and ward number have been highlighted above to show their locations.
Step 4: Voter Signature
The voter must complete the certification section by signing where highlighted. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.87(4)(b)1. If the voter needed assistance signing their name to 
the certification, the assistor signs the voter’s name and signs their own name 
below in the assistor’s field. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(5).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 133
Figure 22: The certification and voter signature section from the 
regular absentee certificate envelope (EL-122). This section is largely 
the same for all absentee certificate envelope designs
Q If the voter accidentally signed the assistant signature line, should the 
ballot be rejected?
A No, assuming it is the voter’s signature. If the poll worker can reasonably 
tell that the signature belongs to the voter, the ballot should not be 
rejected because the voter has still substantially complied with the 
requirements of making the certification. The same is true if the voter 
draws an arrow up to the voter signature line. However, if the signature is 
illegible, and the poll worker cannot reasonably determine that it is the 
voter’s signature, the ballot should be rejected unless the municipal clerk 
attempts to return it to the voter to be corrected. These steps should be 
documented on the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104).
Step 5: Witness Information
The witness must complete this section by signing and printing their name and 
providing their address. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(4)(b)1.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 134
Witness Address
What constitutes a witness address has been the subject of extensive 
litigation. For more detailed Commission guidance on what constitutes a 
witness address, see Chapter 14.17: “Witness Address Requirements” on 
page 157. Election officials should read that section in its entirety before 
deciding whether to return or reject an absentee ballot due to a witness 
address insufficiency.
Q What if the witness’ address is outside the municipality or outside of 
Wisconsin? Should the ballot be rejected?
A No. There is no residence requirement for the witness.
Witness Printed Name and Signature. The witness must add both their printed 
name and signature for the certificate to be sufficient.
Figure 23: The witness certificate section of the regular EL-122.
Q If the witness signed or printed their name, and then drew an arrow or used 
“ditto” marks or similar for the signature, should the ballot be rejected?
A Yes, unless the municipal clerk attempts to return it to the voter to be 
corrected. Both the witness’ printed name and signature are required. 
These steps should be documented on the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 135
Step 6: Additional Review for Military & Overseas Voters5
Step 5 is only required for the EL-122M envelope for military and overseas voters. 
Temporary overseas voters will also use the EL-122M.
There are 3 Different Formats for the EL-122M
The first is the Brown certificate envelope that looks the same as the others, 
which is used by military and overseas voters who requested their absentee 
ballot by mail or by email. The second and third are differently-formatted 
versions of the EL-122M, containing all the same required information, which are 
used by military and overseas voters who requested that their absentee ballot 
be delivered by email.
5 Chapter 7.4 of the manual provides extensive detail on military and overseas voters. This section is 
limited only to what is necessary for their absentee certificate envelopes.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 136
Figure 24: Example of the EL-122M formatting for military and overseas 
voters who request to receive their absentee ballot package by email.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 137
Figure 25: Example of the EL-122M formatting for military and 
permanent overseas voters who request to receive their 
absentee ballot package electronically through MyVote
Date of Birth
Date of birth is required for military and permanent overseas voters (not 
temporary overseas voters), and it should be entered by the voter next to their 
signature. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.22(5), 6.24(7). If the voter has omitted their date of birth, 
first contact the municipal clerk to confirm that the voter is either a military voter 
or permanent overseas voter. If they are a temporary overseas voter, the ballot 
should not be rejected for missing date of birth because that is not required 
for temporary overseas voters. If the voter is indeed a military or permanent 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 138
overseas voter, and the date of birth is missing, the ballot should be rejected 
unless the municipal clerk attempts to return it to the voter to be corrected. These 
steps should be documented on the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104).
Q What if the voter accidentally put the date of signature instead of their date 
of birth?
A It is the same situation as if they had omitted it. See the above paragraph 
for guidance.
Witness Need Not be a U.S. Citizen
Q There is no requirement that the witness for a permanent or overseas voter 
(temporary or permanent) to be a U.S. citizen.
Figure 26: Voter signature certification box from the EL-122M printed envelope.
Federal Write-in Absentee Ballot (FWAB)
Poll workers may encounter a FWAB when opening and processing absentee 
ballots. This is a federal form that will look completely different from the EL-
122M. Military and overseas voters are permitted to use this special form as a 
backup ballot in case the ballot they received from the municipal clerk is not 
returned on time. See Chapter 7.4: “Types of Absentee Voters: UOCAVA” for 
detailed information on how to review a FWAB for sufficiency.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 139
Figure 27: The top left corner of each page of the FWAB will have one 
of the two images below and will clearly label the form as a FWAB.
Step 6: Additional Review for Voters Using Special Voting Deputies (SVD)6
Step 6 is only required for the EL-122SVD envelope for absentee voters who cast their 
ballots with the aid of Special Voting Deputies (SVD). The EL-122SVD is used by SVDs 
conducting absentee voting in residential care facilities and retirement homes.
Voter’s Assistant
If a voter using SVD voting has used an assistant, the assistant must be either 
one of the SVDs or a relative of the voter. Wis. Stat. § 6.875(6)(c)1.
Signatures & Addresses of SVDs Required
In step 3 of this envelope, the signatures and addresses of both SVDs are 
required in the witness section. The individuals who complete Step 3 of this 
envelope must be SVDs because SVDs are the only people who are authorized 
to witness ballots under this voting procedure. Id.
Q If the SVDs provided the clerk’s office address, instead of their own 
residential address, is that acceptable?
A Yes.
6 Chapter 7.3 of the manual provides extensive detail on absentee voting through Special Voting 
Deputies. This section is limited only to what is necessary for their absentee certificate envelopes.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 140
Q If the certificate envelope is missing information in Step 3, should the ballot 
be rejected?
A Both the SVDs printed names and address are required. If any of 
those required fields are missing, the poll workers will have to decide 
whether the certificate is insufficient, and if it is insufficient, reject the 
ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b) . These steps should be documented on 
the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104).
Figure 28: SVD Witness Signature Box of the EL-122SVD certificate envelope.
Step 7: Voters Not Served by SVDs
Step 7 is only required if poll workers encounter EL-122SP (Special), which should 
be uncommon.
This certificate envelope is used by voters who meet the following conditions:
 Ɋ They reside in SVD-eligible care facilities and retirement homes not served 
by SVDs.
 Ɋ They applied for an absentee ballot but not as an indefinitely confined voter.
 Ɋ They do not have an acceptable photo ID on file and did not include a 
copy of their photo ID with their absentee ballot request.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 141
This certificate envelope may appear as a regular envelope with the EL-122SP 
taped or glued on, and that is acceptable.
No Clerk Initials Needed
The clerk does not need to initial the top of the EL-122SP to indicate that the voter 
is either exempt from or has provided sufficient photo ID.
Care Facility Signature
The EL-122SP has an additional Step Four where the authorized representative of 
the care facility signs this section. If the care facility representative completes 
Step Four, the voter is exempt from providing a copy of their photo ID with their 
absentee ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(4)(b)5.
Q What if the Name of Facility is missing from Step Four? Should the ballot 
be rejected?
A Only if it does not otherwise appear on the EL-122SP. Check first to see 
if the name of the facility was included as part of the voter’s address 
in Step 1 of the EL-122SP. If it was, the ballot should not be rejected. If it is 
missing entirely, however, it should be rejected unless the municipal clerk 
attempts to return it to the voter to be corrected. These steps should be 
documented on the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104).
Q What if Step 4 isn’t completed at all?
A If Step Four is not completed, but the voter has included a copy of their 
photo ID in the certificate envelope with their ballot, the ballot should not 
be rejected.
Figure 29: Step Four of the EL-122SP (Special) certificate envelope
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 142
Sec. 14.15.13: Absentee Voters 
at the Polling Place
Poll workers should follow these procedures if a voter with an absentee 
designation in the poll book comes to the polling place to vote on Election Day.
Absentee voters may appear at the polling place 
for legitimate, legal reasons, including:
 Ɋ To vote in person instead of returning their absentee ballot
 Ɋ To lawfully return a voted absentee ballot either for themselves or as a 
lawful assistant to a voter with a disability
 Ɋ To correct an insufficiency on a certificate envelope
Appearing at a polling place does not mean that the voter is no longer eligible
