to be an absentee voter. See Wis. Stat. § 6.85(1). The commission voted to agree 
with this interpretation, see complaint.7
Voter Wants to Vote In Person
Voters are permitted to vote in-person on Election Day if they have not 
returned their absentee ballot (including by placing it in the mail or drop box). 
Wis. Stat. § 6.86(6). To find out if a voter has already returned their ballot, poll 
workers can consult the poll book (which may have an “Absentee Returned” 
watermark), or the Absentee Ballot Log (EL-124).
Q The poll book has an “Absentee Issued” watermark. Can the voter still vote 
in person?
A Only if they have not returned the ballot (by any method). Poll workers 
should ask:
Q Have you mailed or personally delivered your absentee ballot to the 
clerk’s office?
A If YES – Voter cannot vote in person. They have already voted. This is true 
even if the voter says they don’t think their ballot is going to make it to their 
clerk on time.
A If NO – Voter can still vote in person. Any voter can choose not to return 
their absentee ballot and still vote in person on Election Day.
7 See also EL 24-34, Yunker v. Luedtke (2025), pgs. 6-7, on appeal to Waukesha County Circuit Court 
(2025CV001277). Available at: elections.wi.gov/resources/complaints/el-24-23-yunker-v-luedtke.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 143
Q The voter says they returned their absentee ballot, but the municipal clerk 
said they have no record of receiving it. It may have gotten lost in the mail. 
Can the voter still vote in person?
A Unfortunately, no. If the voter has placed their absentee ballot in the mail 
or drop box, or returned it in person, the option of voting in person at the 
polls is closed to them for that ballot, even if the ballot is never received by 
the clerk. Wis. Stat. § 6.86(6).
Q What if the voter says they didn’t return their absentee ballot, they vote in 
person, and then an absentee ballot later arrives for that voter?
A The absentee ballot would be rejected, and the municipal clerk should 
be notified immediately.  The municipal clerk will gather information 
and then determine whether the voter should be referred to the 
county’s District Attorney for violating the law against double voting. 
Wis. Stat. §§ 7.15(1)(g), 12.13(1)(e).
Q The voter has brought their blank absentee ballot with them but says they 
want to vote in person. What happens to the absentee ballot?
A The voter can vote in person, but they should be issued an in-person 
ballot and should not vote the absentee ballot. The voter has brought a 
live ballot into the polling place, so careful steps should be taken to ensure 
that only one ballot can be voted and counted.
A First: Confirm with the voter that they want to vote in person. Inform them 
that once they decide to vote in person, they cannot change their mind 
and vote the absentee ballot.
A Second: Before issuing the in-person ballot to the voter, the poll workers 
should direct the voter to tear the unvoted ballot and certificate envelope 
in half, then surrender the envelope and ballot pieces to the poll worker 
for retention. Additional complications could arise if poll workers take 
possession of the unvoted absentee ballot before the voter spoils it, even 
if it’s blank. See Wis. Stat. §§ 6.86(5), 6.86(6). The poll workers should retain 
the pieces together in the spoiled ballots envelope and clearly document 
the incident on the Inspector’s Statement.
A Third: Document the occurrence thoroughly on the Inspector’s 
Statement (EL-104).
A Fourth: Permit the voter to cast an in-person ballot like normal.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 144
Voter Wants to Return their Absentee Ballot
A voter may lawfully return his or her voted absentee ballot in person to their 
polling place before 8 p.m. on Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(6). The Commission 
voted to agree with this interpretation, see complaint. 8
If the absentee voter returns the ballot to the polling place, the poll workers 
should indicate that the absentee ballot was hand-delivered on the Inspectors’ 
Statement (EL-104) and Absentee Ballot Log (EL-124).
Q Can the voter bring their absentee ballot materials and complete and vote 
the absentee ballot at the polling place?
A Yes, though they cannot also vote in person. This would be the equivalent 
of the voter returning their voted absentee ballot directly to the polling 
place. The voter should not expect poll workers to serve as their witness, 
though they may do so on their discretion. After the voter has completed 
their ballot and sealed it within the certificate envelope, poll workers 
should process it as if the voter had returned it to the polling place.
Lawful Third-Party Return of Absentee Ballots
In addition to the voter themselves, two types of third-parties are permitted 
to return absentee ballots to the polling place on behalf of a voter:
1. Agents for hospitalized voters; and
2. Assistants aiding voters with disabilities. No other third party is permitted 
under law to return an absentee ballot on behalf of another voter.
Agents for Hospitalized Voters. An agent may return a completed absentee 
ballot on behalf of a hospitalized voter by following the procedures required in 
Wis. Stat. § 6.86(3)(a). See Sec. 7.2.3: “Hospitalized Voters” for more information. 
Absentee ballots returned by this method should be processed like any other 
absentee ballot, but a notation should be made on the Inspector’s Statement 
(EL-104) that a hospitalized voter returned a ballot by agent.
Assistants Aiding Voters with Disabilities
Pursuant to the Voting Rights Act, any Wisconsin voter who requires assistance 
with mailing or delivering their absentee ballot to the municipal clerk because 
of a disability must be permitted to receive such assistance by a person of the 
8 See also EL 24-34, Yunker v. Luedtke (2025), pg. 5, on appeal to Waukesha County Circuit Court 
(2025CV001277). Available at: elections.wi.gov/resources/complaints/el-24-23-yunker-v-luedtke.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 145
voter’s choice, other than the voter’s employer or agent of that employer or 
officer or agent of the voter’s union. Carey v. Wis. Elections Comm’n, 624 F. Supp. 
3d 1020 (W.D. WI 2022).
When an election official is presented with an absentee ballot 
delivered in-person, the official may ask the following questions9:
Q Are you the voter?
A If the response is yes, the ballot may be accepted.
A If not, then:
Q Are you delivering the voter’s ballot because the voter has determined that 
they require assistance returning their ballot due to their disability?
A If the response is no, then they are not permitted to return the ballot.
A If yes, then:
Q Are you someone other than the voter’s employer, an agent of that 
employer, or an officer or agent of the voter’s union?
A If the response is yes, then the ballot can be accepted
A If no, they are not permitted to return the ballot
Voter is Correcting an Insufficiency on a Certificate Envelope
For more information and detail on correcting insufficient certificate envelopes, 
See Chapter 14.18: “Correcting Defective Certificate Envelopes” on page 161.
Sec. 14.15.14: Rejecting Absentee Ballots
Note: Ballots should only be rejected after 8 p.m. on Election Day.
Until then, any ballot that may need to be rejected should be set aside.
9 These questions were issued by the Commission as part of guidance issued on September 7, 2022. 
Available at: elections.wi.gov/memo/guidance-absentee-ballot-return-options-under-federal-voting-
rights-act .
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 146
The municipal clerk may prepare a mailer or bundle of voted absentee ballots 
that should be set aside for rejection because the clerk has determined one of the 
grounds to reject applies. The reasons why each ballot should be rejected should 
be recorded on the Absentee Ballot Log (EL-124). If poll workers are unsure why a 
ballot has been set aside to be rejected by the clerk, they should contact the clerk.
Poll workers should be sure they understand their municipal clerk’s policies 
for attempting to return sealed absentee certificate envelopes to voters 
before rejecting any ballots. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9). See Chapter 14.18: “Correcting 
Defective Certificate Envelopes” on page 161.
Best Practice: Rejecting absentee ballots should be uncommon. If 
poll workers perceive that they are needing to reject large numbers of 
absentee ballots, contact the municipal clerk so he or she is aware and 
can help troubleshoot any potential issues.
Grounds for Rejecting Absentee Ballots
Rejecting absentee ballots is required by law in certain scenarios, 
each of which is discussed in further detail in this section:
 Ɋ Insufficient or missing witness certificate envelope. This is the most com-
mon reason an absentee ballot must be rejected. The determination of cer-
tificate insufficiency is made by the municipal clerk, though the Commis-
sion has provided guidance on certain types of certificate insufficiencies in 
See Sec. 14.15.12: “Absentee Certificate Envelopes” on page 127.
 Ɋ Voter is not qualified/eligible. The absentee ballot should only be outright 
rejected on voter qualification or eligibility grounds when the municipal clerk 
can confirm the voter is no longer eligible to vote. If their voter registration is 
inactive, and they did not appear in the poll book, the absentee ballot should 
be rejected outright. If there are substantive questions about the voter’s 
eligibility, and their registration is still active when the absentee is processed, 
the voter should be challenged, not rejected. See Chapter 14.7: “Challenging 
Electors on Election Day” on page 43.
 Ɋ Certificate envelope has been opened or resealed. See Sec. 14.15.12: 
“Absentee Certificate Envelopes” on page 127.
 Ɋ Voter returned more than one ballot in the certificate envelope. See Sec. 
14.15.12: “Absentee Certificate Envelopes” on page 127.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 147
 Ɋ Superseding ballot received.  This can happen either because the voter 
cast a ballot in person and an absentee ballot was later received, or 
when A/B ballots are used. For more information about A/B Ballots, see 
Chapter 5.1: “Ballot Creation Procedures.”
Process for Rejecting Absentee Ballots
The following procedures must be followed when 
rejecting absentee ballots. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b).
 Ɋ Do not issue a voter number or check the voter into the poll book. Their 
participation will be recorded and resolved during post-election reconciliation.
 Ɋ Do not open the envelope containing the ballot
 Ɋ Mark the envelope “Rejected ballot #_” (beginning with the number 1). 
Write the reason for rejection on the envelope.
 Ɋ List the voter’s name, identifying number of the rejected ballot (Rejected Ballot 
#1, etc.), and the reason for rejection on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104).
 Ɋ Record the reason for rejection next to the voter’s name on the Absentee 
Ballot Log, and record the ballot as not counted (EL-124).
 Ɋ Place each rejected certificate envelope in the Certificate of Rejected 
Absentee Ballots (EL-102) brown carrier envelope.
End of Night Procedure for Rejected Absentee Ballots
After the polls have closed and all rejected absentee ballots have 
been processed, poll workers complete the endorsement section of 
the Certificate of Rejected Absentee Ballots (EL-102) brown carrier 
envelope. The EL-102 must be signed by the Chief Inspector and one 
of the poll workers representing each of the 2 major political parties. 
Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b). Best Practice: If there are not poll workers from 
each of the major parties, the EL-102 should be completed by at least 2 
unaffiliated poll workers.
The EL-102 is then returned to the municipal clerk in the same manner as the 
official ballots voted at the election. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 148

                                         
 
Election Inspectors 
• Ballots of absentee voters which are rejected pursuant to the provisions of §6.88, Wis. Stats., shall be 
kept in their certificate envelopes and returned enclosed in this carrier envelope to the municipal clerk. 
• These ballots may not be counted, and the names of voters may not be entered on the poll list or 
assigned a number on the registration list. 
• Each certificate envelope should be numbered and the reason for rejection listed on the back of the 
envelope and on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104). 
• This carrier envelope will be returned by the inspectors to the municipal clerk after the polls close.  
§6.88(3)(b), Wis. Stats. 
 
Municipal and County Clerks 
• The municipal clerk is responsible for delivery of this carrier envelope to the county clerk by 4 p.m. on the 
day following the election if a county, state or federal election is held in conjunction with the election for 
which these ballots were cast.  If there is no county, state or federal election held in conjunction with this 
election, this envelope shall be kept by the municipal clerk or returned to the clerk responsible for 
administering the election for which the absentee ballots were cast.  §7.51(3)(d), (5), Wis. Stats. 
• In the event of a recount, the county clerk or appropriate municipal clerk will return this carrier envelope 
to the board of canvassers responsible for conducting the recount.  §9.01, Wis. Stats.

Chapter 14.16: Processing 
Absentee Ballots: “Central 
Count”
Sec. 14.16.1: Observation 
at “Central Count”
Election observers are allowed to attend the meeting of the board of absentee 
ballot canvassers at the “central count” location to observe the processing 
of absentee ballots. Wis. Stat. §§ 7.52(1)(a), 7.41(1). They have the same rights of 
access as they would at a polling place.
The entirety of the Commission’s EL Chapter 4 (Election 
Observers) applies to observation at “central count.” 
Important differences for “Central Count” observation:
 Ɋ Observers must be permitted to observe no later than when the election 
equipment is zeroed out. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(3)(a).
 Ɋ Observers can create or transmit photos, videos, or audio recordings since 
voters are not present, but if a voter arrives to return an absentee ballot 
or correct a certificate envelope, recording and photos are not permitted. 
Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.05(3)(b).
For extensive detail on the rights and conduct of election observers, Chapter 
14.5: “Election Observers” on page 13.
Sec. 14.16.2:  Voters at “Central Count”
Voters cannot cast ballots at “central count” locations, but they, or their lawful 
assistants, can return their absentee ballots directly to these locations by 8:00 
p.m. on Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(4)(b)1.1
1 “The envelope shall be mailed by the voter, or delivered in person, to the municipal clerk issuing 
the ballot or ballots.” The Commission has long-interpreted this provision to mean that voters are 
permitted to return their absentee ballots directly to their polling places or “central count” locations. 
This instruction has appeared in the Commission’s Uniform Instructions for Absentee Voting since at 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 152
Sec. 14.16.3:  Processing Absentee 
Ballots at “Central Count”
The actual work of processing the absentee ballots at the “central count” 
location is very similar to how absentee ballots are processed at the polls:
Step 1: Certificate Envelopes Reviewed for Sufficiency.
Poll workers review certificate envelopes just like they would at the polling place. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.52(3)(a).
For reviewing absentee certificate envelopes, Sec. 14.18.1: “Reviewing the 
Certificate Envelope” on page 161.
For guidance on how the voter or witness can complete missing information, 
Sec. 14.18.2: “Returning the Ballot to the Voter” on page 162.
For guidance on rejecting insufficient absentee ballots, Sec. 14.18.3: “Setting the 
Ballot Aside for Rejection” on page 165.
Step 2: Check Ineligible Voter List(s).
Poll workers also ensure that each absentee voter is a qualified voter and 
is not ineligible due to a felony conviction or adjudication of incompetency. 
Wis. Stat. §§ 7.52(3)(a), 7.52(6).
For additional guidance, Sec. 14.15.7: “Step 3: Check Ineligible Voter List(s)” on 
page 121.
Step 3: Absentee Voters are Checked into the Poll Book.
Whether a municipality is using e-pollbooks (Badger Books) or paper pollbooks, 
poll workers read each absent voter’s name out loud, and they are assigned a 
voter number in the pollbook. Wis. Stat. § 7.52(3)(a). It is not necessary to read 
the absentee voter’s address out loud, but poll workers may do so if they wish, 
if they take care not to read out loud the addresses of confidential or protected 
voters. Wis. Stat. § 7.52(3)(a) (requiring the announcement of the absentee 
voter’s name but not address).
For more detail on checking voters into the poll bookChapter 14.9: “Voting 
Procedure” on page 65.
least 2020.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 153
Step 4: Open Absentee Envelopes and 
Review & Examine Ballots.
Poll workers carefully open the certificate envelopes, retrieve the ballots, and 
inspect them for creases or damage. Wis. Stat. § 7.52(3)(a). If the ballots are so 
defective that the poll workers cannot determine with reasonable certainty for 
whom it was cast, they shall not count the ballot. Wis. Stat. § 7.52(4)(b).
If a ballot needs to be remade, see “Procedure for Remaking Ballots” on page 213.
Important: At “central count” locations, poll workers must record 
the voter’s absentee voter number from the poll book on the back of 
the voter’s absentee ballot before tabulating it. Wis. Stat. § 7.52(3)(a). 
The Legislature considered amending this provision during the 2025-
2026 session, but no bill or amendment was passed. Accordingly, 
poll workers must continue recording absentee voter numbers on the 
backs of ballots until and unless the law changes.
Step 5: Reject Absentee Ballots if Necessary.
The board of absentee ballot canvassers may reject absentee ballots in the 
same manner as they can be rejected at the polls. Wis. Stat. § 7.52(3)(b).
For more detail, see Sec. 14.15.14: “Rejecting Absentee Ballots” on page 146.
Step 6: Tabulate Ballots.
Many municipalities that use “central count” locations have also purchased 
high-speed tabulators that can process many more ballots per hour than 
the ones at polling places. Other municipalities use the same type of voting 
equipment that they use at their polling places.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 154
Note: The “central count” statute describes a ballot tabulation process 
that has been made obsolete by electronic voting equipment. Under 
the literal language of the law, of which the relevant portions were 
written in 2005, poll workers do not unfold the absentee ballots and 
instead deposit them, folded, into the “proper ballot box.” Wis. Stat. § 
7.52(3)(a). Later, the law directs poll workers to sift through the folded 
ballots in the box and see if two or more have been folded together, 
which could be evidence that a voter returned more than one ballot. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.52(4)(a). Nowadays, electronic tabulators require 
ballots to be flattened to be scanned, and traditional ballot boxes are 
increasingly uncommon. Poll workers will notice whether a voter has 
attempted to return a second ballot when they remove the ballot from 
the certificate envelope.
Sec. 14.16.4: End of Night Procedures
Just like the processing of absentee ballots, the end of night procedures at 
“central count” locations are very similar to those at polling places.
The board of absentee ballot canvassers replaces the local board of canvassers 
for absentee ballots counted at “central count” locations. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(1).
See “Closing the Polls, LBOC, & End of Night Process” on page 219 for more 
details on End of Night Procedures.
Complete Election Day Paperwork
Poll workers must maintain, in duplicate, a log that tracks the number of ballots 
that were set aside, defective, or challenged
The reasons each ballot falls under one of those categories. The board of 
absentee ballot canvassers certifies that this record is correct, signs it, and 
attaches it to the tally sheet.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 155
Organize & Seal Election Paperwork & Records
Ballots, used certificate envelopes, and other election materials should be 
secured and packaged by following the end of night procedures described in 
Chapter 14.24: “Closing the Polls, LBOC, & End of Night Process” on page 219, 
along with any additional instructions from the municipal clerk. Wis. Stat. §§ 
7.52(4)(g), (h), (i).
Sec. 14.16.5: Challenging Absentee 
Voters at “Central Count”
The ballots of absentee voters in places using “central count” locations can be 
challenged in the same manner as they could be at polling places. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.52(5)(a).
For more information, see Chapter 14.7: “Challenging Electors on Election Day” 
on page 43.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 156
Chapter 14.17: Witness 
Address Requirements
 Ɋ All certificate envelopes for returning absentee ballots require the witness, 
or for SVD voting, witnesses, to provide an address, and the ballot may not 
be counted if the address is missing entirely. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.87(2) and (6d) 
(requiring but not defining a witness address).
 Ɋ Following litigation, a 2024 Wisconsin Court of Appeals decision, Rise, 
Inc. v. Elections Comm’n , provided a statewide definition of a witness 
address. Below, the Commission’s guidance applies this definition and 
addresses several common questions.
 Ɋ If a municipal clerk, or poll worker, believes a witness address on a 
certificate envelope is not sufficient, see Chapter 7.12: “Witness Address 
Requirements” for more information on correcting insufficient certificate 
envelopes or see Sec. 14.15.14: “Rejecting Absentee Ballots” on page 146 
for more information on rejecting absentee ballots.
Sec. 14.17.1: Frequently Asked Questions
Q Can an election official modify or add information to absentee ballot 
witness certifications?
A No. Election officials cannot modify or add information to absentee ballot 
witness certifications.1
Q What does “address” mean as used in Wis. Stat. §§ 6.87(2) and (6d)?
A It means “a place where the witness may be communicated with.”2
1 Rise, Inc. v. Elections Comm’n, 2024 WI App 48, ¶ 11, 413 Wis. 2d 366, 377, 11 N.W.3d 241, 246 (quoting 
White v. WEC, Waukesha Cnty. No. 2022CV1008 (order issued Oct. 3, 2022)).
2 Rise, Inc. v. Elections Comm’n, 2024 WI App 48, ¶ 3, 413 Wis. 2d 366, 372, 11 N.W.3d 241, 244.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 157
Q What information is required for a sufficient witness address?
A No particular components or information are required, but an 
absentee ballot cannot be rejected under Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b) 
or Wis. Stat. § 7.52(3)(b), or returned to a voter for correction under 
Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9), as long as “the face of the certificate contains sufficient 
information to enable a municipal clerk to reasonably identify a place 
where the witness may be communicated with.”3 This does not prevent 
returning an absentee ballot to a voter or rejecting an absentee ballot 
after 8 p.m. on Election Day if this standard cannot be met or if there is 
a separate issue, such as a missing witness signature or missing voter 
signature.
The Commission has determined that it is an abuse of discretion for 
a municipal clerk to apply this standard to reject absentee ballots if a 
witness address is missing the state and ZIP code and there is more than 
one municipality of the name given in the United States.4
Q What information is enough for the municipal clerk to reasonably identify 
a location where the witness can be communicated with?
A “As long as the address information provided by the witness is sufficient 
to enable communication with the witness, the purposes of the address 
requirement are fulfilled” and “specific address components are not 
necessary”5 A municipal clerk may reasonably rely upon the clerk’s “own 
knowledge of community places, building names, and local addresses” 
to determine if the envelope contains enough information to identify a 
location where the witness may be communicated with.6
3Rise, Inc. v. Elections Comm’n, 2024 WI App 48, ¶ 59, 413 Wis. 2d 366, 408, 11 N.W.3d 241, 261.
4 See 2026-0002, In re City of Mequon (Decided April 30, 2026). Available at: elections.wi.gov/event/
commission-meeting-april-30-2026 .
5 Id.  ¶ 56–57 (finding that the purposes of a witness address are 1. “to prevent abuses like fraud and 
undo influence” and 2. “to streamline and simplify the absentee voting process”).
6 Id.  ¶ 64–65.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 158
Q Where on a certificate envelope can a clerk look for a witness address?
A A clerk may look for witness address information “on the face of the 
certificate,” rather than just the information on the “address” line.7 If a 
witness writes “same as the voter” or “ditto” on the witness address line, 
“that is sufficient information to enable the municipal clerk to identify a 
place where the witness can be communicated with, so long as sufficient 
address information is provided in the voter’s certificate.”8
Sec. 14.17.2: Best Practices for 
Acceptable Witness Addresses
 Ɋ The Commission believes that the four scenarios below would provide a 
municipal clerk with a place where the witness can be communicated 
with and that a clerk need not examine an address further if:
 Ɋ The witness’s street number, street name, and municipality are present, 
regardless of whether a state name or a ZIP code are provided;
 Ɋ The witness’s street number, street name, and ZIP code are present, but 
there is neither a municipality nor a state name provided;
 Ɋ The witness’s street number and street name are present and match 
the street number and street name of the voter, but no other address 
information is provided; or
 Ɋ The witness certification indicates that the witness address is the same 
as the voter’s address with any or any combination of the following words: 
“same,” “same address,” “same as voter,” “same as above,” “see above,” 
“ditto,” or by using quotation marks and/or an arrow or line pointing to or 
from the voter address.
7 Id.  ¶ 65.
8 Id.  ¶ 65 note 23 and ¶ 66.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 159
Q If one of these four scenarios is not present, should the absentee ballot be 
rejected?
A Not on that basis. If an election official determines that one of the four 
scenarios above is present, the Commission believes that the witness has 
provided a sufficient address, and no further determination is needed. 
If one of these scenarios is not present, an election official must apply 
the standard in Answer 3, which is to look to the face of the certificate for 
information that would allow the municipal clerk to reasonably identify a 
place where the witness may be communicated with. If the information 
can be found on the face of the certificate, it is sufficient.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 160
Chapter 14.18: Correcting 
Defective Certificate 
Envelopes
Note: This section of the manual covers a clerk’s ability to return 
absentee ballots to voters under Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9), or to set the 
absentee ballot aside for rejection under Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b) when 
there are defects on the certificate envelope. For information on a 
municipal clerk’s ability to issue a new absentee ballot to a voter under 
Wis. Stat. § 6.86(5) when the ballot is spoiled or damaged, see Chapter 
7.14 “Spoiling and Replacement Ballots.” Be advised that these two 
sections of the manual are not interchangeable.
Sec. 14.18.1: Reviewing the 
Certificate Envelope
Municipal clerks should make every effort to review absentee certificate 
envelopes as they receive voted absentee ballots from voters so that they 
can choose to exercise their authority under Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9) or role under 
Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b) ahead of Election Day if the certificate has any defects. 
Defect, as used in this chapter, means anything that would lead the certificate 
to be rejected. Doing this review as the ballots arrive leaves the largest amount 
of time for a voter or witness to correct the defect as described below. The clerk 
should document any certificate defects on the Absentee Ballot Log (EL-124).
If the information on the absentee certificate envelope is incomplete, or if the 
certificate is missing, the clerk has two options for how to proceed:
Return the Ballot to the Voter - Time permitting, the clerk may attempt to return 
the ballot and incomplete certificate envelope to the voter for the voter to 
correct the defect; or
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 161
Set the Ballot Aside for Rejection – When the certificate cannot be corrected, 
the clerk should set is aside and inform poll workers that it is insufficient under 
Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b).
Sec. 14.18.2: Returning the 
Ballot to the Voter
If the information on the absentee certificate envelope is missing, incomplete, 
otherwise defective, or if the certificate is missing entirely, the clerk “may return the 
ballot to the voter . . . whenever time permits the voter to correct the defect and 
return the ballot” so that the municipal clerk can cause the completed ballot to be 
delivered to the proper polling place by 8 p.m. on Election Day. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9).
There is not a specific method by which a municipal clerk may “return the 
ballot” to the voter. See Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9). The Commission recommends 
that if the defect is correctable — meaning that the issue has to do with how 
the certificate was filled out rather than the qualifications of the voter or 
witness — the clerk contact the voter directly if possible, such as by phone, 
email, or another convenient method, to notify the voter and advise the voter of 
how they may correct the defect. Whenever time permits, a clerk may choose to 
return the ballot to the voter by returning it by mail, or by returning it to the voter 
in person in the clerk’s office or at the polling place on Election Day. How much 
time is enough is a matter of local discretion, but for mailing the absentee back 
to the voter less than one week is likely not enough time.
For correcting the certificate envelopes in person in the clerk’s office or alternate 
site, the Commission recommends that the clerk allow this possibility until 
the clerks must prepare ballots for delivery to the polling places or board of 
absentee ballot canvassers. See Wis. Stat. § 6.88(2). After the ballots have been 
sent to the proper polling place or board of absentee ballot canvassers, the 
Commission recommends that clerks have a notice, or a staff member on duty, 
to explain to voters where they must go to make a correction.
It is incumbent upon the voter to correct the defect and municipal clerks and 
election officials cannot correct voter or witness errors on the certificate.1 If 
necessary, a clerk may include a new certificate envelope to the voter when 
returning a ballot for a voter to correct Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9).
1 See Rise, Inc. v. Elections Comm’n , 2024 WI App 48, ¶ 11, 413 Wis. 2d 366, 377, 11 N.W.3d 241, 246 
(quoting White v. WEC, Waukesha Cnty. No. 2022CV1008 (order issued Oct. 3, 2022)).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 162
If the certificate envelope containing the ballot has been returned to the voter, 
the voter must ensure that the corrected certificate envelope is returned so it 
is delivered to the polling place no later than 8 p.m. on Election Day. Id. A voter 
may accomplish this by mailing the ballot back to the clerk or delivering the 
ballot to the clerk or at the voter’s polling place or board of absentee ballot 
canvassers meeting. If a municipal clerk returns a ballot to a voter, the clerk or 
the clerk’s provider should choose the “not returned, sent to voter for resolution” 
status for the ballot in WisVote. If a voter does not return it or place it in the 
mail with the defect corrected, the voter may still vote in person at the polls on 
Election Day, or vote in person absentee pursuant. See Wis. Stat. § 6.86(6).
Defect in Voter Information Section
If the voter is correcting the original certificate envelope (to fill in a missing 
required field, for example), the original witness does not need to be present to 
witness the voter correcting the defect under Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9).
Defect in Witness Information Section
Regarding the Witness’s Printed Name: If an absentee certificate is missing the 
witness’s printed name, it is within the clerk’s discretion how to proceed. Statute 
directs that the absentee ballot certificate language appear in “substantially” 
the form listed in Wis. Stat. § 6.87(2), which includes a field for a witness’s printed 
name, in addition to the signature. A clerk should determine whether a missing 
witness printed address renders the certification “insufficient” when determining 
if they should take action under Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b).
If the witness address or witness signature is missing, the certificate is defective 
but can be corrected. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(2), (6d) and (9). Please see Chapter 
14.17: “Witness Address Requirements” on page 157 for a description of 
what constitutes a sufficient witness address. To correct the certificate, the 
Commission believes that the voter and their original witness may go together 
to the clerk’s office or polling place for the witness to correct the defect and, if 
the voter consents and communicates that consent to the clerk, the witness 
alone may go to the clerk’s office or polling place for the witness to correct the 
defect in the witness information section.
Issuing a New Certificate Envelope
A new certificate envelope may be necessary if the voter or witness are unable 
to correct the defect on the existing envelope. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9). The certificate 
may only be replaced “if necessary” and the Commission cautions against 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 163
issuing a new certificate unless the original has been damaged and can no 
longer be used due to the potential difficulty of both the voter and the original 
witness being able to complete a new certificate envelope.
The clerk may not issue a new certificate envelope to the witness only — if a new 
certificate envelope is issued to allow a witness to correct a defect, the voter must 
also be present because the voter must complete the voter signature section. A 
poll worker at the polls may issue a new envelope, if necessary, pursuant to the 
clerk’s authority under Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9). If a new certificate envelope is issued 
and completed, the Commission recommends labeling the original defective 
certificate envelope with “void - new certificate envelope issued” but still 
maintaining the record so that it is accounted for in the event of a recount.
If the municipal clerk has provided the voter with a new envelope, the voter 
must request that their original witness re-complete the witness portion of 
the absentee envelope. See Wis. Stat. § 6.87(2). The original witness must be 
used in this case because they were the one who witnessed the voter marking, 
folding, and depositing the ballot into the envelope. A new witness cannot be 
used because there is no way for a new witness to witness the voter marking 
their ballot if a voter is attempting to correct a defect of the certificate envelope 
under Wis. Stat. § 6.87(9)
If the voter is correcting a defect and the clerk has issued a new 
certificate envelope, the voter should take the following steps:
 Ɋ Open the original certificate envelope (if present)
 Ɋ Verify their ballot
Best Practice: The voter should not remove the ballot from the polling 
place or the clerk’s office.
 Ɋ Seal the ballot in the new certificate envelope and complete the 
certifications with their original witness. Wis. Stat. § 6.87(2).
If the voter corrects a defect of the certificate envelope, they may only correct 
the defect — they may not vote a new ballot. See Wis. Stat. §§ 6.86(5), 6.87(9).
The clerk or poll worker should document that a correction was made (and if 
the ballot was accepted on Election Day) on the Absentee Ballot Log | EL-124.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 164
Sec. 14.18.3: Setting the Ballot 
Aside for Rejection
Best Practice: If the clerk determines that there is not enough time 
for a voter to correct the defect, if the clerk has notified the voter of 
the defect but the voter has not come in to correct it and has not 
responded to the inquiry, or if the defect cannot be corrected, the 
clerk should gather all such ballots, label them as “to be rejected” and 
separate them when preparing ballots for delivery to the polling places 
or board of absentee ballot canvassers under Wis. Stat. § 6.88(2).
If there are ballots that cannot be corrected, such as for voters who died before 
an Election Day, a clerk should also make clear to the poll workers or board of 
absentee ballot canvassers which ballots can still be corrected by the voters, 
and which cannot. Keeping the different kinds of ballots separate — those 
that appear sufficient, those that appear insufficient but are correctable, and 
those that are insufficient and uncorrectable — will help the polling places and 
absentee ballot board of canvassers stay organized.
 Ɋ The clerk should package these ballots together and make clear to the 
poll workers who will be responsible for processing the ballots at the 
polling places under Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3) or the board of absentee ballot 
canvassers under Wis. Stat. § 7.52(3) which ballots the clerk believes 
could still be corrected and which ballots the clerk believes could not 
be corrected due to the voter or witness being unqualified. Though the 
poll workers or canvassers will need to make their own determinations 
regarding the sufficiency of the absentee ballot and are responsible 
for officially rejecting defective envelopes, knowing which ballots have 
certificate envelope defects right away will greatly simplify their Election 
Day processes, and better allow them to facilitate correction by voters or 
witnesses during before 8 p.m.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 165
Chapter 14.19: How to 
Determine Voter Intent
When a voter has marked a ballot in a way that does not clearly indicate his 
or her voting objective and the standards laid out in Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2) do not 
apply, the poll workers must attempt to determine the voter’s intention. Roth 
v. LaFarge School District Board of Canvassers, 2004 WI 6 , 268 Wis. 2d 
335, 677 N.W.2d 599 , 02-0542 .
Determining a voter’s intent is necessary whenever poll workers need to 
remake a ballot.  See “Overvoted & Crossover Voted Ballots” on page 211 for 
more information about remaking a ballot.
Sec. 14.19.1: Determining Voter Intent
All poll workers should be part of the determination process, and the majority must 
agree that the voter’s intention can or cannot be determined. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(b). 
Even though tabulators may be used to assist in counting, the decision on how to 
treat a questionable ballot is made by the poll workers.
When a voter’s intent can be determined despite the voter failing to fully comply 
with the proper procedures, the vote must be counted. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2). 
This may be due to the voter applying a nonstandard mark to the ballot by a 
candidate’s name. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(c). If there appears to be an erasure mark 
next to one candidate’s name and the voter has marked one or more other 
names, the name with the erasure mark cannot be counted if it would result in 
an overvote. Id.
One common example of when a determination of voter intent and the validity 
of a ballot must be made is when a voter has overvoted (cast more votes than 
allowed) an office on the ballot. If a majority of the poll workers agree that the 
voter’s intention can be determined, the vote for that office is counted as the 
majority decided. If a majority of the poll workers agree that a determination 
of intent cannot be made and the office has truly been overvoted, the ballot is 
treated as an overvote for that office only. See Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)1. A record 
is made on the Poll workers’ Statement (EL104) that a vote was not counted for 
that office because of an overvote. Id. All other offices on that ballot must be 
counted if voter intent can be determined. Id.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 166
Sec. 14.19.2: Examples
Here are some examples of common marks election officials may 
see, along with the determination of how to record the vote:
Figure 31: Crossing out name/writing “this one”
Vote counted for Glen Flora
Figure 32: Star near name
Vote counted for Crystal Lake
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 167
Figure 33: Circling name
Vote counted for Tony Wilson
Figure 34: Underlining name
Vote counted for River White
Figure 35: Arrow pointing to name
Vote counted for Stanley Haugen
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 168
Figure 36: Negative writing by name
Votes counted for Madge Sarona and Casey Spooner (not Crystal Barronett)
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 169
Chapter 14.20: Counting Votes
Sec. 14.20.1: Statutory Authority
The information in this section represents the Commission’s interpretation of 
the application of the law set out in Wis. Stat. § 7.50, to the general situations 
described. Poll workers and candidates should review the law or consult an 
attorney about any specific application of the law.
The counting of votes is always done publicly after the polls close at 8:00 p.m. 
by the poll workers. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(1). The governing body of a municipality 
may also appoint tabulators to assist poll workers in the counting of votes. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.30(3). Any person, including candidates at the election, may 
observe the counting of votes. Wis. Admin. Code §§ EL 4.05(1 )(a), 4.06.
Sec. 14.20.2: Determining Voter Intent
This is a brief overview of determining voter intent. See Chapter 14.19: “How to 
Determine Voter Intent” on page 166 for more information and examples 
when it comes to determining voter intent, including for write-in scenarios.
When a voter has marked a ballot in a way that does not clearly indicate his 
or her voting objective and the standards laid out in Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2) do not 
apply the poll workers must attempt to determine the voter’s intention. Roth 
v. LaFarge School District Board of Canvassers, 2004 WI 6, 268 Wis. 2d 335, 677 
N.W.2d 599, 02-0542.
All poll workers should be part of the determination process, and the majority must 
agree that the voter’s intention can or cannot be determined. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(b). 
Even though tabulators may be used to assist in counting, the decision on how to 
treat a questionable ballot is made by the poll workers.
When a voter’s intent can be determined despite the voter failing to fully comply 
with the proper procedures, the vote must be counted. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2). 
This may be due to the voter applying a nonstandard mark to the ballot by 
a candidate’s name. Id. If there appears to be an erasure mark next to one 
candidate’s name and the voter has marked one or more other names, the name 
with the erasure mark cannot be counted if it would result in an overvote. Id.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 170
One common example of when a determination of voter intent and the validity 
of a ballot must be made is when a voter has overvoted (cast more votes than 
allowed) an office on the ballot. If a majority of the poll workers agree that the 
voter’s intention can be determined, the vote for that office is counted as the 
majority decided. If a majority of the poll workers agree that a determination 
of intent cannot be made and the office has truly been overvoted, the ballot is 
treated as an overvote for that office only. See Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)1. A record is 
made on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL104) that a vote was not counted for that 
office because of an overvote. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)1. All other offices on that 
ballot must be counted if voter intent can be determined. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)1.
Sec. 14.20.3: Procedure for Hand-
Counting Paper Ballots
The procedure for counting paper ballots is as follows:
1. If there are multiple ballot boxes, open boxes one at a time.
2. Count the ballots in each box (without examining them) to determine the 
total number.
3. Determine if the number of ballots is equal to the number of voters.
a. Check to see that two or more ballots are not folded together.
i. If two or more ballots are folded together, set them aside until the 
count is completed.
ii. Compare the total voter count and the number of ballots including 
the ballots that were set aside. To determine the total number of 
votes, see Sec. 14.24.9: “Completing the Tally” on page 222.
iii. If a majority of poll workers agree that the ballots folded together 
were voted by the same person, the ballots are removed, marked 
with the reason for removal, set aside, and preserved. Wis. Stat. 
§ 7.51(2)(a).
b. When the number of ballots is equal to or less than the number of 
voters, proceed with counting the ballots.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 171
4. If the number of ballots is greater than the number of voters make certain 
that there is no alternative reason for the ballot overage. Consult the 
“Drawdown Considerations and Procedure” section of this chapter. 
Proceed to Step 5 once the overage is addressed. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(e).
5. Mix the ballots so that it cannot be determined in what order they were 
placed in the ballot box. See Id. Count and record the votes on two 
separate Tally Sheets (EL-105). Reconcile the tally sheets when the counting 
for each office is complete. See Id.
a. State statutes do not specify the method for counting ballots. See 
Id. (“The inspectors shall then open, count and record the number of 
votes.”). Here is the method recommended by the Commission:
i. One election official reads each ballot while a second election 
official observes, and two other election officials mark the votes 
using hash marks (/) on the Tally Sheets (EL-105).
ii. Rotate these tabulation jobs among election officials after each 
office or separate ballot-type is counted. All election officials and 
tabulators should participate in the counting of ballots (unless 
a greeter was appointed, who may not participate). High school 
students serving as poll workers may participate in the counting.
iii. When counting is complete the tally sheets are compared for 
accuracy and totals documented for each candidate.
b. Determine if any of the ballots are “Defective, Objected to, or Rejected” 
by applying the principles from the section with this title below.
6. Announce the results of the votes cast at the polling place and prepare all 
election materials for delivery to the municipal clerk. See Chapter 14.24: 
“Closing the Polls, LBOC, & End of Night Process” on page 219 Wis. Stat. 
§ 7.51(4) Do not seal the completed Tally Sheets in the ballot bag.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 172
Sec. 14.20.4: Procedure for 
Counting Optical Scan Ballots
1. Open the optical scan tabulating unit to remove any ballots from the 
auxiliary ballot box that were not entered into the tabulating unit. Enter 
them into the optical scan unit.
2. Ballots which were stored in the auxiliary ballot box because they were 
rejected when entered into the optical scan unit should be examined by 
two election officials (one representing each political party, if affiliated) 
to determine the cause for rejection. The two election officials make a 
duplicate ballot to correct the problem unless the ballot is completely 
defective. See “Overvoted & Crossover Voted Ballots” on page 211. 
The duplicate ballot is entered into the machine. The original ballot is 
preserved in the Original Ballots envelope. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(3).
3. Remove all write-in ballots from the write-in compartment (if equipment 
deflects ballots with write-in marks to the write-in compartment).
a. Tally write-in votes on duplicate Tally Sheets (EL-105).
b. Do not reenter the ballots into the optical scan unit because the rest of 
the ballot has already been counted.
4. Examine the ballots from the main bin for write-in votes that were not 
separated by the machine (There may be write-in votes for which the 
voter did not make a mark or complete the arrow).
a. Record write-in votes on the tally sheet.
b. This may require a change to the printout from the voting equipment. 
If a voter completes the arrow or fills in the oval next to a candidate’s 
name, and also writes in a candidate but fails to complete the arrow 
or fill in the oval next to the write-in blank, the machine will incorrectly 
count the vote for the candidate on the ballot. Write-in votes, even if the 
arrow/oval is not completed, should be counted instead of the vote for 
the candidate on the ballot if the write-in is a registered candidate or 
if a ballot candidate died. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(d). Therefore, the returns 
may need to be amended to reflect the correct number of votes. In this 
case, subtract a vote for the ballot candidate and add a vote for the 
write in candidate on the tape. Document the reason for any change on 
the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 173
Special Considerations Where Optical Scan 
(OS) Voting Systems are Used
Write-in Votes
A voter is not required to mark a square, arrow or oval to vote for a write-in 
candidate. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(d). Therefore, care must be taken to assure that 
eligible writein votes are counted when the voter fails to mark a square/arrow/
oval next to the writein line. It is equally important to ensure that write-in votes 
counted by an electronic tabulator are eligible to be counted.
The reading capability of optical scan voting equipment is limited. The voting 
equipment “reads” a vote based on its assessment of the marks placed on 
the ballot by the voter. The optical scan voting equipment approved by the 
Commission may not “see” a write-in vote when the square/arrow/oval has not 
been marked. The OS voting equipment cannot differentiate between registered 
and non-registered write-in candidates, nor is it able to determine whether write-
in votes are eligible to be counted. Therefore, poll workers must examine each 
ballot when canvassing the election to determine if a writein vote has been cast 
and if that vote has been counted properly or improperly. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(a).
Adjusting the Machine Tape
If a voter has marked the square/arrow/oval next to a ballot candidate and has 
also written in a name but did not mark the square/arrow/oval next to the write-
in line, the equipment will “see” only the vote marked for the ballot candidate 
and record that vote. In this case, the poll workers must adjust the machine 
tape to subtract one vote from the ballot candidate’s total and then determine 
the eligibility of the vote for the write-in candidate.
Overvoted Ballots
If an optical scan ballot has been overvoted, it must be remade by the 
election officials, and remaking any entries regarding overvoted contests will 
depend on if the voter’s intent can be determined, with the overvoted contest 
left blank if voter intent cannot be determined. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2). It may 
also be possible to override the contest if voter intent cannot be determined 
depending on the municipality.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 174
Sec. 14.20.5: Defective, Objected To, 
& Rejected Ballots
Whenever a ballot is found to be entirely defective, is objected to, or is rejected, 
the ballot must be identified with a number and set aside, and a notation must 
be made on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL104). Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(b), (d).
When there is a question on how a vote should be counted because the 
choice(s) is not clearly marked as prescribed by the instructions on the ballot, 
the decision is made by a majority of the poll workers. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(b).

First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 176
Missing Initials on the Ballot
If the ballot is missing a poll workers’ or municipal clerk’s initials, that is 
not by itself a reason to reject the ballot. “…the only reason that a missing 
endorsement would result in a ballot not being counted in the election-day 
canvass is if it is necessary to put the unendorsed ballot aside to reconcile the 
number of ballots with the number of electors who are recorded as having 
cast ballots in the election.” Gonfiantini v. Rock Cty. Bd. Of Canvassers, 2025 WI 
App 26, 416 Wis. 2d 118, 20 N.W.3d 773 , 24-1233.
Sec. 14.20.6: Drawdown 
Considerations & Procedure
If there are more ballots than there are voters recorded on the poll list, the poll 
workers may proceed with a drawdown as allowed in Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(e). This is 
the process of randomly selecting and removing a ballot so the number of ballots 
and the total number of voters shown on the poll list agree. A drawdown may be 
performed regardless of whether the polling place utilizes hand count ballots, 
optical scan ballots, or direct recording equipment (DRE) voting equipment.
Best Practices
Before proceeding with a drawdown, poll workers should eliminate 
every possibility that the number of ballots and recorded voters do not 
match for an explainable reason, such as a ballot jam or a voter not 
signing the poll list. If the discrepancy can be explained, a drawdown 
is not necessary. A drawdown should be the last resort option after all 
efforts to determine the cause of the discrepancy have failed.
 Ɋ Ensure that each voter was issued a voter number.
 Ɋ Ensure no voters were issued the same voter number.
 Ɋ The issuance of the same voter number to two or more individuals 
can explain why the number of ballots exceed the number of voters. 
Similarly, the number of ballots would be off by one if a voter signed 
the poll list but was not issued a voter number.
 Ɋ Ensure no voters were issued the wrong ballot.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 177
Best Practices
In the case of two or more reporting units, if there are more ballots than 
voters for one reporting unit and less ballots than voters for the other 
reporting unit, but the total number of ballots for both units is equal 
to or less than the number of total voters, voters were likely given the 
wrong ballot. This should be noted on the inspectors’ statement but 
does not warrant a drawdown.
 Ɋ Count the physical number of ballots to rule out a ballot jam.
 Ɋ Review the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104) for any oddities.
If any oddities explain the discrepancy, carefully document why the 
poll workers believe that there is not an excess of ballots and do not 
proceed with a drawdown.
Procedure
1. Before drawing down ballots, be certain that there is no alternative reason 
for the ballot overage.
2. Ballots cast by challenged voters should have been issued a voter 
number and are counted and included in the total number of voters. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(c), (3)(c).
3. Place all ballots face up to check for blank ballots (blank ballots are 
ballots on which no votes were cast for any office or question). Blank 
ballots are marked, set aside, but preserved. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(c).
4. If the number of ballots still exceeds the number of voters, place the ballots 
face down and proceed to check initialing. Mark, set aside, but preserve any 
ballot not bearing the initials of two poll workers, or any absentee ballot not 
bearing the initials of the municipal or a deputy clerk. Id.
5. If the number of ballots still exceeds the total number of voters 
recorded on the voter lists, separate the absentee ballots from the 
Election Day ballots. Absentee ballots will either contain only one set of 
initials, either the municipal clerk’s or a deputy clerk’s, or two initials of 
special voting deputies.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 178
6. If there are more absentee ballots than absentee voters, the absentee 
ballots are placed in the ballot box and one of the poll workers publicly 
and without examination draws by chance the number of ballots equal 
to the excess number of ballots. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(e). Mark these ballots 
as “removed due to an excess number of absentee ballots” And set aside 
and preserve all ballots removed. See Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(d).
7. If there are more non-absentee ballots than voters, those ballots are 
placed in the ballot box and one of the poll workers publicly and without 
examination draws by chance the number of ballots equal to the excess 
number of ballots. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(e). Mark these ballots as “removed 
due to an excess number of ballots.” Set aside and preserve all ballots 
removed. See Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(d).
8. When the total number of ballots and the total number of voters shown on 
the voter lists agree, return the ballots, except for those that were removed 
and set aside, to the ballot box and mix. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(e).
9. Re-open the ballot box and count the ballots one at a time. Id.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 179
Chapter 14.21: Write-In Votes
Sec. 14.21.1: General Guidelines 
for Determining Write-In Votes
When a voter casts a write-in vote in a manner inconsistent with the ballot 
instructions for casting a write-in vote, or the write-in vote is deficient to cause 
the poll workers to question the voter’s intent, the vote is described as “irregular.” 
An irregularity can be caused by failure of the voter to follow instructions, 
less-than-legible handwriting, inadequate erasures, indicating a candidate 
preference with unconventional or stray marks, etc. An irregular vote may be 
counted if the intent of the voter can be determined.
Count Write-In Votes When
 Ɋ The name of the person is misspelled, but the intent of the voter can be 
reasonably determined.
 Ɋ The name of the person is abbreviated, but the intent of the voter can be 
reasonably determined.
 Ɋ A name contains a wrong initial or an initial is omitted.
 ɉ Example: The write-in candidate is Jonathan L. Seagull. The voter writes 
in Jonathan T. Seagull or Jon Seagull.
 Ɋ Only the last name of a person is written in.
 ɉ Count the vote if:
 Ɉ The person is a registered write-in candidate and/or
 Ɉ The intent of the voter can be reasonably determined.
 Ɉ The voter did not make an X or other mark, fill in a square or oval, or 
complete an arrow next to the write-in line
 Ɋ The voter did not make an X or other mark, fill in a square or oval, or 
complete an arrow next to the write in line.
 ɉ No mark is required next to the write-in line to cast a write-in vote. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(d).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 180
 Ɋ A sticker containing the name of a registered write-in candidate and the 
office sought is placed on the face of the ballot, but not in a particular 
office space.
Best Practice: Some of the examples of write-in vote irregularities refer 
to write-in stickers. Although 2015 Act 37 removed the language in Wis. 
Stat. § 7.50 relating to affixing a write-in sticker on a ballot, the Act did 
not specifically prohibit the use of write-in stickers. If a write-in sticker 
is affixed to a ballot, the poll workers must treat it the same as any 
other write-in vote.
Do Not Count Write-In Votes When
 Ɋ A name is misspelled or abbreviated and the intent of the voter cannot be 
reasonably determined.
 Ɋ A write-in sticker is placed on the endorsement (back) side of a paper ballot.
 Ɋ A write-in sticker is found in the ballot box and is not attached to any ballot.
 Ɋ The instruction on the ballot is “Vote for 1” and the name of more than one 
person has been written in for a single office.
 ɉ This is an overvote. No votes are counted for that office.
 Ɋ The instruction on the ballot is “Vote for 1” and the name of more than one 
person has been written in for an office and a vote has also been cast for 
a ballot candidate for the same office.
 ɉ This is an overvote and no votes are counted for that office.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 181
The following four questions should be asked when determining write-in 
vote viability and in what situations votes for ballot candidates may also be 
considered. Taking time to ask these questions should help to sort out the 
confusion write-in votes can cause.
Q Can all write-in votes be counted or just those for registered  
write-in candidates?
If there are at least as many ballot candidates as there are seats to fill; 
only votes for registered write-in candidates may be counted. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(em).
Q Is (or are) the write-in candidate(s) registered?
Q Regardless of whether the write-in vote(s) can or can’t be counted, are 
there enough write-in votes to fill the seats up for election?
If there are at least as many write-in votes as there are seats to fill, votes 
for ballot candidates may not be counted. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(d).
Q How many votes is the voter entitled to cast for the office?
If the number of counted write-in votes plus the number of marked ballot 
candidates exceeds the number of votes to which the voter is entitled, the 
marked ballot candidates may not be counted. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(em).
Best Practice: In a “Vote for 1” situation, the presence of a write-in vote 
means no ballot candidate votes can be counted.
In a “Vote for not more than…” situation, the total number of write-in 
votes cast and the number of votes to which the voter is entitled will 
determine whether ballot candidate votes may be counted.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 182

Examples with Solutions

elections in the Town of Flambeau.
Answer each of the four questions listed below to determine 
whether the write-in vote will be counted:
Q Can all write-in votes be counted or just those for registered write-in 
candidates?
If there are at least as many ballot candidates as there are seats to fill; 
only votes for registered write-in candidates may be counted. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(em).
Q Is (or are) the write-in candidate(s) registered?
Q Regardless of whether the write-in vote(s) can or can’t be counted, are 
there enough write-in votes to fill the seats up for election?
If there are at least as many write-in votes as there are seats to fill, votes 
for ballot candidates may not be counted. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(d).
Q How many votes is the voter entitled to cast for the office?
If the number of counted write-in votes plus the number of marked ballot 
candidates exceeds the number of votes to which the voter is entitled, the 
marked ballot candidates may not be counted. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(em).
Then, consider the following factors that impact the final result:
 Ɋ Which votes, if any, are counted for this contest?
 Ɋ How did the voting equipment count this vote? Does the tape need to be 
adjusted?
For this election, here is the list for registered write-in candidates:
 Ɋ Supreme Court - Sheldon Murry
 Ɋ Flambeau Town Board Supervisor - Eileen Clover
 Ɋ Rusk County District 2 Supervisor - Russell Keystone
 Ɋ Ladysmith School Board - Hixon Hoard

Sec. 14.21.3: Counting Write-In 
Votes at a Nonpartisan Election
Count Write-In Votes When
 Ɋ The name of the person is misspelled, but the intent of the voter can be 
reasonably determined.
 Ɋ The name of the person is abbreviated, but the intent of the voter can be 
reasonably determined.
 Ɋ A name contains a wrong initial or an initial is omitted.
 ɉ Example: The write-in candidate is Jonathan L. Seagull. The voter writes 
in Jonathan T. Seagull or Jon Seagull.
 Ɋ Only the last name of a person is written in.
 ɉ Count the vote if:
 Ɉ The person is a registered write-in candidate and/or
 Ɉ The intent of the voter can be reasonably determined.
 Ɋ The voter did not make an X or other mark, fill in a square or oval, or 
complete an arrow next to the write-in line
 ɉ No mark is required  next to the write-in line to cast a write-in vote. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(d).
 Ɋ A sticker containing the name of a registered write-in candidate and the 
office sought is placed on the face of the ballot, but not in a particular 
office space.
 ɉ The sticker is counted for the name and office listed on the sticker.
Best Practice: Some of the examples of write-in vote irregularities refer 
to write-in stickers. Although 2015 Act 37 removed the language in Wis. 
Stat. § 7.50 relating to affixing a write-in sticker on a ballot, the Act did 
not specifically prohibit the use of write-in stickers. If a write-in sticker 
is affixed to a ballot, the poll workers must treat it the same as any 
other write-in vote.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 192
Do Not Count Write-In Votes When
 Ɋ A name is misspelled or abbreviated and the intent of the voter cannot be 
reasonably determined.
 Ɋ A write-in sticker is placed on the endorsement (back) side of a paper ballot.
 Ɋ A write-in sticker is found in the ballot box and is not attached to any ballot.
 Ɋ The instruction on the ballot is “Vote for 1” and the name of more than one 
person has been written in for a single office.
 ɉ This is an overvote. No votes are counted for that office.
 Ɋ The instruction on the ballot is “Vote for 1” and the name of more than one 
person has been written in for an office and a vote has also been cast for 
a ballot candidate for the same office.
 ɉ This is an overvote and no votes are counted for that office.
The following four questions should be asked when determining write-in 
vote viability and in what situations votes for ballot candidates may also be 
considered. Taking time to ask these questions should help to sort out the 
confusion write-in votes can cause.
Q Can all write-in votes be counted or just those for registered  
write-in candidates?
If there are at least as many ballot candidates as there are seats to fill; only 
votes for registered write-in candidates may be counted. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(em).
Q Is (or are) the write-in candidate(s) registered?
Q Regardless of whether the write-in vote(s) can or can’t be counted, are

If there are at least as many write-in votes as there are seats to fill, votes

Q How many votes is the voter entitled to cast for the office?
If the number of counted write-in votes plus the number of marked ballot

Q Can all write-in votes be counted or just those for registered write-in
