A There are five ballot candidates for this three-seat office, so only votes for 
registered write-in candidates may be counted.
Q Are the write-in candidates registered?
A All three are registered. Three write-in votes are counted.
Q Regardless of whether the write-in votes can or cannot be counted, are 
there enough write-in votes to fill the number of seats up for election?
A Yes. There are three seats to fill and three write-in votes. Therefore, votes 
for ballot candidates may not be counted.
Q How many votes is the voter entitled to cast for the office?
A Three. We have determined that three write-in votes count. (The voter has 
reached the limit of votes to which they are entitled.)
We have also determined that the votes for the ballot candidates may not 
be counted.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 197
In addition, the three counted write-in votes plus the vote for the ballot 
candidate would exceed the number of votes to which the voter is entitled.
Final Result:
Three write-in votes are counted for this contest. (Don’t forget to subtract the 
vote the equipment counted for the ballot candidate.)
Sec. 14.21.4: Counting Write-In 
Votes at a Partisan Primary
Each ballot-status party in Wisconsin holds a primary on the 2nd Tuesday 
in August of an even-numbered year. Wis. Stat. §§ 5.02(12s), 5.62. Though the 
primaries are conducted on the same day, each party’s primary is considered 
a separate election event. See Wis. Stat. § 5.62(1)(a). At a partisan primary, a 
voter may vote only in one party’s primary, and by voting in a party’s primary, 
the voter is participating in the nomination process for candidates of that party. 
Wis. Stat. §§ 6.80(2)(f), 7.50(1)(d).
If different parties appear on separate ballots, the voter marks only one 
party’s ballot, deposits it in the locked or sealed ballot box and then places 
the unused ballots in a locked or sealed discard box or container. Wis. Stat. 
§ 6.80(2)(f). When counting ballots, if two or more party ballots are folded 
together or a certificate absentee envelope contains more than one party 
ballot, examine the ballots carefully and if more than one ballot is marked, 
no ballot may be counted. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.80(2)(f), 7.50(1)(d), and see Wis. Stat. 
§§ 6.88(3)(b), 7.51(2)(a). The ballots are marked “crossover” and placed in 
the “Original Ballots” envelope. In the case of an absentee voter, the ballots 
are returned to the certificate envelope and marked “rejected crossover.” If 
one party’s ballot is marked and the other is not, count the marked ballot 
and deposit the other ballot in the discard container. Wis. Stat. §§ 6.80(2)(f), 
7.50(1)(d), and see Wis. Stat. §§ 6.88(3)(b), 7.51(2)(a).
If optical scan voting systems are used, the voter identifies their party 
preference either by voting in only one party or by marking a party preference. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.50(1)(d). Selecting the party preference will not prevent crossover 
voting, but it is a safeguard so that a voter will not lose all votes if he or she 
does crossover. Id. Selecting a party preference preserves the votes cast in 
the preferred party, and leads to votes cast in for another party simply not 
being counted. Id. If a voter does not select a party preference and votes for 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 198
candidates of more than one political party, no partisan votes are counted (if 
there are nonpartisan offices or referenda on the ballot as well, those may still 
be counted). Id.
General Rules Regarding Party Preference
1. Selecting a party preference preserves the votes cast in the preferred party 
even if the voter crosses over and votes in another party. Id.
2. Casting votes for candidates of only one party is the same as selecting a 
party preference. See Id.
3. If no party preference is selected, and the voter marks more than one 
party’s ballot, no votes will count. Id.
4. If no party preference is selected, and the voter marks only one party’s 
ballot, but on that same party ballot writes in the name of a ballot 
candidate or registered write-in candidate of another party, all votes are 
counted except the office with the write-in vote. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(1)(d), (2)(g).
5. Regardless of if a party preference is selected, the name of a ballot 
candidate or registered write-in candidate of Party X written on a Party Y 
ballot will never count. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(1)(d), (2)(g), (2)(h).
6. Within the same party, a voter may write in the name of a ballot 
candidate or registered write-in candidate for an office under a different 
office, but it is counted only if there are no ballot candidates for the 
different office or if a ballot candidate has died. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(g).
The following scenarios presume a write-in vote is eligible to be counted, either 
because it is for a registered write-in candidate, there are no ballot candidates, 
or a candidate has died.
Count Write-In Votes at a Partisan Primary When
 Ɋ The voter has selected a party preference. The voter casts votes in the 
preferred party and then marks candidates in another party:
 ɉ The votes cast in the preferred party only are counted. See Rule 1
 Ɋ The voter has selected a party preference. The voter casts votes for ballot 
candidates in the party selected and writes in, on that same party ballot, 
the name of a person who appears as a ballot candidate on a different 
party ballot:
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 199
 ɉ All votes are counted except for the office with the write-in vote. See 
Rules 1, 2, and 5
 ɉ The voter has not physically voted on more than one party’s ballot.
 ɉ The voter benefits from the party preference safety net.
 ɉ Votes for a candidate of one party may not be counted on the ballot of 
a different party.
 Ɋ The voter has selected a party preference. The voter casts votes for ballot 
candidates in the selected party and then writes in a name on another 
party’s ballot:
 ɉ The votes cast in the selected party are counted. See Rules 1 and 5
 ɉ The name written in on the other party ballot does not count.
 ɉ Although the voter has physically voted on more than one party’s 
ballot, the voter has benefited from the preferred party safety net.
 Ɋ The voter has not selected a party preference but has voted for 
candidates of only one party. The voter writes in, on that same party 
ballot, the name of a person who appears as a ballot candidate on a 
different party ballot:
 ɉ All votes are counted except for the write-in vote. See Rules 2, 4, and 5
 ɉ The voter has not physically voted on more than one party’s ballot.
 ɉ Votes for candidates of one party may not be counted on the ballot of 
a different party.
 Ɋ A candidate’s name is printed on a party ballot for one office, and the 
voter writes in that candidate’s name for a different office on the same 
party ballot:
 ɉ The vote is counted for the person for the office where the name is 
written if there are no ballot candidates for that office or if a candidate 
for that office has died. See Rule 6
 Ɋ The name of a registered write-in candidate is written in under an office 
other than the one for which the candidate registered, but in the same 
party for which the candidate registered:
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 200
 ɉ The vote counts for the write-in candidate for the office where the 
name is written if there are no ballot candidates for that office or if a 
candidate for that office has died. See Rule 6
 Ɋ The name of a registered write-in candidate is written in under the office 
for which the write-in candidate is running, but in a different party:
 ɉ The write-in vote is not counted. See Rule 6
Do Not Count Write-In Votes at a Partisan Primary When
 Ɋ The voter has selected a party preference. On the face of the ballot, in no 
party or office space, the voter writes the name, office and political party 
of a ballot candidate or registered write-in. The political party the voter 
has written is different from the preferred party the voter selected:
 ɉ The vote is not counted. Any votes cast in the preferred party are 
counted. See Rules 1 and 5
 ɉ The voter has voted on more than one party’s ballot but has benefited 
from the party preference safety net.
 Ɋ The voter has not selected a party preference. The voter casts votes for 
candidates on one political party’s ballot, and then votes for a candidate 
on another party’s ballot or writes in a name on another party’s ballot:
 ɉ No votes are counted for partisan contests. See Rule 3
 ɉ The voter has physically voted on more than one party’s ballot without 
the benefit of the party preference safety net.
 Ɋ The name of a registered write-in candidate is written in under a party 
other than the one indicated on the campaign registration statement:
 ɉ The vote is not counted. See Rule 5
 Ɋ A candidate’s name is printed on a party ballot for an office and the voter 
writes that candidate’s name under a different office on a different party 
ballot:
 ɉ The vote is not counted. See Rule 5
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 201
The 4 Principles of Counting Write-In Votes 
at a Partisan Primary with Examples
Principles
1. If one or more ballot candidates of a specific party are listed under a 
given office:
 ɉ Only votes for registered write-in candidates are eligible for counting in 
that party’s contest.
2. If one or more ballot candidates of a specific party are listed under a 
given office, but one or more are deceased:
 ɉ All write-in votes are eligible for counting in that party’s contest.
3. If there are no ballot candidates of a specific party listed under a given 
office:
 ɉ All write-in votes are eligible for counting in that party’s contest.
4. If there is at least one write-in vote cast (regardless of the eligibility of the 
write-in vote):
 ɉ No votes for ballot candidates may be considered in that party’s contest.
Using the following partisan primary ballot examples, determine how each office 
should be counted by answering the four questions that follow the example.
14.21.4 Example 1
Figure 44: Governor
Webster Siren is a registered write-in candidate.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 202
Q Can all write-in votes be counted or just those for registered write-in 
candidates? (See Principle 1)
A There is one position to be filled and three ballot candidates. Only votes 
for registered write-in candidates are eligible to be counted.
Q Is the write-in candidate registered?
A Webster Siren is a registered write-in candidate. The vote for Webster 
Siren is counted.
The voter is entitled to no more votes.
Since Webster Siren’s vote has been counted, the following question is not 
necessary, but the answer has still been provided.
Q Regardless of whether the write-in vote can or can’t be counted, are there 
enough write-in votes to fill the seats up for election? (See Principle 4)
A Yes. There is one position to be filled and one write-in vote. Ballot 
candidates may not be considered.
Q How many votes is the voter entitled to cast for the office? (Refer to the 
ballot instructions.)
A One. The instructions are “Vote for 1.” The voter has marked four 
candidates, which is three too many.
Final Result:
 Ɋ There are four votes marked for this office. The voter is only entitled to one vote.
 Ɋ Only the write-in vote for Webster Siren is counted.
 Ɋ The voting equipment has recorded this office as overvoted — no votes 
were recorded.
 Ɋ The tape is adjusted to add a write-in vote to this office in this party.
Note: If Webster Siren were not a registered write-in candidate, the 
vote for Webster Siren would not be counted. And since there is a 
write-in vote for this “Vote for 1” office, ballot candidates cannot be 
considered. So, if Webster Siren were not a registered write-in, no votes 
would be counted.
 Ɋ The reason for the adjustment is recorded on the Inspectors’ Statement 
(EL-104).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 203
14.21.4 Example 2
Figure 45: Town Board
Stinnett is a registered write in candidate.
Q Can all write-in votes be counted or just those for registered write-in 
candidates? (See Principle 1)
A There is one position to be filled and three ballot candidates. Only 
registered write-in candidates are eligible to be counted.
Q Is the write-in candidate registered?
A Yes. Stinnett Longlake is a registered write-in candidate. The vote for write-
in candidate Stinnett Longlake is counted.
The voter is not entitled to any more votes.
The following question is not necessary, but the answer has still been provided.
Q Regardless of whether the write-in vote can or can’t be counted, are there 
enough write-in votes to fill the seats up for election? (See Principle 4)
A Yes. There is one position to be filled and one write-in vote.
Q How many votes is the voter entitled to cast for the office? (See Principle 1)
A One. The instructions are “Vote for 1.” The voter has marked one candidate 
and written in a candidate.
Final Result:
 Ɋ The write-in vote for Stinnett Longlake is counted.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 204
 Ɋ The voting equipment has recorded a vote for the ballot candidate, 
Madge Trego.
 Ɋ The poll workers must adjust the tape by subtracting a vote from Madge’s total.
 Ɋ The reason for the adjustment is recorded on the Inspectors’ 
Statement (EL-104).
Write-In Vote Irregularities for the Partisan Primary
 Ɋ A voter writes in the name of a registered write-in candidate under an 
office other than the one for which the candidate is registered:
 ɉ The vote is counted for the office under which the name is written if:
 Ɉ The office under which the name is written has no ballot candidates 
or one or more ballot candidates is deceased, and;
 Ɉ The office under which the name is written is the same party in 
which the registered write-in candidate is running.
 Ɋ A name is written in the margin, rather than in an office space:
 ɉ If the person whose name is written in is a registered write-in 
candidate, the name is counted as a vote for the write-in candidate in 
the office and party in which he or she is registered.
 ɉ If the name is that of a ballot candidate, the vote is counted for the 
candidate in the office and party for which he or she is registered.
 Ɋ The voter marks the ballot for a name that is printed on the ballot and 
writes in another person’s name for the same office:
 ɉ If the write-in vote is deemed eligible, the write-in vote is counted. The 
vote for the ballot candidate is not counted. (See Principles 1 and 4)
 Ɋ The name of a registered write-in candidate for one party is written in 
under the same party, but for an office other than the one for which the 
candidate registered.
 ɉ The write-in vote counts for the person for the office where the name 
is written, if there are no ballot candidates for the office or one or more 
ballot candidates are deceased. (See Principles 2 and 3)
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 205
Sec. 14.21.5: Counting Write-In 
Votes for Governor & Lt. Governor
If a voter writes in the name of a ballot candidate or registered write-in candidate 
for lieutenant governor but does not write in a name of a candidate for governor, 
a vote is counted for the candidate for lieutenant governor. Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(i).
If a voter writes in the name of a ballot candidate or registered write-in candidate 
for governor but does not write in a name of a candidate for lieutenant governor, 
a vote is counted for the candidate for governor. Id.
However, a write-in vote for governor or lieutenant governor alone shall not be 
added to the votes of the same candidate if there are different combinations of 
governor/lieutenant governor.
For example:
 Ɋ A write-in vote for Mary Jones for governor and Samuel Smith for 
lieutenant governor may not be added to a write-in vote for Mary Jones 
for governor with a different candidate for lieutenant governor or no 
candidate for lieutenant governor.
 Ɋ A write-in vote for Mary Jones for governor and Samuel Smith for 
lieutenant governor may not be added to a write-in vote for Samuel 
Smith for lieutenant governor with a different candidate for governor or no 
candidate for governor.
 Ɋ A write-in vote for Mary Jones for governor may not be added to a write-in vote 
for Mary Jones for governor with a different candidate for lieutenant governor.
 Ɋ A write-in vote for Samuel Smith for lieutenant governor may not be 
added to a write-in vote for Samuel Smith for lieutenant governor with a 
different candidate for governor.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 206
In these scenarios, the write-in votes are listed separately on 
the tally sheet for each combination of candidates:

Write in Candidates 
(Governor / Lt. Governor)
Counted as a Vote for
Mary Jones / Samuel Smith Mary Jones / Samuel Smith
Mary Jones / Jack Frost Mary Jones / Jack Frost
Mary Jones / No Candidate Mary Jones (Governor only)
Robin Bird / Samuel Smith Robin Bird / Samuel Smith
Jay Blue / Samuel Smith Jay Blue / Samuel Smith
No Candidate / Samuel Smith Samuel Smith (Lt. Governor only)
Sec. 14.21.6: Counting Write-In Votes for 
President & Vice President of the U.S.
Registered & Non-Registered
Generally, only votes for registered presidential/vice-presidential write-in 
candidates are required to be counted. However, there is a circumstance that 
would require poll workers to count votes for non-registered write-in candidates 
for president/vice president.
Write-in votes for President/Vice-President are to be 
counted in two instances under Wis. Stat. § 8.185(1):
 Ɋ The candidate has filed with the Commission a list of presidential electors 
and a Declaration of Candidacy (a “registered write-in candidate”) as 
discussed earlier; and
 Ɋ A non-registered candidate who is written in for President receives 10% of 
the total votes cast in the ward, or the municipality if not divided into wards.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 207
To comply with Wis. Stat. § 8.185(1), poll workers must document any non-
registered write-in candidate for President/Vice-President who appears to be 
receiving a large number of votes.
Best Practice: Before beginning to count write-in votes for President/
Vice-President, the poll workers may easily determine the number 
of votes a non-registered write-in candidate would need to meet the 
10% threshold by taking the total number of votes cast in the ward and 
moving the decimal point left one digit. It will likely be obvious from 
your voting equipment tape whether the total number of scattering 
votes for President/Vice-President is even close to the 10% threshold 
and whether poll workers will need to document any votes for non-
registered write-in candidates. If the number of total scattering votes 
is not close to the 10% threshold, the focus is only required to be given 
to the registered write-in candidates, as meeting the threshold would 
be mathematically impossible.
Examples:
Total votes cast for president in the ward = 5,280. 10% of 5,280 = 528
Total votes cast for the office in the ward = 410. 10% of 410 = 41
Total votes cast for the office in the ward = 42. 10% of 42 = 5
If a write-in candidate is at or close to 10% of the votes cast in the ward for 
the office of President/Vice-President, the votes for the candidate must be 
individually tallied. However, once it becomes evident that it is mathematically 
impossible for a non-registered write-in candidate to reach the 10% threshold, 
votes for that candidate no longer need to be tallied.
Each prospective write-in candidate for the offices of President and Vice-
President is required to file a Declaration of Candidacy and a list of “electors.” 
“Electors” are individuals who will support the candidate in the Electoral College 
should they receive the highest number of votes in Wisconsin. When the 
deadline for filing these documents has passed, the Commission provides a 
list of “registered” Presidential and Vice-Presidential write-in candidates to the 
municipal clerks.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 208
Wis. Stat. § 7.50(2)(i) provides direction on how votes should be counted 
for President and Vice-President in certain circumstances:
 Ɋ A registered write-in vote for only President is counted.
 Ɋ A registered write-in vote for only Vice-President is not counted.
 Ɋ A write-in vote for President and Vice-President that combines the name 
of a registered write-in candidate with the name of a candidate on the 
ballot is not counted.

Chapter 14.22: Overvoted & 
Crossover Voted Ballots
This chapter explains how to process overvoted or crossover voted ballots 
in municipalities that do not allow an override function on the voting 
equipment and those that do. Please see the section below that describes 
the process applicable to your municipality. This information does not apply 
to municipalities that use hand count paper ballots. Hand counted paper 
ballots do not need to be overridden or remade and the canvassers will simply 
determine whether any votes on the ballot must be counted.
Sec. 14.22.1: Definitions
Overvoted ballot: A ballot with at least one contest in which the voter has voted 
for more than the maximum number of selections allowed. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2).
Crossover vote:  A ballot where the voter has not selected a party 
preference and has voted in more than one party, which is prohibited by 
state law. Wis. Stat. § § 5.81(4) and 7.50(1)(d).
Figure 46: Example: Overvoted Contest
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 211
Sec. 14.22.2: Municipalities that Do Not 
Allow Use of the Override Function
If your municipality uses electronic tabulation equipment and does not allow 
for the use of the override function in the event of an overvoted or crossover 
voted ballot, those ballots will have to be remade by poll workers at the polls on 
Election Day if there are any votes that still must be counted. Wis. Stat. §§ 5.81(4), 
5.85(2), and 7.50(1)(d).
Remaking Ballots with Overvotes
Poll workers may be required to remake ballots with overvotes. Wis. Stat. 
§ 5.85(2)(a). These are ballots with at least one instance in which the voter has 
voted for more than the maximum number of selections allowed in a contest. 
Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2).
For Election Day voters, poll workers should instruct the voter of the overvote 
error and provide them the opportunity to spoil their ballot and be issued a 
new ballot. Wis. Stat. § 6.80(2)(c). A voter may be issued up to three ballots. Id. 
If the voter does not vote a new ballot 1, or for any overvoted absentee ballot, 
two poll workers must remake the ballot so the electronic tabulator will accept 
it. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)1. On a new ballot, poll workers transfer all votes from 
the overvoted ballot in which the voter’s intent can be determined. Wis. Stat. 
§§ 5.85(2)(b)1., 7.50. No votes are transferred for the overvoted contest, unless 
voter intent can be determined. Wis. Stat. §§ 5.85(2)(b)1., 7.50. See Chapter 
14.19: “How to Determine Voter Intent” on page 166.
Remaking Ballots with Crossover Votes
At a partisan primary, those in Party B would not. If the voter fails to select 
a party preference and then votes in more than one party’s primary, the 
electronic tabulator should reject the ballot and notify the voter of a crossover 
vote if they are present. See Wis. Stat. §§ 5.81(4), 5.91(6), and 7.50(1)(d).
1 For example, the voter left the voting location before their ballot was tabulated.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 212
Procedure for Remaking Ballots
To remake a ballot, the following steps must be followed:
1. At least two poll workers (if party-affiliated inspectors are present, use one 
of each party) must participate in this process. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)1.
2. Poll workers shall announce to observers, if present, that a ballot is being 
remade and the reason for doing so. Wis. Admin. § Code EL 4.03(1)(b)3. and 
see Wis. Stat. § 5.85(3).
3. Note and select a reason for remaking the ballot in the endorsement 
section of the ballot. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)2.
Figure 47: Ballot Endorsement Section
 ɉ “Overvoted” ballots are ballots with at least one instance in which the 
voter has voted for more than the maximum number of selections 
allowed in a contest. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(a).
 ɉ “Damaged” ballots include ballots in which the voter circled names 
rather than marking the arrows or ovals, ballots with selections that 
were erased or crossed out by the voter, and ballots mutilated by postal 
equipment. See Wis. Stat. §§ 5.85(3) and 7.50(2).
 ɉ “Other” ballots include ballots emailed or faxed to a voter and ballots 
accessed and completed by a military or overseas voter through 
MyVote Wisconsin. See Wis. Stat. § 5.85(3).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 213
4. These ballots are labeled “Original Ballot # (serial number)” and 
“Duplicate Ballot # (same serial number)” in the endorsement section of 
the ballot. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)2. Remade ballots are issued a sequential 
serial number, starting with 1, regardless of the reason the ballot was 
remade. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)2. and (3). These ballots are labeled: original 
ballot number, duplicate ballot number, same serial number. Id.
5. Transfer votes based on voter intent from the original ballot to the duplicate 
ballot so the electronic tabulator will accept the ballot. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)1. 
Any votes cast correctly on the original ballot can be transferred onto the 
duplicate ballot. An overvoted contest on the original ballot is left blank 
on the duplicate ballot, unless voter intent can be determined. Wis. Stat. 
§§ 5.85(2)(b)1. and 7.50. The duplicate ballot for a crossover voted original 
ballot without party preference is a blank ballot. Nonpartisan contests, if any 
are present on the ballot, would still be counted.
6. Describe on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104) why the ballot had to be 
remade. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)1.
 ɉ Use a code of “OV” to identify overvoted ballots.
 ɉ Use a code of “DM” to identify damaged ballots.
 ɉ Use a code of “OT” to identify ballots remade for other reasons.
7. The “duplicate” ballots are initialed by the election officials in the 
endorsement area and inserted into the tabulating device. Wis. Stat. 
§ 5.85(2)(b)2. and (3).
8. The original ballots are bundled together, marked and placed in the 
“Original Ballots Envelope.” Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)2. and (3).
9. The “Original Ballots Envelope” is placed in the ballot container. Id.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 214
Sec. 14.22.3: Municipalities that 
Allow Use of the Override Function
If the voting equipment is programmed to permit the use of the override 
function to tabulate overvoted ballots and ballots with crossover votes, 
that function may be used instead of remaking ballots for which the voter’s 
intent regarding the contest(s) in question cannot be determined. Wis. Stat. 
§ 5.85(2)(b)1. See also Wis. Stat. § 7.50.
Best Practice: To avoid confusion and any complications during 
a recount, Commission staff recommend all ballots at the polling 
place be treated uniformly, either by remaking the ballot or using the 
override function, as instructed by the municipal clerk. Clerks should 
clarify this with poll workers before Election Day. When programmed to 
use the override function, the voting equipment will display a warning 
message after a voter inserts an overvoted ballot or a ballot with 
crossover votes into the machine. An poll worker should be assigned to 
monitor the tabulation equipment at all times that voters are inserting 
ballots into the machine. The poll worker monitoring the tabulator 
should position themselves so they are able to assist when needed 
without impeding voter privacy.
There are currently several different types of tabulation equipment in use in 
Wisconsin, and each piece of equipment displays a different notification when 
an overvoted or crossover voted ballot is inserted into the machine. The lack 
of standard content for notifications across different equipment creates the 
potential for voter confusion and reinforces the need for election officials to be 
familiar with the notification screens and the impact on a ballot if the override 
function is used. A poll worker should monitor the use of the equipment and be 
able to explain to the voter that votes for at least one ballot contest will not be 
counted if the voter decides to cast an overvoted or crossover voted ballot. Poll 
workers may familiarize themselves with the equipment used at their polling 
place by attending the public test for their municipality. Wis. Stat. § 5.84(1). This is 
a good opportunity to see how the tabulators react to different types of events.
Below are two separate procedures for using the override function when 
processing an overvoted ballot or a ballot with crossover votes, depending upon 
whether it is an absentee ballot or one that is being cast while the voter is present.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 215
Sec. 14.22.4: Procedure: Overvoted & 
Crossover Voted Absentee Ballots
1. The poll worker inserts the ballot without inspection into the tabulation 
equipment, and the machine displays the warning message. Wis. Stat. 
§ 5.91(7) and (12).
2. The inspector selects the option to return the ballot.
3. The inspectors review the ballot to attempt to determine the voter’s intent 
for the contest or contests in question. Wis. Stat. § 7.50. For example, a stray 
mark could have mistakenly caused the machine to identify the ballot as 
overvoted or crossover voted.
 ɉ If voter intent can be determined, the ballot is remade to correct the 
error for the contest(s) in question and include all other votes using the 
process outlined in the Election Day Manual. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(3).
 ɉ If voter intent cannot be determined, continue to Step 4.
4. The inspector reinserts the ballot into the tabulation equipment and casts 
the ballot using the override function.
5. The inspector makes a notation on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104) to 
indicate that the override function was used to cast the ballot. Wis. Stat. 
§ 5.85(2)(b)1. The reason why the override function was used should also 
be included in this notation. The ballot is not marked.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 216
Sec. 14.22.5: Procedure: Overvoted & 
Crossover Voted Ballots When Voter 
is Present at the Polling Place
1. The voter inserts the ballot into the tabulation equipment, and the 
machine displays the warning message. Wis. Stat. § 5.91(7) and (12).
2. The poll worker assigned to monitor the tabulation equipment explains the 
notification to the voter.
 ɉ Overvoted Ballot: Casting an overvoted ballot with the override function 
would result in no votes being counted for the overvoted contest(s) but 
votes for all other contests would be counted. Wis. Stat. § 5.85(2)(b)1.
 ɉ Crossover Voted Ballot: Casting a ballot that contains a crossover vote 
would result in no votes being counted for any partisan office but votes for 
any nonpartisan contests (if present) would be counted. Wis. Stat. §§ 5.81(4) 
and 7.50(1)(d).
3. The voter is informed that they can spoil the ballot and mark another 
ballot (up to three ballots) OR cast the ballot as is.
 ɉ If the voter chooses to spoil the ballot, the poll worker selects the option 
to return the ballot, returns the ballot to the voter without examining 
it, and directs the voter to spoil and exchange the ballot at the ballot-
issuing table for a replacement ballot. See Wis. Stat. § 6.80(2)(c).
 ɉ If the voter wants the ballot cast as is, the inspector should first offer 
to use the option to return the ballot to the voter so that the voter may 
review the ballot before making the final decision. If the voter does not 
wish to examine the ballot, the election official casts the ballot using 
the override function. If the voter wishes to examine the ballot first, the 
voter or inspector selects the option to return the ballot and the voter 
determines whether to spoil the ballot or to have the poll worker cast it 
using the override function.
4. The poll worker makes a notation on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104) to 
indicate that the override function was used to cast the ballot. Wis. Stat. 
§ 5.85(2)(b)1. The reason why the override function was used should also 
be included in this notation. The ballot is not marked.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 217
Chapter 14.23: Polling Place 
Accessibility Reviews
The Commission conducts reviews of polling places on Election Days to check for 
compliance with the Americans with Disabilities Act, Help America Vote Act, and 
Wis. Stat. § 5.25(4). Clerks and poll workers will not receive notice in advance of a 
review. The goal of these reviews is to document the polling place as a voter with 
a disability would on any normal Election Day. Reviewers are not observers and 
may complete their work outside of any designated observer area.
When a reviewer arrives at a polling place, they will introduce themselves to 
the Chief Inspector and provide a letter of introduction that explains the review 
program. The letter includes contact information for Commission staff in case 
the Chief Inspector has any questions or concerns. The visit should be noted 
on the Inspector’s Statement (EL-104). The reviewer will take photos and record 
measurements on a tablet. If the chief inspector is available, the reviewer will let 
them know they have completed their review before they leave.
See Part 11: “Accessibility” for more information regarding Accessibility.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 218
Chapter 14.24: Closing the Polls, 
LBOC, & End of Night Process
Chapter 14.21 covers the important steps of closing the polls, the work of the 
local board of canvassers (LBOC) and the end of night process for poll workers 
on Election Night.
Sec. 14.24.1: Closing the Polls
All polling places in Wisconsin close at 8:00 p.m. However, voters in line at the 
time the polls close must be permitted to vote. Wis. Stat. § 6.78(1m).
To close the polling place:
 Ɋ The Chief Inspector announces the closing of the polls.
 Ɋ The End of Line Officer stands behind the last voter in line at 8:00 p.m. to 
clearly identify who is entitled to vote. The End of Line Officer should be 
established by the municipal clerk prior to Election Day. The End of Line 
Officer may be a poll worker, election registration official, employee of the 
clerk, or police officer. Wis. Stat. § 7.37(13)
 Ɋ Poll workers should continue counting and processing absentee ballots 
received before 8 p.m. and should not close the polls until all absentee ballots 
are counted. Wis. Stat. § 7.52(1)(a) (permitting counting of absentees past 8 
p.m. at “central count”). Wis. Stat. § 6.78(4) (permitting in person voters to cast 
ballots past 8 p.m. if they are in line by 8 p.m.). Wis. Stat. § 6.87(6) (permitting 
municipal clerks to transport absentee ballots to polling places until 7:59 p.m.).
Do not close the polls on the tabulator before verifying that all absentee ballots 
have been tabulated.
Sec. 14.24.2: Court-Ordered 
Extension of Voting
If the poll workers are informed that a court has issued an order extending 
the hours that the polling place is open beyond 8:00 p.m., the polls cannot be 
closed until the time indicated in the order. The end of line rule applies to the 
new time. A voter entering the polling place after that time will have his or her 
ballot marked with the notation “Section 6.96.” The poll workers should then 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 219
make the same notation on the poll list next to the voter’s name. This enables 
ballots cast after the polls close pursuant to a court order to be removed from 
the count if the court order is set aside. Wis. Stat. § 6.96.
See Part 12: “Emergency Preparedness & Contingency Planning” for more 
information on seeking extensions of voting hours.
Sec. 14.24.3: What is the LBOC?
Once all voters have cast their ballots, and once the polls are officially closed, 
the poll workers become the local board of canvassers (LBOC). The LBOC 
completes the process of reconciling poll lists, counting ballots, completing the 
required forms, and transmitting unofficial results. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(1).
The doors to the polling place must NOT be locked when voting ends — all post-
election paperwork, tallying, and end of night tasks are open to the public.
Sec. 14.24.4: Tally & Return
Election officials looking to the plain language of Wis. Stat. § 7.51 might be 
confused by a statutory scheme that was written when the hand counting of 
paper ballots was the norm. The use of electronic tabulators streamlines many 
of the tally and return procedures in statute. A provision was added to the 
statue clarifying that electronic tabulators can replace some functions if the 
general process still follows the canvass of paper ballots insofar as applicable. 
Wis. Stat. § 7.51(6).
This chapter discusses the legal requirements of Wis. Stat. § 7.51 in the context 
of modern electronic tabulation systems. The chapter also clarifies end of night 
packaging of election materials to conform with best practices identified by 
Wisconsin election officials.
Sec. 14.24.5: Who Can Attend the LBOC?
The (LBOC) is a governmental body subject to open meetings law. Wis. Att’y 
Gen. Op. 5-14 .
Anyone, including a candidate, has the right to observe the end of night activities. 
Any observers who are present may remain, and they become regular members 
of the public attending a public meeting. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4 regulating the 
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 220
conduct of election observers does not apply once the LBOC begins its work. 
Attendees are permitted to transmit photographs, videos, and audio recordings, if 
they are not disruptive. Wis. Admin. Code § EL 4.06.
Best Practice: Canvassers should not permit a member of the public to 
photograph or record the entire results tape as it may have sensitive 
information like county or municipal IP addresses. Canvassers should 
not cover sensitive information with their hand or a sheet of paper. The 
better process is to direct the member of the public to submit a public 
records request for the results tape so that anything sensitive can be 
properly redacted and explanations of the redactions provided.
Sec. 14.24.6: What is the 
Purpose of the LBOC?
The purpose of any of the boards of canvassers is to “publicly canvass” the 
votes. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(1). Canvass is the process of making sure all eligible 
ballots have been counted, discrepancies are explained and documented, and 
results are determined. This entails much more than just reading the unofficial 
results from the tabulator tape and packaging everything up to go back to the 
municipal clerk. All the important work that poll workers do after the polls close 
serves the important purpose of ensuring that the unofficial results accurately 
reflect the reality of how many people voted and who they voted for.
Sec. 14.24.7: Central Counting 
Location for Unified Tabulation
Different from Absentee Ballot “Central Count”
Wisconsin law allows for the governing body to authorize the municipal clerk to 
complete the LBOC at a “central counting location”—meaning the clerk takes all 
voted ballots (not just absentees) at the end of the night to a single place to run 
them through an electronic tabulator. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(1). This is not the same thing 
as the meeting of the municipal board of absentee ballot canvassers counting 
all absentee ballots in one location (commonly known as “central count”).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 221
The Commission is not aware of any municipality in Wisconsin that uses a 
“central counting location” as a method of unified tabulation under Wis. Stat. 
§ 7.51(1) This statute likely exists from an earlier time when the use of electronic 
tabulators was more expensive and less widespread.
Sec. 14.24.8: What is the Correct 
Order of Operations?
Several of the processes outlined in this chapter can be completed at the same time 
by different poll workers. For example, some poll workers can work on reconciling the 
poll book while others review voted ballots to count qualified write-in ballots.
Sec. 14.24.9: Completing the Tally
The first task the LBOC completes is determining and confirming the total number 
of voters who were checked into the poll book, so that number can later be 
compared against the total number of ballots that the tabulator reports that it 
counted. The number checked in should match the total number of ballots cast.
What is a Tally Sheet?
The tally sheet is a required piece of Election Day documentation that states the 
total number of votes cast for each office and for each individual who receives 
votes for that office, including qualified write-ins, as well as the votes for and 
against each referendum. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(4)(a).
The Commission maintains a sample Tally Sheet (EL-105) on its website.
Before completing the Tally Sheet (EL-105), poll workers must first confirm that 
the total number of voters checked into the poll book matches the number of 
votes the tabulator recorded.
For places that use optical scan voting equipment, the results tape from the 
tabulator replaces the Tally Sheet, but poll workers will still need to use the 
paper tally sheet to record qualified write-in votes.
Step 1: Reconciling the Poll Book
The purpose of reconciling the poll book (or poll list) is to ensure both poll books 
agree, meaning they contain identical information and notations (except voter 
signatures). Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(a).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 222
Best Practice: Poll workers should complete poll book reconciliation 
throughout the day to avoid having to complete the entirety of this 
task after polls close.
Note: Jurisdictions that use e-poll books (Badger Books) do not 
complete this step.
This is an important documentation step because one copy of the poll book 
goes to the municipal clerk, one goes to the county clerk, and both need the 
same information to complete their respective canvasses.
Any differences between the poll books must be reconciled, meaning 
they must be explained, accounted for, and documented:
 Ɋ Sometimes, the issue is simple: “A poll worker forgot to write down the 
voter number in the second copy of the poll book.” That can be corrected 
and added to the second poll book during this reconciliation step.
 Ɋ If the issue cannot be reconciled after consultation with the Chief 
Inspector and the municipal clerk, the poll workers should describe the 
problem in detail on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104).
Note: Be on the lookout for voter entries that have an “Absentee 
Returned” watermark, but no absentee voter number assigned. This 
can be a sign that an absentee ballot was received but not counted. If 
poll workers discover this, all work should stop until the ballot is located 
or it can be determined why the voter does not have a voter number 
beside it.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 223
Best Practices for Reconciling the Poll Book:
Compare Voter Numbers and Notations in Main Poll Book
 Ɋ Compare the first and last voter number on each page, as well as 
every third or fifth number to ensure they match. Example: If Ms. 
Ima Voter was assigned voter number 29, that number should be 
recorded beside her name in both copies of the poll book.
 Ɋ Compare the notations (if any) alongside the name of each voter on 
both poll books to ensure they match
 Ɋ The last voter number assigned should be circled in red in both poll 
books, and recorded on the Poll List Certification (EL-107C), which 
is usually printed on the front page or cover of each poll book. This 
is an important step to help municipal and county clerks with their 
respective canvasses.
Reconcile the Supplemental Poll List
 Ɋ Be sure to perform the same careful review of the supplemental poll 
list, which includes individuals who registered to vote on Election Day 
and voters casting a presidential-only ballot.
Don’t Forget to Check the Separate Lists for Confidential & Protected Voters
 Ɋ Remember that there are separate portions of the poll book for 
confidential voters and protected voters. Be sure to reconcile these 
sections as well.
Complete Poll List Certification (EL-107C)
Once the poll books have been reconciled, poll workers should complete the Poll 
List Certification (EL-107C), which is usually printed on the front page or cover of 
each poll book. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(a). Every poll worker who completed the work 
of checking in voters should sign the certification. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(a).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 224
Step 2: Record the Total Numbers of Voters 
on Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104)
Once the poll books are reconciled and any differences explained, poll workers 
record the total numbers of voters who checked into the poll book on the 
spaces indicated on the Inspectors’ Statement. The total number of voters is the 
number of people who have a voter number next to their name on the poll list 
and supplemental poll list. This includes the total number of in-person voters, 
the number of absentee voters, and the number of provisional voters.
Step 3: Confirm Number Match between Last 
Voter Number & Tabulator Number
The last voter number issued to either an in person or absentee voter should be 
the same as the number of total ballots that the tabulator shows were counted. 
These two numbers must match, and if they don’t, additional steps are 
required. See Wis. Stat. § 7.51(2)(c).
Quick Troubleshooting if These Numbers Don’t Match:
 Ɋ Was a voter number skipped or used twice? Poll workers may have to go 
through the poll book to determine this.
 Ɋ Was a voter number given to a voter who decided not to receive or 
cast a ballot?
 Ɋ Does the number of paper ballots explain the discrepancy? Poll workers 
should count the total number of marked paper ballots to determine if a 
jam or other issue caused the mismatch. If a tabulator’s auxiliary bin was 
used during Election Day, double check that area.
If the last voter number still does not match the total votes reflected on the 
tabulator. See Chapter 14.20: “Counting Votes” on page 170 for further 
troubleshooting and possible draw down procedures.
Step 4: Examine & Count Write-In Votes
Optical scan tabulators can tell when the oval next to a write-in candidate has 
been filled in, but they cannot assign the vote total to a particular candidate. 
This means poll workers must inspect ballots by hand to tally how many votes 
qualified write-in candidates received. Determining which write-in votes are 
eligible to be counted, and which are not, can be complicated.
See Chapter 14.21: “Write-In Votes” on page 180.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 225
Sec. 14.24.10:  The LBOC Paperwork 
“Tells the Story” of Election Day
Boards of canvassers — at the school district, municipal, county, and state 
level — are all tasked with canvassing the unofficial returns that are reported 
on Election Night. They cannot do this without detailed documentation and 
organized materials, which are prepared and finalized by the poll workers 
serving as the LBOC at the end of the night. These materials are also essential 
in the event of a recount. Different materials are secured and routed to the 
municipal, county, and/or school district clerks.
Important: Understand Local Procedures
This section provides an overview of the legal requirements and best 
practices that fit most jurisdictions. Individual municipal or county 
clerks may have their own instructions for how to prepare end-of-
night documentation and how to package materials. If instructions or 
materials from the municipal clerk conflict with this section, poll workers 
should confer with the municipal clerk and defer to their preferences 
assuming they don’t conflict with state law.
Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104)
The Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104) is a record of everything of note that 
happened at the polling place during Election Day. It should be used throughout 
the day to make notes and document incidents and must be completed by poll 
workers after the Tally Sheet (EL-105). Wis. Stat. § 7.51(4)(a).
Every section of the Inspectors’ Statement must be completed before the end 
of the night, including the Incident Log. There should always be something to 
note on the Inspectors’ Statement, but if the day was truly uneventful, the box 
at the top of the Log must be checked, or poll workers should write “None.” No 
detail or incident is too small or insignificant to document. If canvassers later 
discover an issue with the returns, and the Incident Log is blank, they will have 
no information to understand what occurred.
The Commission maintains a template of the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104), as 
well as a version that can be used by polling places with reporting units such 
as polling places with both optical scan tabulators and direct record electronic 
(DRE) equipment (EL-104mu).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 226
The Chief Inspector, or another poll worker designated by the chief, completes the 
first part of the Inspectors’ Statement, and verifies that all information is correct.
At least three poll workers certify the correctness of the tally sheets and 
Inspectors’ Statement and sign their names. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(4)(a).
Best Practice: At least three are required, but all poll workers who 
worked at the polling place that day should sign their names. If the 
standard Inspectors’ Statement does not have sufficient space for 
the number of poll workers working at the polling place, the poll 
workers may sign on an additional page that is then attached to the 
Inspectors’ Statement as an addendum.
Additional Documentation Accompanying the Inspectors’ Statement
Poll workers should also complete the following documentation that completes 
the Inspectors’ Statement. All these documents should be stapled together 
when complete.
Absentee Inspectors’ Statement.
Some municipal clerks prepare separate forms for issues relating to absentee 
ballots, including a separate Incident Log or Diary of events.
Observer Log (EL-109)
This should have been completed throughout the day as observers checked in. 
Poll workers should review it for completeness at the end of the night.
Orders to Leave (EL-110)
If poll workers ordered anyone to leave, ensure this documentation is complete.
Challenge Documentation (EL-104C)
If any voters are challenged, ensure this documentation is complete.
The Tally Sheet (EL-105)
Once the total number of voters and total number of ballots have been 
reviewed and compared, and any inconsistencies have been fully documented, 
poll workers complete the Tally Sheet (EL-105). Wis. Stat. § 7.51(4)(a).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 227
For offices and candidates that were counted using electronic tabulators, 
poll workers can paperclip the results tape to the Tally Sheet (EL-105) which 
completes the requirement.
However, the paper Tally Sheet will always be used to count qualified write-in votes.
If poll workers aren’t sure how to complete the Tally Sheet, they should confer 
with the Chief Inspector or municipal clerk.
Any poll worker who assisted with the tally must sign the Tally Sheet. Wis. Stat. 
§ 7.51(4)(a). At least three poll workers, including the Chief Inspector, must sign 
the tally sheet to complete it. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(4)(a). If partisan appointed poll 
workers are used, at least one of these poll workers representing each party 
must be a partisan appointee. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(4)(a).
What to Look for to Check Completeness:
 Ɋ Election Information: Correct ward(s), municipality, date, type of election
 Ɋ Tally Marks (if any): Are there 5 marks in each filled-in box? Is the written 
total the same as the tally?
 Ɋ Compare the totals
 Ɋ Review the Certification: Is the information correct? Did everyone who helped 
count the ballots sign the certification? Were all questions answered?
Important: Publicly Announce the Returns
Once the Tally Sheet is completed, poll workers shall publicly announce 
the returns in the polling place (even if no members of the public or 
candidates are present). Wis. Stat. § 7.51(4)(a).
The Ballot Container Certificate (EL-101)
The Ballot Container Certificate (EL-101) is completed and affixed to the bag, box, 
or container into which all marked ballots are secured for transport. The bag, 
box, or container will be sealed with a tamper-evident seal so that no ballot can 
be inserted or removed without breaking the seal. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(3)(a). Record 
the security seal number on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104). Only ballots and 
no other documents are sealed in this container.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 228
Sealed in container:
 Ɋ Damaged Ballots
 Ɋ Rejected Ballots
 Ɋ Voted Ballots
Not sealed in container:
 Ɋ EL-106
 Ɋ Provisional Ballots
 Ɋ Used Certificate Envelopes
Note: If the poll workers working at the polling place are nominees 
from political parties, at least one poll worker from each party must 
participate in the securing of the marked ballots. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(3)(a).
The EL-101 should be signed by the Chief Inspector and at least one other poll 
worker. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(3)(a). If poll workers are party affiliated, the EL-101 should 
contain one signature from the poll worker of each party, up to three total 
signatures (Democratic, Republican, Unaffiliated).
“Original Ballots” Envelope.
Poll workers should separately bundle all ballots that have been identified as 
damaged, defective, overvoted, objected to or set aside, and place them in this 
envelope. This envelope of ballots goes inside the Ballot Container along with 
the other voted ballots.
Remaining Voted Ballots.
All other voted ballots are also secured in the Ballot Container.
Inspectors’ Certificate of Provisional Ballots (EL-108) 
& Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123R)
Provisional ballots (sometimes known as sec. 6.97 ballots) are cast most often 
by voters who cannot show acceptable ID at the polling place. See Chapter 
14.13: “Provisional Voting” on page 95.
If any provisional ballots were cast, each ballot will be sealed within a 
Provisional Ballot Certificate Envelope (EL-123). Those certificate envelopes 
should be bundled inside a carrier envelope with the Inspectors’ Certificate of 
Provisional Ballots (EL-108) affixed. Wis. Stat. § 7.51(3)(a).
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 229
Provisional ballots are not secured inside the ballot container. Id. They should 
instead be placed in a separate bag or ballot container, which gets its own 
tamper-evident seal and EL-101. The bag or container containing provisional 
ballots should be labeled “Provisional Ballots.”
Best Practice: Poll workers should inform the municipal clerk if there 
are any provisional ballots.
The Inspectors’ Certificate of Provisional Ballots (EL-108) is signed by the Chief 
Inspector and two other poll workers. Id.
Poll workers must also complete the Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123R). This 
form lists the voters who cast provisional ballots, as well as important information 
about how and why they qualified to cast a provisional ballot. It should not be 
sealed inside the ballot container. The municipal clerk uses this form to send to all 
boards of canvass who will certify the election results. Wis. Stat. § 6.97(3)(b).
Certificate of Rejected Absentee Ballots (EL-102)
If any absentee ballots were rejected, poll workers must complete the Certificate 
of Rejected Absentee Ballots (EL-102). Each absentee ballot that is rejected must 
contain documented reasons for doing so on the Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104).
See Sec. 14.15.14: “Rejecting Absentee Ballots” on page 146.
Poll workers complete the endorsement section of the Certificate of Rejected 
Absentee Ballots (EL-102) brown carrier envelope. The EL-102 must be signed by 
the Chief Inspector and one of the poll workers representing each of the two 
major political parties. Wis. Stat. § 6.88(3)(b).
Best Practice: If there are not poll workers from each of the major 
parties, the EL-102 should be completed by at least two unaffiliated 
poll workers.
The EL-102 is then returned to the municipal clerk in the same manner as the 
official ballots voted at the election. Id. Certificate of Rejected Absentee Ballots 
(EL-102) brown carrier envelope goes inside the Ballot Container along with the 
other voted ballots.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 230
Used Certificate Envelopes of Absentee Voters (EL-103)
Absentee certificate envelopes are not destroyed or thrown away after the 
ballots are removed and counted. They are bundled and secured inside 
a carrier envelope, which is affixed with the Used Certificate Envelopes of 
Absentee Voters (EL-103). Wis. Stat. § 7.51(3)(d). The certificate is signed by 
the Chief Inspector and two other poll workers, one from each party (unless 
appointed without regard to party affiliation). Id.
Sec. 14.24.11: Combining the LBOC & 
Municipal Board of Canvassers (MBOC)
If a municipality has one polling place, no provisional ballots, and does not 
use “central count” for absentee ballot canvassing, the LBOC can be combined 
with the municipal board of canvassers (MBOC). Wis. Stat. § 7.53(1)(a). When the 
canvasses are combined, the members of the LBOC also serve as the members 
of the MBOC. Id.
If the above conditions are met, the LBOC can complete the municipal canvass on 
Election Night. Wis. Stat. § 7.53(2)(d). They complete the canvass statement, certify the 
municipal election results, and officially determine the winners. Wis. Stat. § 7.53(2).
In municipalities with more than one polling place, the MBOC meets after 
the election to certify the results of the municipal elections and make 
determinations of the winners. Id.
See Chapter 14.27: “Municipal Boards of Canvassers” on page 242 for 
detailed procedures on completing the MBOC on election night.
Board of Canvassers Statement (EL-106)
If the MBOC is conducted on Election Night, poll workers will need to complete 
the Board of Canvassers Statement (EL-106) at the polling place.
For detailed instructions on how to complete the EL-106, see the information 
provided with that form. Attach one original Tally Sheet (EL-105) to the EL-106. 
Each poll worker signs the certification on the canvass form.
Do not put the completed EL-106 in the ballot container — it instead gets routed 
to the municipal clerk.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 231
Sec. 14.24.12: Routing of Materials
Following the completion of all required end-of-night documentation, 
everything needs to be packaged and routed to the appropriate election 
official. Election officials can greatly assist their municipal clerks by packaging 
materials efficiently, so the clerk does not have to sort through a jumble of 
documents after Election Day.
Note: Poll workers should supplement this section with specific 
instructions and guidance from their municipal clerk. Municipal clerks 
may have jurisdiction-specific resources for closing the polls and 
packaging materials and supplies. If the municipal clerk’s instructions 
differ from this section, poll workers should confer with the municipal 
clerk and defer to their preferences.
To the Municipal Clerk:
 Ɋ All bundles of voted ballots, secured inside the ballot container.
 Ɋ All voted provisional ballots, secured inside a separate ballot container.
 Ɋ Unused (blank) ballots
 Ɋ Used Absentee Certificate Envelopes, secured in their carrier envelope
 Ɋ Original Tally Sheets (EL-105).
 Ɋ Copy of the voting equipment results tape (if any).
 Ɋ Original Inspectors’ Statement (EL-104) and any attachments, including the 
Absentee Event Log (if any), challenge documentation, and observer log.
 Ɋ Statement of the Board of Canvassers (EL-106) (if required)
 Ɋ Both poll books with certifications completed, as well as the supplemental 
poll list.
 Ɋ Absentee Ballot Log (EL-124)
 Ɋ Provisional Ballot Reporting Form (EL-123R)
 Ɋ Election Day Registration Forms
 Ɋ Any supplies indicated by the municipal clerk
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 232
To the School District Clerk:
Poll workers should not route any materials to the school district clerk on Election 
Night unless they are directed to do so by their municipal clerk.
To the County Clerk:
Poll workers should not route any materials to the county clerk on Election Night 
unless they are directed to do so by their municipal clerk.
First Edition, June 2026 Election Day 14 – 233
